The map showing world's division over Infantino

BBC News Tue, 11 Aug 2026 11:02:42 GMT

Gianni Infantino's previously untouchable status at the top of world football is now under significant pressure

As recently as mid-July, Gianni Infantino was considered a shoo-in to be re-elected as Fifa president next March.

All but a handful of Fifa's 210 members had endorsed his bid for a fourth term in charge of world football, and he faced no meaningful challenge from any quarter.

But that picture looks very different now, after two weeks of relentless fallout to his now-abandoned plan to sell off stakes in the World Cup and other competitions to private investors.

The world is far from united over whether Infantino should stay or go.

To illustrate that division, BBC Sport has mapped the apparent view of all 210 member associations who are able to vote (Nepal, Fifa's 211th member, is currently suspended and has no voting rights).

In some cases, countries have publicly stated their overt support or opposition regarding Infantino.

Others have directly told BBC Sport where they stand when asked to do so, while some are assumed positions based on statements made by the continental confederation the country sits within.

Whereas once Infantino looked set to saunter to a fourth term unopposed, he now might need to have enough numbers on his side to stay in post.

If a unity candidate is found to oppose him, then the election would become a direct battle.

Fifa election votes between two candidates require a simple majority - each candidate would require a total of 106 votes to win.

If there are three or more candidates, a first-round win requires two thirds of the vote. If there is no winner, there is a second round where a simple majority is needed.

Europe has 55 votes, Africa 54, Asia 46, North America 35, Oceania 11 and South America 10.

But while the current tally looks like 73 in support of Infantino, 124 in opposition to him and 13 undecided, that picture is fluid and is likely to change in the months ahead.

Trump says it would be 'terrible mistake' to remove Infantino

Several European countries, including England and Wales, have formally withdrawn letters of support for Infantino that they had sent for next year's election.

On Monday, the backlash against Infantino intensified when European governing body Uefa, North America's Concacaf and the Asian Football Confederation (AFC) signed a joint letter accusing him and Fifa of breaking their trust "through deception".

It is a significant coalition, and those regional governing bodies represent 136 associations between them.

But there are already dissenting members within the confederations, like Bhutan, the Philippines, and the UAE within Asia, who have all backed Infantino.

Indeed, Asia is considered a potential swing region for Infantino's future, with many associations not yet making their position known.

Mexico has broken ranks within Concacaf by coming out in support of Infantino, too. The USA and Canada have stated support for Concacaf's latest statement, but have not outright declared their opposition to Infantino's rule.

And the Oceania Football Confederation (OFC) said it had launched a consultation process among its members on 30 July, but hasn't yet taken a position regarding FFE. This is expected on Thursday after the conclusion of its executive committee meeting in Fiji.

Infantino still has staunch allies too, particularly within Africa (Caf) and South America (Conmebol), who have benefited from unprecedented investment since he took over a decade ago.

That investment could help him gain critical votes from nations within AFC, Concacaf and OFC - who are lower down the football food chain and have benefited significantly from the Fifa Forward programme, which has made billions of dollars available for investment.

And Saudi Arabia - which has yet to take a public position but was awarded hosting rights for the 2034 World Cup on Infantino's watch - will have its own football leadership elections in August. The results could dictate the kingdom's position, and influence the view of others in the region.

It cannot be guaranteed that every nation will toe the line of their confederation.

While Caf president Patrice Motsepe (right) remains a key ally of Infantino, Concacaf leader Victor Montagliani (left) could emerge as his most serious challenger

Infantino has shown no desire to step down, but there is a chance that his time in charge could be over by the autumn.

That could come to pass if Fifa Council members try to force his resignation at an emergency meeting.

Such a meeting can be called, and must take place within a fortnight, if 19 of the 37 members call for it.

The council is made up of nine members from Uefa, seven from AFC, six from Caf, five from Concacaf, five from Conmebol, and three from Oceania. Also on the panel are Infantino himself and Fifa secretary general Mattias Grafstrom.

Given its makeup, then, this approach is probably unlikely.

Which leaves the possibility of a candidate standing against Infantino next March. Nobody has put their name forward yet, but if the leaders of Uefa, Concacaf, and AFC are serious about toppling Infantino, uniting behind a single candidate is likely their best option.

The deadline for a candidate to announce their intention to stand is November.

Historically, other confederations have been angered by European dominance of football, its competitions and its wealth.

That is perhaps why Concacaf president Victor Montagliani has been consistently mooted as the most likely challenger to Infantino in recent weeks.

The map of votes could change significantly based on who stands.

If AFC president Sheikh Salman were to step forward, for example, there is a chance that could sway some of the associations in Asia that are currently supportive of Infantino towards him.

For now, the global picture remains unclear because of emerging splits within confederations, the potential for key players to declare one way or another in the coming months and the hitherto lack of a clear candidate to challenge Infantino.

What is clear is that Infantino is no longer coasting towards a fourth term, if he clings on that long.

England set to be one of the first countries to eliminate hepatitis C

BBC News Tue, 11 Aug 2026 08:54:57 GMT

England is on track to become one of the first countries in the world to eliminate hepatitis C, a dangerous virus that attacks the liver, figures show.

The target of treating 80% of all known cases has already been met, and deaths from the virus have fallen by 36% in the last decade, just short of what is needed by 2030.

Taking antiviral tablets for 8 to 12 weeks can cure more than 95% of cases.

Initiatives including A&E blood tests, GP registration testing and free at-home tests have helped to find people who were previously undiagnosed, says NHS England.

Hepatitis C is spread through contact with blood infected with the virus, such as by sharing needles with someone who has it.

Donor blood is already screened for it.

It is a silent disease, meaning people often have no symptoms until much later.

Untreated, it can cause serious and potentially life-threatening liver damage.

NHS England says that since 2015, more than 100,000 people have been diagnosed and treated for hepatitis C, meaning the country is already meeting that target.

Another goal - a 65% reduction in hepatitis C-related mortality compared with 2015 levels - has yet to be met, but might be before the 2030 target date.

Around 50,200 adults are living with hepatitis C, figures for England in 2024 suggest.

Estimates indicate 84.6% of those living with hepatitis C have been diagnosed - just short of the 90% target.

The Hepatitis C Trust says England is "on the cusp" of one of the most significant public health achievements in our country's history.

Prof Frankie Swords, NHS national medical director, added: "England is now leading the world in the mission to eliminate this disease and on course to beat the WHO's 2030 target, but we are determined to keep up the momentum and finish the job.

"We are committed to finding and treating everyone who needs support and would urge those at greater risk to come forward by ordering a free and confidential home-testing kit online."

Adults born in Ukraine, Romania, Estonia, Latvia, Poland, Albania, Lithuania, Bulgaria, Czechia or Slovakia are particularly urged to test, as some may have been infected through medical or dental procedures before 1991.

People can order a free, confidential NHS home self-testing kit without needing to speak to a GP.

Paul Eatwell, 65, was diagnosed after a routine blood test.

The grandfather from Blackburn, Lancashire, said: "My first reaction was disbelief. I remember saying: 'Are you sure? Surely there's been some mistake.'

"I didn't feel ill. I kept wondering how I could possibly have caught it."

He says the medical support he received made a huge difference.

While it has not been established how Eatwell caught the virus, it has been suggested that surgery in South Africa decades ago may have been the cause.

From 1970 to 1991, more than 30,000 people in the UK were infected with HIV and hepatitis C from contaminated blood products and fusions.

The infected blood scandal is known as the biggest treatment disaster in the NHS.

About 3,000 have since died and more deaths will follow.

A public inquiry found authorities covered up the scandal and exposed victims to unacceptable risks.

Swimming lessons 'vital' as schools gap revealed

BBC News Tue, 11 Aug 2026 05:32:33 GMT

Children at English primary schools with more pupils on free school meals are less likely to leave able to swim 25m, BBC research has found.

While 72% of Year 6 children on average left school able to swim 25m in 2024-25, this drops to just 55% for schools with a higher proportion of disadvantaged children.

Ashley Jones, head of water safety and education at the Royal Life Saving Society (RLSS), said it was "absolutely vital" children were equipped with basic swimming and self-rescue skills.

A Department for Education (DfE) spokesperson said it was aware of the gap and it "needs to change".

The national curriculum in England states when they leave Year 6, all pupils should be able to:

A BBC Freedom of Information request to the DfE has revealed the proportion of Year 6 pupils at individual state schools, excluding special schools, who are able to meet the targets.

While 70% of 2024-25 Year 6 leavers nationally could on average perform a safe self-rescue, only half of children from schools with the highest level of free meal eligibility could do so.

A safe self-rescue includes being able to tread water for 30 seconds, children being able to float on their back for 60 seconds and swim 15m to exit the water unaided.

Most school groups only get six weeks of 30-minute sessions to learn basic swimming skills – three hours in total.

The findings come after several water-related deaths this summer, including an 18-year-old man who died after getting into difficulty at sea at the weekend in East Sussex.

School performance ranged from no pupils leaving Year 6 able to swim 25m to others meeting the target at 100%.

Only 38 schools in the highest category for disadvantaged pupils hit the swimming target. Meanwhile, it was met by nearly 400 schools with the lowest proportion of children on free school meals.

For parents, there can be several obstacles when it comes to helping their children learn to swim and become water competent.

In 2021, Swim England published its Decade of Decline report warning almost 2,000 swimming pools could be lost forever by 2030.

Accessibility is one of the factors Ashley Jones from the RLSS says limits children's swimming education.

"We've got some incredible public leisure facilities, just not enough of them," he said.

"That means people have got to travel further.

"Then you are dependent on public transport, which can be costly, and access to a swimming pool in terms of cost and time is still really challenging."

Some schools are tackling transport costs in innovative ways, such as in Derby where a pop-up swimming pool was set up on school grounds.

The RLSS works to help people stay safe in, on, and around water. A key element of its work is using education to help people become water competent.

Jones explained: "You can be a really competent pool swimmer, but we wouldn't dare put you in 6ft of surf or throw you in a raging river.

"Building competence, you've got to have knowledge, you've got to have skills, you've got to have experience, crucially, and you've got to have training."

In order to help children build water competency, Jones believes swimming lessons need to be universal, engaging and accessible.

The RLSS would like to see "a fully funded, fully supported, high-level learn to swim programme" that is supported by water safety education and then applied in an outdoor environment.

Hampshire-based swimming teacher Bethany Harris said it was "really tough" to teach a life skill in that time to children who have not had much access to swimming.

She believes school swimming lessons should start earlier in life to ensure nobody is left behind.

"Ultimately, in three hours you can't take someone from being a nervous swimmer to confidently being able to swim 25m with a stroke that is calm and is going to keep them safe," she said.

"I don't think many of us would come out of that with something that was going to save our lives potentially.

"I think it needs to come in basically as we're teaching them to read and write in the infant school."

Harris lives "on the doorstep" of Hawley Lake, near Farnborough, where a 14-year-old died in May after going missing while swimming.

"Even being inland away from the coast you've got dangerous water all about," she said.

The bottom fifth of local authorities for the average proportion of children able to swim 25m were all urban areas.

Only four out of 31 of those council areas were coastal, with the remaining areas being landlocked.

Overall, fewer children in urban areas could swim the distance proficiently than in rural areas.

Harris would also like to see pools offer more initiatives to ensure they are accessible to all families.

She said: "Ten years ago, there used to be a summer scheme where you could swim for £1 all summer long through some leisure centres.

"I haven't heard of anything like that happening locally these days."

A spokesperson for the DfE said a new PE Partnership Network would help close the gap seen by disadvantaged children.

It will bring together schools, local clubs and national bodies like Swim England.

"This means targeted support for the areas and pupils who need it most, so that every child, wherever they live, gets the same chance to learn to swim," the spokesperson said.

Swim England is working closely with the government to support the delivery of school swimming lessons.

"Swimming is a life skill, not just a sport, and every child should have the opportunity to leave primary school able to swim and be safe in, on and around water," a spokesperson said.

"However, schools have faced significant pressures in recent years, particularly those serving disadvantaged communities, including competing curriculum demands, increasing transport costs and access to suitable swimming pools.

"These challenges are real, so it is more important than ever that there is a collective approach to helping children gain a skill that could one day save their life."

Additional reporting by Jonathan Fagg and Emily Unia

Perseid meteor shower reaches peak: When and where to catch a glimpse

BBC News Tue, 11 Aug 2026 01:59:24 GMT

The best annual meteor shower has once again returned to our skies, and the Perseids dazzling display is set to peak on Wednesday night.

But skywatchers will be treated to not one, but two, impressive astronomical events within just a few hours of each other.

A deep partial solar eclipse will be also visible in the UK in the early evening before the Sun sets and the stars emerge. While the two events are unrelated, their timing makes for an exceptional night of stargazing.

Astronomer Dr Becky Smethurst said it was "the perfect cosmic coincidence of events".

Here is when to look up and where you will have the best views of the meteor shower.

Meteor showers happen when the Earth passes through trails of debris from asteroids or comets - a snowball of frozen rock and gas orbiting the Sun.

As that debris enters our planet's atmosphere, it burns up and creates spectacular streaks of light - meteors.

Royal Astronomical Society Research Fellow Dr Becky Smethurst described the Perseids as "one of the best nights for astronomy all year".

The Perseids, pronounced 'Per-see-ids', are caused by debris left behind specifically by the Swift-Tuttle comet.

This comet is considered large with its solid core measuring 16 miles (26km) across.

As it takes 133 years to complete a single orbit around the Sun - much longer than our own - some of the pieces of rock we see burning up could have been left behind more than a century ago.

"It's sort of like the Universe's own cosmic clock in a way," Smethurst said.

These meteors are visible all around the world and named because they appear to originate from the constellation Perseus.

The Perseids is active from mid-July to 23 Aug this year, but on most nights the shower will only display a few meteors each hour.

But the best time to watch is on the peak nights, which this year is in the early morning hours between midnight and dawn on 12 and 13 August.

At this time there will be many, many more meteors. If you are really lucky, you could see more than 100 'shooting stars' per hour.

If possible, choose somewhere away from cities and artificial light. Light pollution will hide the fainter meteors, leaving only the brightest ones visible.

This year could offer especially good viewing conditions because there is a new moon, meaning there will be no bright moonlight.

Give your eyes around 20 to 30 minutes to adjust to the darkness and look towards the widest unobstructed area of sky possible.

For most of England and Wales, stargazers will have largely clear skies throughout the night with just a small chance of one or two mist patches.

If you plan to stay up late in the very far north of England, Northern Ireland or much of Scotland you may need to employ a little more patience.

While some cloud breaks are expected, there will be large areas of cloud at times and even the risk of one or two showers.

For those in north-west Scotland, an early night might be a better option.

The forecast may yet change so keep checking with BBC Weather for the latest details.

During an eclipse, the sky darkens offering a short window to also see the Perseids at the same time.

But this will only be in the areas witnessing a total eclipse - like Spain - and not in the UK where only a partial eclipse will be witnessed.

But even there, the chances are extremely slim, "you would have to get very, very lucky to see a shooting star during the total eclipse," Dr Smethurst said.

Remember for a solar eclipse you must use certified solar glasses - looking directly at the sun without proper eye protection can cause permanent eye damage.

How to get a good night's sleep during hot weather

BBC News Thu, 25 Jun 2026 14:48:15 GMT

A period of extreme heat across the UK this week has broken records for the hottest day and night temperatures ever recorded in June.

It can be hard to sleep during periods of hot weather, but these simple tips could help.

Hot weather can make you feel tired and lethargic during the day.

That is because you are using more energy to regulate your internal temperature.

But if your sleep is disturbed at night, try to avoid napping during the day.

When it is hot, feeling sleepy can be precious - save it for bedtime.

Hot weather can encourage you to change your habits. Don't. That can disrupt your sleep patterns.

Try to keep to your usual bedtime routine, and do the things you normally do before bed.

Take steps to make sure your bedroom is as cool as it can be at night.

During the day, close the curtains or blinds to keep out the sun. Make sure you shut the windows on the sunny side of your home, to keep out hot air.

Open all the windows before you go to bed, to get a through breeze.

Reduce your bedding but keep covers handy. Thin cotton sheets will absorb sweat.

Wearing loose-fitting clothes made of natural fibres like cotton or linen can keep you cooler than sleeping naked.

However hot it is in your bedroom, your body temperature will fall during the night.

That is why you sometimes wake up feeling cold even though the temperature is higher than usual.

Using even a small fan can be sensible in hot weather, especially when it is humid.

It encourages the evaporation of sweat and makes it easier for your body to regulate your internal temperature.

If you don't have a fan, try filling a hot water bottle with ice cold liquid instead.

Alternatively, cool socks in the fridge and put those on. Cooling your feet lowers the overall temperature of your skin and body.

You should make sure you drink enough water throughout the day but should avoid drinking very large amounts before bed.

It's about striking the right balance.

You don't want to wake up thirsty - but you don't want to have to take an additional trip to the bathroom in the early hours either.

Be careful about soft drinks. Many contain large amounts of caffeine, which stimulates the central nervous system and makes you feel more awake.

Avoid drinking too much alcohol as well. Many people drink more when the weather is hot.

Alcohol might help you fall asleep but it also promotes early morning waking and a poorer quality of sleep overall.

If you are struggling to sleep, get up and do something calming. Try reading, writing, or even folding your washing.

But avoid using your phone. Mindless scrolling might seem appealing, but the blue light from the screen can stimulate your brain and leave you feeling more awake.

Return to bed when you feel sleepy.

Children are often quite robust sleepers - but they can be very sensitive to changes in family "mood" and routine.

Make sure usual bedtimes and bath times do not go out of the window just because it is warm.

As part of the bedtime routine, the NHS UK website recommends lukewarm baths. Make sure they are not too cold, as that will boost your child's circulation which could increase their temperature.

A baby cannot let you know if they are too hot or too cold, so it is important to monitor their temperature.

They generally sleep best when their bedroom temperature is between 16C and 20C - use a room thermometer to check.

Most of us need about seven to eight hours of good-quality sleep each night to function properly.

Teenagers, children and babies typically need more sleep because they are still growing.

But remember that the majority of people can still function well after a night or two of disturbed sleep.

Although you might yawn a little more frequently than usual, you will probably be fine.

These tips were based on suggestions by Prof Kevin Morgan, former director of the Clinical Sleep Research Unit at Loughborough University, and Lisa Artis, of the Sleep Council. This article was first published in July 2019.

What it's like to chase solar eclipses around the world

BBC News Sun, 09 Aug 2026 01:31:41 GMT

On Wednesday, parts of Europe will be plunged into daytime darkness as the moon passes in front of the sun, temporarily blocking its light and casting a shadow across the continent.

For many of us, it will be a once-in-a-lifetime experience. But for some, following eclipses has become something of an addiction.

Armed with two high-spec cameras, a specialist telescope and a drone, Mark Griffiths has spent two days driving from Cheshire to Galicia - one of the best places to view the eclipse - to record the event.

It's a hobby that started six years ago, when he went to Shetland to see the northern lights - though the weather was so dreary he "didn't see anything".

But it didn't put him off and he soon found he couldn't stop. He loved the thrill of witnessing and capturing the sky's most spectacular events.

He's since filmed the northern lights numerous times - he goes to Iceland once a month and leads tour groups there - and has photographed various auroras, eclipses and bioluminescence.

But this is his first total solar eclipse.

"I kept wanting more," he says, and mainland Europe's first total solar eclipse for more than 25 years is "like what the World Cup is to football" for enthusiasts like him.

"I'll be honest, it's like an addiction now," he tells me.

I'm speaking to him the day after he's finished his marathon drive from England, across France and into Spain. He only paused to take photos of the night sky and rest for a few hours in Bordeaux. "I'm quite sleep deprived."

The path of total eclipse begins at the tip of Siberia before curving round the Arctic Ocean and crossing parts of Greenland and Iceland.

It then proceeds across a stretch of the northern Atlantic Ocean before reaching northern Spain and finishing in the Mediterranean. Other parts of Europe will see a partial eclipse.

The total eclipse is only expected to last a couple of minutes for people who watch it, but that doesn't deter eclipse hunters from crossing continents to witness it.

Samantha Phillips-Brown and her husband Gregory have flown to Iceland and hired a camper van so they can drive to a remote area to see it.

This will be the second solar eclipse they've witnessed.

It was the awe-inspiring power of the first one they saw, near their home in upstate New York in 2023, that convinced them to travel for this one. They booked their flights and van more than a year ago.

"It was such a phenomenal experience to see the Sun start going dark and hear, suddenly, the birds stop chirping," recalls Samantha. "It's just a very strange experience."

Gregory thinks of how Iceland's first settlers must have felt witnessing such a phenomenon more than a millennium ago, a time of mythological rather than scientific explanation.

"They really didn't know what was happening, didn't know how long it was going to last... it must have been quite terrifying for them, in a way."

They are spending the days before the eclipse driving round eastern parts of Iceland to go hiking and camping.

The path of totality crosses the western part of Iceland and they hope to find a spot in the Westfjords peninsula to witness it.

Although they've heard rumours of roads being closed to stop too many people overwhelming the area, so are keen to get there a couple of days before the spectacle.

Samantha, who her husband describes as an "obsessive travel planner", is all over the Facebook groups and social media pages being used by eclipse hunters to share information and advice.

She's expecting a good turnout given the number of eclipse glasses she's seen people with in Iceland. "They're really trying to prepare everybody," she says.

"What I expect, as we start inching westward, is we're going to see more and more of them."

Andrew Wills is also going to an island to see the eclipse, albeit a much warmer one: Majorca.

He's travelling with his wife and his dad, who is 90. Father and son witnessed mainland Europe's last total solar eclipse together in 1999 while stood atop St Agnes Beacon in Cornwall.

The landmark, believed by some to be a Bronze Age bowl barrow, offers panoramic views across the Cornish countryside and out to the Atlantic. It was a cloudy day, remembers Andrew.

"We didn't see the shadow rushing across the sea towards us, though we did see the dawn appearing and it getting closer very quickly," he says. "It was awe-inspiring."

He has high hopes for a clearer day in Majorca where he has booked a villa near the town of Andraitx, located in the west of the island.

Andrew has earmarked a spot nearby he hopes will give a good view across the sea. Their location in the path of totality means the eclipse will likely happen low on the horizon.

"We're going to take a couple of camping chairs with us, so Dad can sit if we can get there an hour or so before."

The islands of Majorca and Menorca will be the last places on land to view the eclipse before the Sun sets and the shadow disappears.

Eclipses have fascinated Andrew ever since he saw his first in St Agnes all those years ago.

"We live on a planet where we have this amazing coincidence. Where the Sun is 400 times bigger than the moon but the moon is 400 times closer.

"And so when the moon passes in front of the Sun and between Earth, it casts this perfect shadow."

It made him appreciate the delicacy of life on our planet in the way astronomer Carl Sagan once did when speaking of the Pale Blue Dot photograph.

"We are tiny little specks in a huge vast area of nothingness," says Andrew. "We're amazingly lucky to be alive."

Trump’s Air Force One deception faces scrutiny over ‘decoy’ passengers

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 21:46:34 GMT

Journalists and others were allowed to fly on presidential jet despite security threat while Trump was spirited away

Sign up for the Breaking News US newsletter email

Democrats called on Tuesday for a congressional briefing over a stunning charade in which Donald Trump was secretly spirited to different plane than the one the public and press believed he was travelling on, an episode first reported by the Washington Post and also by the New York Times. Critics now question why reporters and the public had been deliberately misled.

When US intelligence allegedly found a credible threat that Iran plotted to kill him last month, Trump left Air Force One in a catering truck. While the president was reportedly snuck out the side door of the plane, more than a hundred aides, officials and reporters, were kept onboard to fly towards Britain, without being told there was a threat to their lives.

“It appears that the idea was we can reduce the danger to President Trump by offering up 100 or so people to be killed by the Iranians in his place,” said Steven Cash, executive director of the Steady State and a former CIA intelligence officer and senior adviser to the Department of Homeland Security’s intelligence branch.

Richard Blumenthal, a Democratic senator for Connecticut, called the operation “unprecedented and surreal” and “downright scary” on CNN. “There has to be a congressional briefing because it involves not just the safety of the president.

“I want to know what precautions were taken to make sure that all of the passengers on that Air Force One, which was judged too dangerous for the president to fly on, what precautions were taken to keep the staff and reporters on that plane safe?”

The White House had publicly said the president would fly to Britain onboard Air Force One – albeit an older version instead of the new Qatari-gifted aircraft. In reality, Trump boarded the presidential jet in full view of television cameras before being spirited away minutes later and transferred to an air force C-32A.

The deception was sufficiently elaborate that journalists travelling with the president – and some White House staff – believed Trump remained onboard Air Force One. Reporters were instructed to keep the shades over their windows closed during the flight.

The C-32A carrying Trump flew to RAF Mildenhall in Britain, arriving at about 10.20pm. The older Air Force One and the travelling press pool arrived minutes later. Photographers subsequently captured Trump descending from the presidential aircraft, apparently after he had been secretly reunited with it.

Trump then greeted troops at the base before boarding the newly refurbished Boeing 747 donated by Qatar and flying on to Washington.

Ned Price, a former deputy to the US ambassador to the UN, said he did not begrudge any White House taking extraordinary measures to protect the president but the current administration had perpetuated an “outright lie” for weeks and turned reporters into “unwitting conspirators”.

Price, an ex-special assistant to President Barack Obama on the national security council, added: “There was a way to do this where they didn’t need to lie. They could have enlisted the travelling press pool, who would have very willingly, I am certain, not reported on certain security arrangements.

“Once all was said and done and the president was safely in the UK or back at Andrews air force base, they could have said, this is what we did and this is why we did it,” Price added. “But they feel no compunction to the truth. They feel no loyalty to the truth. Instead it’s just an effort over the past month to lie and to mislead.”

Journalists also raised concerns. Jake Tapper, an anchor on CNN, tweeted: “Obviously the life of the president is paramount, and previous White Houses have used deception to protect the president’s life. But no officials I’ve spoken to have ever before heard of using an AF1 full of WH staff and journalists as a decoy during an imminent threat.”

The White House has not directly confirmed the details of the operation. Its communications director, Steven Cheung, instead told the Post the president faced numerous threats and that officials used “every tool at our disposal” to protect him.

The revelation sheds new light on an abrupt decision that had already raised questions about the security of the Qatari aircraft. Trump had flown the newly refurbished jet to the Nato summit – its first international journey – but unexpectedly announced that he would instead use the older Air Force One on the first leg of his return journey.

At the time, Trump said the new aircraft was being flown to Mildenhall so US troops stationed there could inspect it. The New York Times and CBS News subsequently reported that intelligence officials had raised concerns about a possible attack on Trump or his aircraft amid the escalating conflict with Iran.

During the summit, the US military was conducting major strikes against Iran, adding to fears of Iranian retaliation. The new account suggests that the threat was taken more seriously than previously understood.

Not everyone is convinced the threat justified what happened next. “From the Iranian perspective, pulling off an attempted targeting of presidential aircraft at this depth would be extremely implausible; it would require close-in clandestine operators with man-portable anti-air systems,” said Ankit Panda, the Stanton senior fellow in the nuclear policy program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace. Panda said it was not clear there had been a specific, credible threat within Turkey itself, though presidential security routinely plans around “known unknowns”.

“Iran doesn’t have surface-to-air missiles that could target an aircraft taking off from Ankara,” said Dr Jeffrey Lewis, a distinguished fellow at the Foreign Policy Research Institute. Iran could in theory strike a plane on the runway with a ballistic missile, he said, “but that seems very unlikely”. His guess: US officials were worried about “a terrorist-like attack using a man-portable air defense system,” such as a Chinese-made QW-12 or FN-16, or a North Korean version of the Russian Igla.

James Jeffrey, a former US ambassador to Turkey and deputy national security adviser under George W Bush who is now a distinguished fellow at the pro-Israel Washington Institute, said the secrecy around moving Trump to another aircraft pointed to serious intelligence behind the threat. “This is very unusual, and indicates that they had very good intelligence of a threat, and that they did not trust the aircraft that he was in,” said Jeffrey, who has extensive experience traveling onboard Air Force One. Jeffrey said he believed the intelligence may have come from Israel, which has extensive intelligence capabilities focused on Iran. While Israeli officials sometimes seek to “not only inform but influence” US policy, he said they would not risk fabricating a threat involving the president’s safety. “They wouldn’t play with something as serious as the president’s safety,” Jeffrey said, adding that any such warning would prompt the US government to verify the intelligence, gather more information about the threat and follow up on it.”

Trump himself appeared to hint at the security concerns when reporters questioned him about why they had been ordered to keep their window shades closed. “You’re probably on a dangerous flight,” he said. “But if I go, you go. Right?”

Asked later whether there had been a credible Iranian threat against Air Force One, Trump said: “Well, I have a threat all the time. I’m number one on their list before you.”

The operation has nevertheless prompted questions about the decision to deceive the press corps and the public, particularly after the White House continued to maintain the cover story.

“The way to mitigate the threat included offering up the remaining people on Air Force One as an unwitting decoy,” Cash said. What was unclear, he added, was the order of operations – whether Trump’s team waited until he was safely away before letting the rest of the plane take off, or sent it up not knowing. If officials had simply announced Trump wasn’t onboard once he had landed safely, he said, they could have accomplished most of what they wanted without anyone remaining in the dark. “It really does sound like they were intended to serve as a decoy.”

Cash pointed to Intelligence Community Directive 191, the government’s “duty to warn” policy, which requires intelligence agencies to alert people who are the specific, credible target of a threat of killing, serious injury or kidnapping – including, he said, journalists and civilians, not just officials.

“If there’s intelligence collected that shows that a human being or a group of human beings are in danger, they need to be warned,” Cash said. “The question is: were the people who remained on the plane warned? If not, it violated ICD 191.” The directive, adopted by the director of national intelligence in 2015, does allow waivers where a warning would compromise sources, methods or operations – but Cash said he knew of no exception written for using people as decoys. “Probably because nobody ever thought that anybody would come up with the idea.”

Others defended the operation. Ari Fleischer, a former White House press secretary under President George W Bush, said that if the report was accurate, the Secret Service, air force and White House deserved credit for protecting the president. “If there’s a credible threat against any POTUS, their job is to protect him,” he wrote on X.

The extraordinary journey echoes an operation carried out by Bill Clinton in 2000, when the former president secretly flew into Pakistan onboard an unmarked executive jet while Air Force One was used as a decoy.

But Matthew Hoh, a senior fellow at the Eisenhower Media Network and Iraq war combat veteran, described the Trump incident as unique. “If it was a credible threat and they allowed Air Force One to take off thinking it was going to be shot down or whatever was going happen, they saw the pilots, the air crew, the White House staff, the journalists as all being expendable, dispensable, meaningless,” he said.

“There’s a lot of implications here with this story that help us understand this administration better or maybe reinforce the understanding we already have of this venal and vainglorious White House.”

“I’m proud of my faith, I’m proud of my standards, I’m a proud conservative woman, but Christian woman, a God-fearing woman,” she said.

“Every teenager has made a mistake; every person has made a mistake,” she added. “I’m here to talk about the woman I am today, things may have happened a decade ago, but I am the hardworking woman who stood up there and won Miss North Carolina USA fair and square.”

Boltinhouse claimed pageant organizers had not offered her a clear reason for being dethroned: “Honestly, I don’t know; I wasn’t really given anything.”

A Blaize Productions, license holder for Miss North Carolina USA, told The News & Observer that the reasons “were clearly shared and expressed” to Boltinhouse.

Mincey said Boltinhouse had “earned the respect and admiration of millions who support her. And one thing is for certain: Brittany Boltinhouse will not be canceled.”

Romell Glave soars to 100m title as Jeremiah Azu makes it British one-two

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 21:10:08 GMT

Olympic champion Marcell Jacobs pulls up injured

Italy’s Nadia Battocletti wins gold in women’s 5,000m

On Marcell Jacobs’ right arm there is a tattoo of the Roman colosseum and a gladiator: a reminder of his fighting spirit and what Italy’s capital means to him. But in the swirling bearpit of a European 100m final, his body dramatically broke down.

That was the first shock. The second was the sight of two Britons, Romell Glave and Jeremiah Azu, coming through to take gold and silver.

True, the finishing times were nothing special. But try telling that to the 16,000 crowd who cheered to the rafters as Glave took gold in a modest 10.09 sec. Azu took silver in 10.16 ahead of Germany’s Owen Ansah, who claimed bronze 10.19.

And Jacobs? Well, he looked a little saddened and weary as he hobbled over the line in last in 12.26.

“We’ve been working on my start to the first 30 metres, and I was able to execute that today and to be European champion,” said Glave, who was wearing sunglasses and certainly left his rivals in the shade. “It’s amazing feeling, and to do it here on home soil as well in Birmingham, to be able to bring a one-two for British athletics.”

This was the third time that Britain has won gold and silver in the men’s 100m at the European champions, following Darren Campbell beating Dwain Chambers in 1998 and Zharnel Hughes finishing ahead of Reece Prescod in 2018.

“It wasn’t really pressure mentally,” added Glave, who celebrated by jogging around the track with a gold crown around his head. “I was ready for this occasion. It’s just physical. I have to fill in that gap because last week, I’ve been a bit up and down with my body. I was able to come out here regardless of how I was feeling, and also show up because the objective was to win.”

Glave was unable to build on a European bronze medal in 2024 after what he says was a fracture in his back that became inflamed. But the 26-year-old has been working on his sprint mechanics and the mental side of his sport with his coach Michael Afilaka – and this was his reward.

Jacobs had been bidding for his third consecutive European 100m title, putting him in an exclusive club alongside the great Soviet sprinter Valeriy Borzov and Britain’s Linford Christie. And after imperiously winning his semi-final, he looked a near certainty for gold.

But that was before his injury-ridden body broke down before halfway as he chased Azu, who had made a lightning start. Still, Azu was happy enough with silver.

“Normally, I’m quite consistent,” he said. “This year, it’s been one race great, the next not up and down. But when it comes to championships, I know I find my best, and I show that today. We always say times are irrelevant at this point. You want to go out there and race for medals.”

There was an Italian success in the women’s 5,000m as Nadia Battocletti obliterated the field in the final 200m. “The race was super tactical, but then I played my winning card,” said Battocletti, who won a slowly run race in 15:37.84. Spain’s Marta García took silver, more than two seconds back, with another Italian Micol Majori winning bronze.

But there was disappointment for Britain’s Hannah Nuttall, who slipped to fifth after being in a prominent position at the bell.

In the women’s 100m hurdles final, Nadine Visser of the Netherlands took gold by one-thousandth of a second to defeat Poland’s Pia Skrzyszowska. Both women finished with a time of 12.67 but after a long time to assess the photo-finish, Visser was given the award. “I won the dip from Pia and that doesn’t happen very often,” she said

Stokes’ presence and group stage drama add to One-Day Cup’s quiet resurgence

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 19:35:51 GMT

Dawn of the Hundred may have ‘devalued’ the competition, but events at New Road show there is still life in county cricket

The Metro Bank One-Day Cup has been a bit of a sleeper hit this summer. Words such as “devalued” and “second tier” have followed the tournament since the dawn of the Hundred in 2021 but there has been a quiet resurgence of late.

The prolonged dry spell has provided certainty and pleasant conditions but the big one has been the Hundred’s tractor beam slightly easing. Essentially, the full availability of England players – plus one extra overseas spot per franchise – has lessened the strain on county 50-over squads and upped their quality a touch.

And though he is just one man, Ben Stokes’s presence in Durham’s ranks has also played a small part. Wherever Durham travel, supporters have been able to watch a bona fide England great in the flesh; close enough to read the tattoos or catch a whiff of expensive cologne as he prowls the boundary rope between spells.

This was not by design, of course, but rather an unexpected bonus after Stokes pulled the pin on his England career in June. Having not registered for the Hundred auction, the all‑rounder could not slip into the tournament as a late addition. Whether he would have, given his rekindled love for Durham, is uncertain.

Stokes was at New Road on Tuesday, where his team were looking to lock down their place in the knockout phase, and the defending champions Worcestershire needed a big win to boost their net run-rate and pip them to third in Group B. Jake Libby, the home captain, was also eyeing his fifth List A century to equal the world record.

Libby fell for 46 – the domestic record of four in a row will have to suffice – but his team still squeezed out the win. Problem was, it was not quite big enough, having posted 291 and then needed to keep the visitors under 205. Durham, 214 all out in 38.5 overs after stumbling to 199 for nine, progress to the quarter-finals.

Still, for all the local disappointment, or the relief in the away dressing room after Hampshire lost a nail-biter at Derbyshire, this was still a pretty life-affirming day all the same. A crowd of about 3,000 may not jump off the page but they were engaged, buzzed throughout, and stayed to applaud the players as they left the field.

New Road was pretty much bliss in the sunshine, even if at one stage, when the hosts slipped to 162 for six in the 32nd over, it looked like a damp squib might unfold. Step forward Tom Taylor, blazing a career-best 86 from No 7 – eight fours, three sixes – to give his fellow bowlers plenty more to work with in the defence.

Save for Ben McKinney, back from Southern Brave and cracking 82 from opener, Durham slightly froze in the chase. Stokes came and went for one, bowled off an inside edge by Taylor to induce his latest rueful look at the pitch. But in the end, as the game within a game tightened, Durham’s lower order just did enough.

Contrary to some perceptions about the county game and the Hundred’s supposedly more family‑friendly vibe, this was a mixed crowd of all ages. The hospitality tent was packed with a “Ladies Day” charity fundraiser, while gaggles of enthusiastic youngsters hunted autographs throughout.

after newsletter promotion

The most in-demand signature was that of Stokes, naturally, but pretty much anyone in full kit was being pestered for a scribble or a selfie. A portion of the membership would sooner the County Championship was being played in high summer but 50-over cricket does offer a full range for folks to enjoy.

And for any chuntering about the Hundred and its (undeniable) broader impact, the money from the sale last year has helped a county such as Worcestershire. While the problem of the annual floods at New Road needs a long-term solution, the club have managed to wipe out circa £4m in debt over the past year.

“It has given us a chance to breathe,” says Ashley Giles, the chief executive. “There are fewer sleepless nights than before. Although we still have to be smart and in some ways it ramps up the pressure on us a bit, as there are no more excuses.”

There were not that many previously, to be fair, with resourcefulness underpinning Worcestershire’s triumph in this competition last year. Going by the efforts to keep their title defence alive, thwarted though they were, it is still there in abundance.

‘I am quite optimistic’: Newcastle’s Jaissle on leading club after turbulent summer

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 19:15:09 GMT

The former Al-Ahli head coach, chosen by the Saudi owners to replace Eddie Howe, admits ‘vacuum’ after sale of three key players

Matthias Jaissle has been described as “fire” and a “rockstar”, but his formal “unveiling” as Newcastle’s manager revealed a rather more considered and conservative character than advertised.

At the precise moment the clock struck 3pm on Tuesday the 38‑year‑old German walked into a packed media suite at St James’ Park. Before taking his seat on the stage Jaissle, wearing a smartly understated open-necked suit complete with a silk pocket square, took a deep breath and jumped down into audience, making a point of shaking everyone’s hand. It was a courteous and classy gesture.

Once back on that podium, his evident excitement at exchanging Saudi Arabia’s Al-Ahli for the Premier League was tempered by the sort of realism inevitable at a club that, in Anthony Gordon, Sandro Tonali and Bruno Guimarães, has just sold its three best players.

“This is now a vacuum as you call it,” Eddie Howe’s successor said in his excellent English. “They were great characters, great players, ­experienced players. Obviously we are in that transition phase. My job is to make the best out of it all. I am quite optimistic. We are ambitious, that’s the most important thing and why I decided to come here. But it will not happen overnight. I’m pretty sure it will be a successful story. But we need time now.”

Regular chats with the England head coach, Thomas Tuchel, promise to help minimise any culture shock. “It’s been a long connection,” he said. “Thomas was my youth coach when I was a youth player at Stuttgart. He was amazing, I really admire him.

“He’s a really special coach and I’m looking forward to staying in touch with him, and getting insights on the league. That’s something I want to use for sure. But it’s also important to build your own opinions because every coach is their own human being and has their opinions and, ­sometimes players get stuck in a box. It’s ­important for me to be neutral about players.”

Allied to a willingness to chall­enge assumptions, such refusal to take labels at face value hinted at the qualities that prompted David ­Hopkinson, Newcastle’s Canadian chief executive, to dub Jaissle “fire” and “a rock star”.

If the one-time RB Salzburg manager’s brand of football – “front foot, intense, aggressive, vertical and, of course, also successful” – fits the latter tag, Newcastle fans have greeted him with an adaptation of a late 1970s Euro-disco hit.

Where the Spanish female pop duo Baccara soared to No 1 with Yes, Sir I Can Boogie, travelling supporters attending the friendly win in Valencia last Saturday chorused: “Oh Jaissle I can boogie/You try me once, you’ll beg for more.”

Jaissle said: “All my staff members already said they’re looking forward to sitting after a match in the bar and listening to the song. So let’s see about that! I heard it once and I was smiling. I’m really happy the fans already appreciate me a lot. The welcome from the fans was unbelievable when I was having a walk yesterday in the city. It was really giving me a nice feeling.”

There were more “big smiles” when he recalled a phone call last month asking him if he would be interested in replacing Howe but, little more than 48 hours after arriving in Newcastle for the first time following a training camp at La Manga in southern Spain, Jaissle is under no illusions as to the scale of the challenge ahead.

“When I received the call at the end of July and it was clearly most likely Eddie would quit there were no doubts or question marks. I had a big smile.”

Burnham’s prison plans could raise risk of mistaken releases, union warns

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 18:48:23 GMT

Concerns raised over proposed changes to early release scheme as probation staff vote to strike over workloads

Andy Burnham’s plan to stop the early release of PC Andrew Harper’s killers and other serious offenders could increase the risk of some more dangerous prisoners being released by mistake, unions have warned.

In another blow to the proposals announced by the prime minister, probation staff have voted overwhelmingly to go on strike, warning of already unrealistic workloads.

While the vote by members of Napo was not directly connected to the early release plans, the probation officers’ union has previously criticised the proposals as rushed. The union said the 91% vote in favour of a strike would come as a blow to ministers.

Burnham said earlier he recognised “the depth of public concern” about the proposed early release of Harper’s killers and announced a change to the early release scheme. The scheme is designed to free up urgently needed spaces in overcrowded prisons across England and Wales.

After an uproar about the proposed release of Harper’s killers Jessie Cole and Albert Bowers, Burnham said on Tuesday morning that he had instructed the justice secretary, Alex Norris, to find more prison spaces so the pair and other serious offenders would not be freed.

Writing on Facebook, Burnham said places could be provided by removing foreign offenders more quickly, finding a way to release lower-risk prisoners stuck in jail on indeterminate sentences, and “making greater use of the entire prison estate including using the female estate for male offenders”.

Downing Street had said on Monday that there was no feasible way of exempting just a handful of prisoners from the scheme to block the release of Cole and Bowers, who were sentenced to 13 years for Harper’s manslaughter. Under the scheme, they were due to be freed in January.

Tom Wheatley, the president of the Prison Governors’ Association, said further changes to the scheme could lead to prisoners being wrongly released. “Mistakes are more likely due to having to recalculate sentences against a very tight deadline,” he said.

Wheatley said removing overseas prisoners was likely to be difficult as many would resist this and a number did not have proper documentation. He said victims would also want to be sure that anyone sent back to home countries would properly serve their sentence there.

Mark Fairhurst, the national chair of the Prison Officers’ Association, said placing men in women’s prisons would require investment and retraining. “Repurposing women’s prisons to accommodate male prisoners is not something that can simply be achieved by changing the sign above the door,” he said.

“There would inevitably be additional staffing, training, security and operational requirements. “The physical design and facilities of women’s prisons are not necessarily suitable for a male population, and those differences would need to be properly assessed before any decision was taken.

“You cannot create safe prison places simply by finding additional buildings. You need the staff, training, infrastructure and resources to operate them safely.”

The Ministry of Justice said it was disappointed by the Napo strike vote and it would continue to engage with the union. Officials in Norris’s department are understood to be working at full speed on preparing detailed and workable plans to free up spaces by September.

When the early release scheme was announced, there were exemptions for prisoners convicted of rape, serious child sexual abuse and grooming offences but not manslaughter, prompting pressure to block the release of Harper’s killers, including from police and his family.

Officials initially said it would be unrealistic to just block the release of Cole and Bowers and that exempting all manslaughter cases risked not creating enough prison spaces to ease the crisis. But in his Facebook post, and speaking to reporters on a later visit to Derbyshire, Burnham said he was aware of the strength of public feeling about the plans.

“It’s my job to speak up for the public on this issue,” he said during the visit. “I take that duty very seriously indeed, and I’m confident that there’s more that can be done, and will be done to address public concern.”

He said on Facebook he understood “the depth of public concern about the potential early release of those involved with the killing of PC Andrew Harper – and I share it”.

He wrote: “I am increasingly confident that a more focused approach along these lines will allow us to reduce the number of people released early and, in the case of the most serious offenders like those convicted of the killing of PC Harper, prevent their early release altogether.

“In advance of the justice secretary’s proposals being presented to parliament, we will seek to engage other political parties in discussions with the aim of gaining the widest possible public and political consensus on the approach being taken.”

Last week, Napo warned that ministers seemingly did not “have a clue about how ill-equipped the probation service is to take on this additional work” connected to early release, such as informing victims that a perpetrator was being released.

Announcing the result of the strike ballot, Ben Cockburn, the Napo national chair, said the vote was “professionals warning they cannot protect the public properly when there are not enough people to do the work”.

A union statement said: “The result should come as a blow to government as it prepares to expand early release from prison and implement Sentencing Act changes, increasing the number of people managed in the community while probation is already struggling to cope.”

Harper, 28, was fatally injured when he got caught in a strap attached to the back of a car and was dragged down a country road as the killers fled the scene of a quad bike theft in Sulhamstead, Berkshire, in 2019.

Cole and Bowers, two of three men sentenced over the police officer’s death, are due to be released halfway through their 13-year sentences, having been convicted of manslaughter in 2020. A petition demanding an intervention has nearly 900,000 signatures.

The early release changes were part of the Sentencing Act passed earlier this year that cut the minimum amount of time many offenders would have to spend in prison.

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 18:15:54 GMT

Reform deputy leader condemned for seeking disclosure of documents that could reveal reporter’s sources

Reform’s deputy leader Richard Tice has been accused of using “nasty tactics” in an attempt to intimidate journalists and deflect attention from his own financial affairs after issuing a legal threat to the Guardian.

In a letter sent from his lawyers, a Guardian correspondent was singled out for her reporting on a series of Tice’s financial transactions that were referred to the National Crime Agency.

Tice is now suing the NCA, claiming that the information could only have come from an officer within the agency.

In a separate letter to the Guardian, Tice’s lawyers made clear they would be seeking disclosure of documents that could reveal the details of sources.

John Whittingdale, a former Conservative culture secretary, condemned the move.

“The essence of a free press is that journalists can use verified information from sources without being forced to reveal who they are. This betrays a failure to understand how important that principle is to journalism.”

Margaret Hodge, the peer and former Labour MP and anti-corruption campaigner, said: “It is never acceptable for individuals to seemingly abuse our laws to silence investigative journalism and close down public criticism. That’s why we need to urgently reform our laws to stop rich people using their wealth to close down legitimate scrutiny.”

Jemimah Steinfeld, the chief executive at the Index on Censorship, added: “This has the appearance of an attempt to force a journalist to break the golden rule: that sources must be protected.

“This has been vital public interest reporting by the Guardian. The sources of such journalism are sacred. If the facts are incorrect, then they can be challenged, but the sources should not be chased down.

“This appears to be a tactic designed to not only silence journalists but those who talk to them. It is a nasty tactic and we should all be concerned.”

Theo Bamber, the chief executive at the News Media Association, said that “robust protection for journalists’ sources is absolutely fundamental to ensuring that reporters can scrutinise authority and hold the powerful to account on behalf of the people impacted by their actions and decisions.

“The use of legal threats by an individual or organisation trying to force journalists to disclose their confidential sources is an attack on this important principle, and one that must be resisted at every turn.”

Tice and Reform’s leader, Nigel Farage, have been under intense scrutiny in recent months because of a continuing investigation by the Guardian into their financial affairs.

The Guardian revealed in a series of detailed reports that Reform UK received a secret £1m donation via a fundraising vehicle run by Tice and a secret £5m gift made to Farage shortly before he announced he would stand in the 2024 general election.

The Guardian also revealed that these and other transactions were reported by bankers to the National Crime Agency over fears they could involve laundered money.

Throughout its reports the Guardian has referred to financial industry sources.

Tice has suggested in a document filed at the high court that the Guardian’s award-winning City editor, Anna Isaac, could not have had sources at multiple banks.

In legal correspondence sent directly to Isaac, Tice’s lawyers suggested he is seeking to unmask sources based on his own assessment of the reporter’s work.

Farage has also issued proceedings against the NCA.

A GNM spokesperson said: “Richard Tice’s legal threat against the Guardian appears to be a calculated attempt to deflect attention away from legitimate questions about his financial affairs and the funding of Reform UK.

“Instead of providing answers to those questions, he seems intent on intimidating journalists and seeking to expose sources.

“The Guardian will oppose these attacks on the freedom of the press and will not be deterred from public interest-driven investigations into politicians and the parties they represent.”

Farage previously claimed – without providing any evidence – that the details of the £5m gift must have come from Russian spies hacking his phone.

The Guardian described Farage’s claim as “absurd” and “an attempt to deflect attention from legitimate scrutiny of his financial affairs”. The spokesperson added: “Nigel Farage is once again hiding behind a baseless attack on the media rather than facing up to scrutiny from journalists and politicians.”

The Guardian view on wildfires: firefighters can’t handle this crisis alone

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 18:04:47 GMT

The response to this summer’s record heat and drought should be led by ministers – not left to the emergency services

The wildfire that began on protected heathland in the New Forest in Hampshire on Sunday was reported to be under control on Tuesday. But with temperatures forecast to reach 36C or higher in parts of England on Thursday, the risk of further outbreaks remains high. Firefighters responded to 185 wildfires in the first 10 days of August. In coastal Suffolk, conservationists and locals are counting the cost of the blaze that devastated Dunwich Heath. Its vegetation and rare bird populations are expected to take decades to recover.

Although the immediate cause of many wildfires is litter, the risks are elevated by heat and drought, and repeated warnings from climate scientists do not necessarily lessen the shock and distress caused. As people and governments across Europe assess this summer’s damage, experts including the UN climate chief, Simon Stiell, hope that the fires will focus minds on the urgent necessity of cutting emissions – as well as investing in emergency planning. Europe is currently heating up faster than any other continent, due in part to proximity to the melting Arctic.

The global, existential nature of the climate crisis makes it an awkward fit with Andy Burnham’s current focus on “everyday fixes”. But if he does not find more to say about the summer’s disasters, he is in danger of being left behind. The National Trust has already surpassed its £500,000 fundraising target to restore Dunwich Heath. Conservationists and farmers are publicly debating the best ways to prevent fires. The Fire Brigades Union (FBU) is pushing for 5,000 additional firefighters across the UK, along with new equipment.

In March, the then fire safety minister, Samantha Dixon, said that the fire and rescue service had suffered from “decades of drift and inertia”. Her speech also confirmed that a new College of Fire and Rescue would oversee professional standards, as recommended by the Grenfell inquiry. But three weeks ago Ms Dixon was sacked. If the government wants to avoid an impression that drift and inertia are ongoing, they need to provide an update, along with a response to repeated calls from the fire service inspectorate for new enforcement powers. MPs on the home affairs committee threw their weight behind this idea two years ago.

The fire and rescue service is popular with the public. Even highly critical sections of the Grenfell inquiry report did not significantly undermine confidence in it. This summer’s events seem likely to reaffirm this positive regard, and rightly so: public servants who do dangerous, difficult jobs, when many other people are on holiday, deserve to be appreciated. But scrutiny of the service is also important. An inquiry by the home affairs select committee, which was summarised in a letter to the home secretary in 2024, contained strong criticism of its culture and leadership.

Such concerns deserve a fuller response than they have so far received from ministers. So do the FBU’s call for investment to cope with rising risks, and proposals for a temporary ban on disposable barbecues. Ill-informed and ideological attempts to blame rewilding should also be addressed by ministers. Working groups on how to reduce wildfire risks must produce plans. The latest report from the government’s Climate Change Committee found that existing adaptation plans are not fit for purpose. This summer’s extraordinary heat, drought and wildfires should be a spur to action on both the fire service and the wider climate crisis.

The Guardian view on AI money in US politics: not the way to hold an urgent democratic debate

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 18:04:02 GMT

Amid public concern over the impact of new technology, Silicon Valley’s use of its wealth to influence specific election races is an unwelcome development

As the huge societal ramifications of artificial intelligence have become clear, some of the tech industry’s most senior figures have taken to writing thinkpieces on what comes next. The latest to do so is Mark Zuckerberg, who this week published 6,500 words making the case for a laissez-faire approach to AI development. Worrying too much about regulation, Meta’s CEO unsurprisingly opines, could lead to the United States falling behind in the race to own the future.

Mr Zuckerberg’s plea for maximum autonomy is, of course, self-serving. Much of his essay – which airily envisions “invention superpowers” for every entrepreneur and “an abundance of jobs in the future” – reads like unadulterated tech-utopianism. But the lobbying at least has the merit of being out there in the open. As midterm elections loom in the US, darker arts are also being used to counter popular concern about what AI will mean for a host of topics, from the labour market to child safety, the environment and cybersecurity.

Replicating a strategy deployed by cryptocurrency backers in the 2024 election cycle, Silicon Valley is pouring hundreds of millions of dollars into Super Pacs – groups unaffiliated to political parties or campaigns – to ensure that voices sympathetic to its deregulatory AI agenda get elected. OpenAI’s co-founder and president, Greg Brockman, and Palantir’s co-founder, Jo Lonsdale, have given large sums to Leading the Future, an organisation dedicated to “identifying, maintaining, and growing pro-AI candidates”. Vast amounts are being spent to try to tilt the balance in individual contests, sometimes through disingenuous means.

Earlier this summer, Leading the Future spent $6m on ads attacking Alex Bores, a New York state assemblyman and pioneer of AI regulation at state level. Rather than focus on his AI campaigning, the ads attacked Mr Bores’s progressive credentials, alleging that he had carried out tech work in the past for US Immigration and Customs Enforcement, which he denies. Running for Congress in a Democratic stronghold, he lost a tight primary race after battling what was in essence a smear campaign.

Countervailing forces are emerging. Guardrails Alliance, a union-backed Super Pac, is attempting to combat Leading the Future’s influence, while a bipartisan groundswell of opposition to datacentres is generating serious electoral clout. But as tech companies and their billionaire owners seek to shape and above all limit the scope of AI regulation, their influence is distorting debate in a political culture where the power of money already holds far too much sway.

In his essay, Mr Zuckerberg urges that AI’s democratising potential be protected from those who believe “a small set of experts … should decide what is best for humanity”. He appears to miss the irony that an unelected tech oligarchy, to which he belongs, is already performing that function by default. Its pressure has been instrumental in persuading Donald Trump to cancel the signing of an executive order which would have given the government the right to review new AI models. Its wealth is now being used to attack and undermine dissenting voices in the public square.

If a democratic debate about the benefits and dangers of AI is to take place, such presumption must be fiercely resisted in the US and elsewhere. The semi-covert manipulation of political discussion is no way to map out an inclusive future in challenging times.

How viable are plans to free up prison space in England and Wales?

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:58:23 GMT

The PM is proposing three ways of increasing jail capacity – but none offer a quick or straightforward solution

Andy Burnham said on Monday he was “increasingly confident” that PC Andrew Harper’s killers would not be freed in the new year.

Determined to ensure that Albert Bowers and Jessie Cole, who were jailed for 13 years in 2020 for manslaughter, serve their full sentences, he has asked the justice secretary, Alex Norris, to examine three options for increasing prison capacity.

The options outlined by Burnham are speeding up the deportation of foreign offenders, releasing female prisoners from jail and reviewing indefinite sentences given to “lower-risk” prisoners.

How feasible are they?

There were 10,134 foreign nationals held in prisons across England and Wales at the end of June 2026, according to the most recent Ministry of Justice figures.

Removing them from the UK would therefore be a major political coup for Burnham.

But there are several major obstacles.

Going back to Tony Blair, governments have pledged to clear jails of foreign prisoners, but all have failed.

Andrew Neilson, the director of campaigns at the Howard League for Penal Reform, said the government would struggle to achieve any immediate relief for the overcrowding crisis by seeking to deport foreign prisoners.

He said: “Reciprocal agreements will have to be drawn up with many of the countries involved and they take time to draw up, and so it does not provide immediate relief to the capacity crisis.”

Tom Wheatley, the president of the Prison Governors’ Association, said: “Releasing foreign offenders is difficult because many of them don’t want to go, and quite often they are undocumented. If they have come over on small boats, you do not necessarily know where you might be able to deport them to.

“Many victims will want to see that justice has been served. If someone has been physically attacked or raped by someone from a foreign country which is corrupt, it may well be difficult to guarantee that their attacker’s sentence will be served.”

This is an option that has been examined and re-examined by successive governments.

Ministry of Justice officials have been seriously examining schemes to divert female offenders out of jail for more than a year.

Burnham could adopt approaches pioneered by the Greater Manchester authority to steer women away from prison for lower-level offences. These reduced the number being jailed in the region by 55% in eight years.

Turning the biggest women’s prison – HMP Bronzefield in Ashford, Surrey – into a men’s jail would free up more than 500 spaces.

Neilson said: “There should be scope to reduce the number of women in custody, but it is difficult to assess whether Burnham’s plans would work without more detail.”

Wheatley said there would be difficulties in quickly converting women’s prisons so they could hold men.

He said: “Many women’s prisons would not meet the security measures needed for male prisoners.

“For example, some of the prisoner accommodation at HMP Styal is essentially a set of houses within a secure perimeter. The security standards would not meet the requirements needed for men.

“HMP Bronzefield is a newer, secure prison that could be used for men, but it needs to hold women so that women can be brought to London courts.”

Imprisonment for public protection sentences were introduced in 2005 for dangerous offenders whose crimes did not warrant a life sentence. They allowed offenders to be detained for a potentially unlimited period until they could prove that they were no longer a threat to the public. If released back into the community on licence, they could be returned to custody.

Although IPP sentences were abolished in 2012, the change was not retrospective, and more than 2,200 people remain in prison despite having served many years beyond their minimum tariff.

Neilson said: “Around 70% of IPP prisoners are in category C or D prisons so the system does not see them as a great risk.

“But identifying them and then putting in place the structures to be able to help people who have been in jail for many years would not be a quick fix.

“People do recognise that people who have been in prison for many, many years will require proper support. It will not free up space immediately.”

The Office actor Lucy Davis says she has incurable stage four breast cancer

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:55:49 GMT

Star, 53, says disease has metastasised to her bones and it is too late for chemotherapy

Lucy Davis, the actor best known for playing Dawn Tinsley in The Office, has said she has incurable stage four breast cancer.

The 53-year-old said she was diagnosed a year and a half ago and the disease had metastasised to her bones including her spine, right hip and ribs. She said it was too late for chemotherapy.

Davis wrote on Instagram on Tuesday: “As of now, I’m trying to live out whatever might be left of my life in as fun a way as I possibly can.

“I always like to find the teachable moments in anything negative that happens. And cancer has not disappointed in that regard; there’s a lot I have learned from it, and I’m grateful for that.

“As some of you will know, the pain can really be something else. Standing and walking for too long a time can be hard, and I need to use a wheelchair sometimes (so if you see me toddling around in one, feel free to give me a push!).”

Davis, the daughter of the comedian Jasper Carrott, said one of the most important things that had helped her was humour, asking her friends and family to poke fun at her “as much as possible” and to not treat her like a “sick person”.

“There’s nothing that will make you feel more unwell than being treated as if you’re unwell,” she added.

“I’m not scared of whatever comes next. I’m at peace with it. I’ll see my Gracie [her late dog] sooner than expected, and for me leaving my physical body just means going home. Any and all grieving is for my family; it’s far harder for them than for me.”

Davis rose to fame playing the receptionist Dawn in the original version of The Office, which followed the lives of workers at the Slough paper merchant Wernham Hogg, with the show’s co-creator Ricky Gervais playing the manager David Brent.

She played Hayley Jordan on BBC Radio 4’s The Archers for nearly 10 years and appeared in the 2004 comedy Shaun of the Dead opposite Simon Pegg and in the Netflix series Chilling Adventures of Sabrina.

She said she would continue working for animal rights and would like to keep working as an actor, a job she said was one of the “biggest joys” of her life.

She said: “For those of you on a similar journey to mine, I do wish you well. Cancer asks a lot from us, physically and mentally. And we all get to do it however we choose.”
