‘Abusive’ man found guilty of harassing Stella Creasy MP on tube train

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:46:42 GMT

Clifford Wilby, 63, blocked the Walthamstow MP from leaving, filmed her and talked about her ‘failings as a member of parliament’

A 63-year-old man has been found guilty of harassing the Labour MP Stella Creasy after he was “abusive and aggressive” to her on a tube train.

Clifford Wilby, of east London, saw the MP for Walthamstow in July 2025 on a Victoria Line train, Westminster magistrates court heard.

He blocked her from leaving the tube carriage and started talking to her about knife crime and her “failings as a member of parliament” and then filmed her on his mobile phone, the court heard.

Other passengers intervened and got in between them before Creasy left the carriage.

Sarah Madden, prosecuting, said the pair first met during a Zoom meeting in 2024 in Creasy’s capacity as an MP. The meeting was terminated due to Wilby’s behaviour.

Creasy, giving evidence from behind a screen, said during the online meeting he “made a series of very serious allegations”, became abusive and “referred to the fact he had been diagnosed as a psychopath”.

She said he started to make a complaint about a constituent and she reminded him that she could not discuss somebody else’s case.

He became “abusive, aggressive” and they terminated the conversation, said Creasy.

Creasy said that on 15 July last year she had boarded a Victoria line train and was intending to move further down the train at the next stop, and looked up to get off the train and made eye contact with the defendant.

“He said ‘are you Stella Creasy?’, at that point my memory kicked in and I made the connection because I knew his face, he then confirmed he was Clifford Wilby,” said the MP.

“He stood up and blocked my ability to leave the carriage, he began talking to me about his concerns about my constituent, he was shouting although the tube train is a loud environment.”

She added: “Obviously I was concerned that I was in an enclosed space with him. He was very close.

“I stated very clearly this wasn’t an appropriate conversation to be having.”

Creasy said at this point she was backed up against the door of the carriage and was holding her hands out as a gesture “to say stop”.

She got off the train at Finsbury Park by exiting through another door further up the carriage.

Tess McCarthy, defending, put to the MP that she could have got off the train as the incident continued to which Creasy replied “no”.

Wilby said he had been hoping to continue the conversation about the constituent on the tube but the MP “became quite agitated” and he was “quite shocked by her reaction”, so he pressed record on his phone “for my safety”.

He added: “At no point did I stand in front of her. When I first spoke to her she was actually getting off the train and she turned around to speak to me, I stood up because it was noisy.”

The defendant also denied telling Creasy in 2024 he had been diagnosed as a psychopath.

The deputy chief magistrate Tan Ikram found Wilby guilty of using threatening, abusive, or insulting words or behaviour to cause harassment, alarm, or distress and adjourned sentencing until 8 September for reports.

Since Bumble’s launch in 2014, only women ‌could initiate conversations in heterosexual matches on the app

Bumble on Tuesday said it would begin allowing either person ⁠in a match to send the first message, dropping the women-first messaging rule ⁠that has ⁠been ​a defining feature of the dating app since its launch in 2014.

The move ⁠comes as dating-app companies across the globe grapple with user fatigue toward traditional swipe-based platforms and slowing ⁠growth, prompting some, including Bumble and Tinder-owner Match Group, to ​roll out new ‌features and artificial intelligence-powered ‌tools aimed at improving engagement.

Since Bumble’s inception, only women ‌could initiate conversations in heterosexual matches on the app, unlike most dating apps, including Tinder, where either user can send the first message.

The company ​is also extending the reply window for a match to 72 hours from 24 hours as it seeks to increase conversations and reduce the number of matches that expire ⁠without interaction.

The changes are a response to ​demands ​from users for “more flexibility, less ​pressure, and more opportunities to create real, ​meaningful connections”, ‌founder and ​CEO Whitney ​Wolfe Herd said.

Infantino’s opponents developing new framework for running world football

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:32:58 GMT

Uefa, Concacaf and AFC working together on system

Fifa has ignored calls for an independent review

Gianni Infantino’s opponents are working together to produce a new framework for running world football to replace what they regard as a system of patronage that Fifa has adopted since he replaced Sepp Blatter as president 10 years ago.

Repeated calls from Uefa, Concacaf and the Asian Football Confederation for an independent review of Fifa’s governance have been ignored by Infantino since his plan to sell off 21% of the World Cup was made public a fortnight ago, and the rebels are now understood to have taken matters into their own hands.

A source with knowledge of the discussions told the Guardian that the three confederations are working on producing a policy document to demonstrate how the world governing body could evolve in the future.

Those involved have been empowered to ask existential questions such as what Fifa’s precise role should be, how it should be structured and what governance model should be adopted. Financial distribution among member associations is also a key consideration, particularly with an eye on how best to deploy funds from Fifa’s estimated $5bn (£3.7bn) reserves.

If Infantino refuses to engage then the new framework is likely to be adopted by a challenger at the presidential election next year, or could even serve as a template for a breakaway organisation.

After releasing a joint statement on Monday, which called for “leadership that serves football, not seeks to command it”, Uefa, Concacaf and the AFC let it be known that they have begun preliminary discussions about organising new competitions if the standoff with Fifa’s leadership persists.

At present the Under-20 Women’s World Cup next month could take place without European teams, as Uefa is maintaining that its members will boycott the tournament, unless Fifa moves quickly to set up an independent review of the World Cup sell‑off debacle and provide legally binding assurances that such a move will not be repeated.

The Concacaf president, Victor Montagliani, is seen as the most likely presidential challenger to Infantino at this stage – although, as he told the Guardian in June, his current focus is on being reelected as president of the North and Central American confederation next year.

It seemed significant, however, that the joint statement released on Monday contained strong echoes of a speech given by Montagliani at the Fifa congress this year in Vancouver, where he said: “Let me be frank, leadership is not about power.”

Uefa continues to hold firm in its pledge to boycott Fifa competitions, having not received its desired assurances that there will be no recurrence of the issues that caused the current crisis.

Will Andy Burnham’s everyday fixes be enough to get the public on his side?

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:30:14 GMT

The prime minister’s money-saving policies are popular but they are yet to convince voters he can take on the big things

It was last year that Martin Lewis, the money-saving guru, advised the government to find “100 small things that piss people off” and set about sorting them out.

That approach has been adopted almost immediately by Andy Burnham since entering office, as he began his programme of “everyday fixes” to tackle problems that can be dealt with through minor policy tweaks.

Labour strategists say the philosophy behind this is to show that Burnham notices the frustrations of people’s lives, and that he will be decisive about improving these issues.

This started with scrapping VAT on electricity bills and capping bus fares. His latest policy assault is on garish signs on the high street and everything from excessive vape and betting shops to subscriptions that auto-renew without warning.

It is applying to politics the “broken window” theory of crime that suggests clamping down on small-scale antisocial behaviour such as littering can help discourage more serious crime.

As much as anything, Burnham’s moves against rip-off Britain should be a warning to companies that the government is paying attention to their behaviour towards voters, as he mulls over how far to go with a programme of increasing public control over essential services.

The intention is a long way from Keir Starmer’s promise to be a government that treads more lightly on people’s lives, as the new prime minister has taken a bet that voters want leaders who are active about everyday frustrations as well as embarking on major structural reform.

Burnham’s instinct to show the public that he is prepared to act on their behalf is a sound one, according to Peter Kellner, the pollster and former chair of YouGov, who has written recently about the difficulty of winning round “ungrateful voters”.

“Probably the best any government can do in the short run – short of some educational programme about where our taxes go – is gain the perception that they as politicians are on the voters’ sides. This is the key to the Burnham bounce. Plainly the amounts of money involved on bus fares and VAT and so on are small in the great order of things.

“But they are part of a story about where Burnham stands in relation to the responsibility of the government to look after people. How long this lasts I don’t know because we have yet to see what’s in the budget. And if at the end of the day they announce tax rises, then that changes. But if I were his communications adviser, this is what I would have advised him to do.”

He said Tony Blair and Margaret Thatcher successfully told the stories of their premierships in their first five to six years, and that Burnham needs to do something similar in terms of building a narrative. “What we have yet to see is Burnham joining the dots of what he’s done so far. And it’s got to be something clearer than Starmer saying life is difficult and we are doing the best we can and we want to make Britain fairer and more prosperous,” Kellner says.

However, there is also a risk for Burnham that hailing small changes as achievements will veer into the dreaded territory of consumer gimmicks that American strategist David Axelrod warned against.

The former Obama adviser, who was drafted in to help Ed Miliband’s 2015 campaign, memorably branded Labour’s approach on the cost of living in that election as: “Vote Labour and win a microwave”. In other words, it was a consumer retail offer of short-term bargains without an overarching big and inspiring message to pull it all together.

Another cautionary tale can be found in the fate of the Joe Biden and Kamala Harris administration. Together they targeted price gouging and unfair consumer practices. But while the overall economy began to improve under Biden, voters never give the US government credit for small changes like holding down medicine bills and expanded child tax credits because they were unhappy about the lasting effects of food and housing costs inflation that had an effect globally.

It will be a test for Burnham how far he can reap the rewards of dealing with consumer frustrations at a time when he has little room for bigger giveaways to help with the cost of living while the economy is barely growing.

Burnham must also guard against a third possible peril: the potential for mockery if the issues seem too inconsequential for a prime minister to be bothering with – in the vein of John Major’s “cones hotline” for people to report nuisance roadworks.

The new prime minister, however, seems aware of these pitfalls. He is openly flagging the policies as small wins – while promising bigger structural change to take place in the longer term on social care and public ownership of utilities.

Labour was also ready to paint Kemi Badenoch, the Conservative leader, as out of touch when she tried to ridicule the cost of living policies by saying: “Is that it? The problems go way beyond bus fares and VAT on bills.”

Polling so far seems to suggest that voters are tentatively impressed and moving back towards Labour at a time when both Reform UK and the Greens are sliding. However, the latest data from Ipsos Mori says seven in 10 remain dissatisfied with the way the government is running the country. While they make think Burnham is outperforming the other parties and a substantial number feel he is on their side compared with other leaders, voters are still going to take some convincing that the new prime minister really will be able to change their lives for the better.

What effect will Donald Trump’s childhood vaccine recommendations have in the US and beyond?

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:14:08 GMT

Is there any science behind the recommendations? Will they result in lower vaccination rates? And will they cause a resurgence of preventable diseases?

A new executive order from the US president, Donald Trump, calls for sweeping changes to childhood vaccine recommendations.

His plans would result in fewer vaccines being recommended overall; the combined measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine being replaced with three separate shots, and all childhood immunisations being administered during separate medical visits “to the maximum extent feasible”.

Is there any science behind the proposals? And what are the likely implications inside and outside the US border?

The main outcome will be “confusion for parents”, Dr Aaron Milstone of the American Academy of Pediatrics told Reuters, with conflicting messages likely to mean delayed vaccinations.

During any delay, some children may be infected with deadly vaccine-preventable diseases. Measles cases are already at a 30-year high in the US.

An executive order has no immediate, legal effect on the public – but it is likely to put pressure on US states to make changes to requirements that children be vaccinated before attending schools. And it sets the stage for legal challenges against states that have implemented their own vaccine recommendations.

The recommendations are unlikely to be copied in other countries, said Ben Kasstan-Dabush, assistant professor in global health and development at the London School of Hygiene & Tropical Medicine: “No country would want to deal with the inevitably costly outbreak responses that would occur as a result of this policy decision.”

But ideas as well as diseases spread easily across borders. The short-term risk is that parents in other countries get the false impression that the vaccines they are being offered for their children are not safe, and delay or avoid vaccination, putting their own children at risk.

The longer-term risk is that the misinformation leads to lower vaccination rates at a population level, and the world sees a resurgence of diseases that could have been prevented.

“There is a risk of the confused messaging affecting people outside the US,” said Prof Sir Andrew Pollard, director of the Oxford Vaccine Group. “Confidence in vaccines, and getting jabbed, is critical for the health of a population.”

Shortly after Trump’s announcement, the World Health Organization defended the science-based process used to set child immunisation schedules in countries around the world.

WHO spokesperson Tarik Jašarević told a press briefing in Geneva it was important that countries’ vaccine schedule recommendations be made by independent experts and “free of industry and political influence”.

“When these standards are not upheld, trust in vaccines, health experts and scientific institutions, like WHO, can erode,” he added.

Trump himself has many times repeated discredited claims that vaccination causes autism, and appeared to make the same link when signing the order.

The US health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, is a leading vaccination sceptic, who has for years amplified misinformation about vaccines. One study found he was the top “superspreader” of Covid-19 vaccine misinformation on Twitter (now X) in 2021.

Kennedy has tried to reshape US vaccination schedules but has found his attempts blocked by a federal judge. A White House official told reporters that was a separate issue to the executive order.

In 2025, UK vaccine experts called on public health agencies in other countries to get “Kennedy ready”. They argued that “the political agenda against vaccination in the United States raises implications for routine childhood vaccination programmes in the United Kingdom and countries around the world, due to the transnational spread of diseases and ideas”.

No. There is “strong evidence of no link”, said Helen Bedford, professor of child public health at UCL. “The vaccine has, and continues to save, millions of lives.”

False claims of a link between the MMR vaccine and autism were made by British doctor Andrew Wakefield in 1998 in the Lancet medical journal. That paper was retracted, and Wakefield was struck off by the UK’s medical regulator. But he has been lauded by anti-vaccination groups, and attended Trump’s first inauguration.

“The three vaccines have never been administered as separate doses to young children as part of vaccination policy so that policy’s safety and effectiveness are unproven,” said Bedford. “However, just by delaying protection it would be a serious step backwards for protecting our children’s health.”

In fact, says Kasstan-Dabush, giving them separately amounts to “an experiment with children’s health”.

Adam Finn, emeritus professor of paediatrics at the University of Bristol, raised a more practical issue for the US and many other countries.

“Dividing the vaccine into separate components makes no logical sense and is infeasible at this time in any case as no such vaccines are available,” he added.

Studies of communities with low vaccination rates typically find that, while hesitancy can be an issue, accessibility, such as the logistics of getting children to health appointments also play a role. If parents can only get one shot in an appointment, they may not return for the rest.

“Spacing vaccines across many separate visits increases the likelihood of delayed or missed doses and leaves children susceptible for longer, with no demonstrated safety benefit in healthy children,” said Azeem Majeed, professor of primary care and public health, Imperial College London.

Salad is supposed to be good for you – so what’s behind all the cases of lettuce poisoning?

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:03:44 GMT

Iceberg lettuce has been blamed for a surge in American cases of the parasitic illness cyclosporiasis. Symptoms include ‘frequent and sometimes explosive bowel movements’

Name: Salad.

Age: Legend has it that in the Byzantine period, the Greek island of Kos was a centre for growing a long-leafed variety of lettuce that your grandparents might know as cos, though that variety is now more commonly referred to as romaine.

I remember it from the Brexit campaign – the leaves v the romaines. Don’t forget Liz Truss and her iceberg lettuce.

I was trying to. Just as I’m now trying to be healthier by eating more salad. Unless it kills you.

EXCUSE ME?! On 4 August, there had been 10,468 laboratory-confirmed cases of the parasitic illness cyclosporiasis in the US since May and more than 12,255 additional cases requiring further investigation. There had also been two deaths.

What are the symptoms? Vomiting, watery diarrhoea, “with frequent and sometimes explosive bowel movements”, says the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Haven’t I heard it referred to as Trump diarrhoea? It has been – by late-night host Ike Barinholtz, criticising the response to the outbreak, though the president can’t be held directly responsible for this one.

Who or what can? The US Food and Drug Administration has identified one culprit: iceberg lettuce imported from Mexico.

What about the rest of the world? Cyclosporiasis isn’t endemic in Europe, at least, although there has been a rise in cases among travellers returning to the UK from the US and Mexico. However, the UK Health Security Agency saw a sharp rise in cases of shiga toxin-producing E coli last year.

Symptoms? Vomiting, diarrhoea, stomach cramps and fever.

There’s going to be a salad connection, isn’t there? The largest outbreak, in 2024, which affected more than 250 people, 86 of whom were admitted to hospital, was thought to be caused by prepacked sandwiches containing … contaminated lettuce.

I wonder what a microbiologist would say about it? Dr Primrose Freestone, an associate professor of clinical microbiology at the University of Leicester, told the Times that she “wouldn’t touch a bagged salad in a million years”.

Because? The natural microflora on raw leaves can become infected by flies and wasps, which can carry pathogenic bacteria, including E coli and Salmonella.

Why is lettuce more dangerous when it’s bagged? It turns out that the moist environment of the bag provides a great breeding ground for bacteria and pathogens. Freestone’s research found that pathogens grow more than 1,000 times better in a salad bag.

Boiling would kill bacteria, wouldn’t it? True, but it would also render your salad inedible. Freestone recommends washing everything well, and if you must get bagged leaves, refrigerating them, then eating them promptly. “Salad, lettuce and side salad infections can kill,” she warns.

Side salads? In restaurants, you don’t know how long they have been sitting around.

Do say: “Just with chips please.”

Don’t say: “And let’s not forget the Titanic was sunk by an iceberg.”

To all the betting shops littering our high streets: people power is coming and the odds are against you

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:01:17 GMT

Too many shops are blighting too many communities. With the rule change I fought for now on the horizon, ordinary people can fight back

How much gambling do you want in your high street? Are you happy with all the betting shops, the problems they cause, the effect on your environment?

It’s worth asking because for years, local councils have been expected to say yes to new gambling venues, even when communities made clear they did not want them. Now, under new rules announced on Monday, that’s about to change. And about time.

Speaking to local residents I hear a clear message time and again: people are fed up with seeing so much gambling where they live, while having little say over whether new venues open.

I knew grappling with this issue would be an uphill battle. The gambling industry is lucrative and deeply embedded in our communities. When I looked into why councils were struggling to stop new betting shops, casinos and adult gaming centres opening, I discovered the problem.

At the heart of it was the outdated “aim to permit” principle in the Gambling Act 2005. It meant councils were effectively working against a presumption that gambling premises should be permitted, leaving them limited ability to say no. This one principle is the root of the crisis on our high streets: it has stripped local people of any real power to shape their own communities, and let the industry expand largely unchecked.

So in 2021 I started my campaign to put power back where it belongs: in the hands of councils and local communities. Today, after years of campaigning, I’m delighted to say that we’ve won.

The government has announced it is scrapping aim to permit, giving councils and communities greater power over what happens on their high streets. This is a huge victory for London, and for communities nationwide. But it took years of pressure and persistence to get here.

I took a minister to Harlesden high street to see the reality for themselves. I launched a petition so Londoners could make their voices heard. I organised a cross-party letter backed by more than 280 MPs, councillors, mayors and gambling reform advocates, including the then mayor of Greater Manchester, Andy Burnham. I took the campaign into parliament too, raising it at prime minister’s questions and leading a debate.

During my summer tour last year, I took the campaign into communities across London, listening to what people wanted to see on their high streets, and meeting families affected by gambling harms, such as the campaigner Jackie Olden, who watched her mother’s life spiral out of control thanks to a slot-machine addiction.

It was a shock to discover how much gambling machines generate: B3 (higher-stakes) gambling machines – essentially slot/fruit machine-style games, found in places such as betting shops, adult gaming centres and casinos – generate an average of £35,000 in gross gambling yield per machine each year – more than the roughly £26,400 a year earned by someone aged 21 or over working 40 hours a week on the national living wage. The scale of the problem is clear in Brent, where gambling premises outnumber supermarkets in 17 of Brent’s 22 wards. We have 81 licensed gambling venues across the borough. For my constituents, this is about improving the places they live and raise their families. People want to be proud of their high streets again.

The consequences of gambling-related harm can be devastating. A 2023 report estimated there could be between 117 and 496 gambling-related suicides in England each year. I’ve worked with campaigners and advocates with lived experience of gambling harm, and highlighted organisations such as Gambling with Lives and Gambling Harm UK, whose expertise helped make the case for reform.

Sign up to Matters of Opinion

Guardian columnists and writers on what they’ve been debating, thinking about, reading, and more

after newsletter promotion

I’m grateful to residents who signed my petition, representatives who backed my campaign, councils including Brent council that pushed for this change, and every reform advocate who helped build momentum. I’m also grateful to Andy Burnham, who followed through on his support by committing to make this bold change.

Our high streets should work for local people – not for companies profiting from harm. No longer should residents feel powerless when they see changes on their doorstep. That is why scrapping aim to permit matters: it will make a real, tangible difference to people’s lives.

With the support of Londoners and campaigners across the country, we fought, organised and delivered. So, this is your chance: take back your high streets. Get involved, hold your council to account – use these new powers to demand and shape the neighbourhoods you deserve.

Dawn Butler is the Labour MP for Brent East

Flat racing’s biggest draw Bow Echo in shock retirement after training injury

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 17:00:38 GMT

Unbeaten colt’s career cut short after six races

Boughey: ‘He has given us some extraordinary days’

The trainer George Boughey announced on Tuesday the shock retirement of his superstar colt Bow Echo because of injury.

The three‑year-old had cemented his reputation as the outstanding miler in Europe at the Glorious Goodwood meeting two weeks ago, defying the odds by coming from what looked an impossible position a furlong out to win the Sussex Stakes.

Unbeaten in three juvenile starts last season, the Night Of Thunder colt confirmed himself the best of his generation over a mile with a breathtaking victory in the 2,000 Guineas at Newmarket before overcoming trouble in running twice since, in the St James’s Palace Stakes at Royal Ascot and then when making it six successes from six outings when beating his elders in the epic Goodwood contest.

Bow Echo was considered to be on the cusp of joining the small band of horses with a genuine claim to be a “public horse”, a thoroughbred in the process of transcending the sport and building a devoted following outside the racing cognoscenti. The colt’s popularity was enhanced by the exploits in the saddle of Billy Loughnane, the baby‑faced 20‑year‑old now odds on to be champion jockey after a meteoric rise.

The Newmarket-based Boughey had been preparing Bow Echo for a trip to France for the Prix Jacques le Marois in Deauville at the weekend, the latest staging post on the path to a crack at the top American milers at the Breeders’ Cup in the autumn, but has now confirmed the horse’s track career has been cruelly cut short.

In a statement posted on X, the Newmarket trainer said: “It is with great disappointment that we have to announce that Bow Echo has had a setback and will be retired from racing. It has been the honour of a lifetime to train a horse like Bow Echo. He has given us some extraordinary days and taken everyone involved on a journey we will never forget. To retire unbeaten, having won the 2000 Guineas, St James’s Palace Stakes and Sussex Stakes says everything about what an exceptional racehorse he has been.

As heatwaves hit Britain, spare a thought for farm animals

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:57:16 GMT

Claire Bass says animal welfare is a blind spot for the government. Plus, Chris Phillipson on the libraries operating as ‘cool spaces’, Mick Beeby on lido restoration, and Dr Julian McDonough on NHS pressures

Your article rightly highlights the strain heatwaves place on farming (Adapting to global heating: which UK public services are struggling in hot weather?, 5 August). But the government’s thinking on climate adaptation has a troubling blind spot down at the farm: animal welfare.

The Animal Welfare (Sentience) Act 2022 requires ministers to consider animals as sentient beings when developing policy, yet current discourse on heatwaves and farming treats animals almost entirely as economic assets: deaths are framed as “reduced productivity”, rather than as sentient individuals enduring terrible suffering.

During the June heatwaves, French media reported that between 1 and 3 million birds died in a single week as temperatures hit 44C – deaths preceded by hours of panting, heat stress and organ failure. Pigs and poultry can begin suffering from heat above 27C, and when temperatures exceed 35C, British farms and livestock transport systems are pushing against the limits of what conventional cooling systems can contend with.

As the Met Office warns of UK temperatures reaching 45C by 2056, climate adaptation plans for farming must look beyond balance sheets, recognising that what’s at stake is not just the bottom line, but the suffering of millions of sentient beings. Claire BassSenior director, campaigns and public affairs, Humane World for Animals UK

You are right to highlight the importance of libraries operating as “cool spaces” within communities. Such a facility will almost certainly become increasingly important for groups vulnerable to heat-related stress.

Yet, since 2016, over 180 libraries in the UK have been closed or turned over to volunteers, with deprived communities four times more likely to lose their library as compared with richer ones. However, poorer areas are precisely those with least access to green and blue space, tree cover and other sources of protection from extreme heat.

Given the crucial role of libraries in society – with relief from climate change now added to their many other vital functions – a programme of reversal of a decade of cuts is long overdue. Chris Phillipson Emeritus professor of sociology and social gerontology, University of Manchester

To help with its plans to cope with extreme heat (NHS must prepare for annual summer heat pressures, says Yvette Cooper, 5 August), why doesn’t the Labour government implement a nationwide programme of restoring and refurbishing outdoor lidos that have fallen in to disrepair? They might even build some new ones. Mick BeebyBristol

So, we, working in the NHS, must prepare for summer pressures in addition to winter pressures? That’s just pressure, isn’t it? I’m unclear as to what has changed. Dr Julian McDonoughSheffield

Despite the drought, don’t give up on our native plants

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:57:10 GMT

Martin Sullivan and Lorraine Du Feu respond to a recent letter suggesting that we should focus on planting non-native species that can tolerate extreme heat

Richard Eltringham (Letters, 5 August) argues that climate change necessitates switching to a planting palette of drought-tolerant non-native species. It’s not as simple as that. Horticulturists focus on the value of non-native plants for pollinators, but other ecological interactions (for example, food plants for insect larvae) are harder to replace. Native oaks support 2,300 other species; drought-adapted non-natives can’t substitute for all these interactions.

Our native species themselves aren’t tied to a preindustrial climate; there is evidence of rapid microevolutionary changes in oaks in response to recent climate change, so those crisping saplings might be part of selection for drought adaptation. Alternative planting palettes don’t offer a panacea – Mediterranean landscapes are rather brown in summer precisely because of plants’ adaptations to hot and dry conditions, while our dull, wet winters adeptly kill many drought-tolerant species.

Our climate is changing. But a kneejerk shift away from native planting might make things worse. Martin SullivanProfessor of global change ecology, Manchester Metropolitan University

I am puzzled by Richard Eltringham’s suggestion that we should move towards planting non-native species in gardens and parks to cope with increasingly hot and dry summers. Despite current moves towards wildlife-friendly gardening, the majority of plants in these spaces are already exotics, some of which are well adapted for drought, others less so.

Many native plants are also drought-tolerant, and it would be a pity to discourage people from attempts to make their garden more attractive for native insects by using plants that provide for all stages of their lives rather than just adults looking for pollen.

By all means adapt planting to cope with the changing climate, but don’t assume that this means abandoning an admirable trend towards planting native species. Lorraine Du FeuCheltenham, Gloucestershire

Droughts were predicted decades ago – yet water companies did nothing

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:57:06 GMT

Water boards in the south of England should have paid attention to scientific models and built reservoirs, writes Steve Lindsay, while Martin Gray suggests turning salt water into drinking water is the answer

In 1998, I travelled to London for the release of the Meteorological Office’s first forecasts of the impact of climate change on the UK. I was encouraged to see in the audience many representatives from water companies from the south of England. The Met Office modelling showed that “For summer, there is a general tendency for drying in the south of the UK” and “Warming over the UK will be more rapid in the south-east”.

Today, representatives of the southern water boards appear to be surprised that we are experiencing a drought in the south of England (Three-quarters of England in drought as summer’s fifth heatwave looms, 10 August). They should have paid more attention to the science and started the planning and building of reservoirs 28 years ago instead of putting their profits into the pockets of their shareholders. Steve LindsayEmeritus professor of public health entomology, Durham University

How can we be so short of water when, as an island, we are surrounded by the stuff? Solar powered desalination is the answer. Why is it not even being discussed?Martin GrayRadley, Oxfordshire

France’s complex picture of collaborating with and combating Nazis

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:57:01 GMT

Some young French joined the armed Milice, who worked with the occupiers, and other fought in the Resistance, says Michael Rosen. And Anthony Lodge writes on Charles de Gaulle

You editorial (The Guardian view on French cinema’s De Gaulle moment: present anxieties inform a new focus on the past, 5 August) informs us that some new French films covering the second world war are coming soon. Its focus suggested that most of these films will be of a “great men of history” kind. Whatever dilemmas faced leaders in this period, it was the grandparents and great-grandparents of the majority of cinemagoers who faced life-or-death choices and, in some cases, held the lives of their neighbours in their hands.

From my research about members of my family and the areas where they were living, I’ve found local policemen arresting Jewish men and women (like my father’s uncles and aunt) and handing them over to the Nazis. These gendarmes were under the command of local administrators who were “honoured” to do the bidding of Nazi commandants. But then, if you scan the Resistance memorials (often in isolated rural places), you gather that some of the names on those memorials are of Jewish and, in one example I know of, a black member of the Resistance.

Even as the occupiers were facing defeat, Nazi generals were under instructions to massacre partisans, and some young French men joined the armed Milice, who worked with these occupiers to betray the Resistance. Sometimes, immediately after the war, reprisals against the Milice involved summary executions, while the local administrators who did the Nazis’ bidding may have been tried, found guilty and imprisoned but, soon after, walked free under state-ordered amnesties.

This is a complex picture of grassroots, local choices, some being made in support of an illegitimate occupation, part of a Europe-wide genocide and rule by terror, and other choices being made to defy this. Michael RosenLondon

French cinemagoers will be thinking about the threats to French democracy posed by the extreme right, but their interest in Charles de Gaulle runs much deeper. He symbolises resistance to all the forces outside the Hexagone which threaten its autonomy and identity.

To an extent inconceivable in the UK, the French have striven, since the second world war, to maintain freedom from outside interference – in the military sphere (weapons procurement and the nuclear deterrent), in the economic sphere (space exploration, Airbus, automobiles), in the cultural sphere (Paris and Notre Dame).

This urge underlies the desire to maintain and defend the French language, a symbol of internal cohesion within French society and external distinction from the hostile world outside. None of these questions seem to concern people in the UK, but they should. Anthony LodgeCarnbee, Fife

Trump put a wrecking-ball through the East Wing – and through democratic norms

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:00:03 GMT

Our children should be able to visit our nation’s capital without having to think about Donald Trump and about how much he did to deface it

History has shown us how much dictators and aspiring dictators love building monuments to themselves. They enjoy reminding the world that they can do anything, no matter who is responsible for the physical labor or who will be paying the financial and human cost.

They prefer outsized structures, heroic arches, towers and columns designed to prove that their signature and their esthetic will be with us forever, even after they’re dead, though dictators often seem to believe they are too powerful to die.

There’s a chilling photograph of Hitler in the bunker beneath Berlin, not long before his suicide, contemplating an architectural model of the Austrian city of Linz which he had intended (and perhaps still intended) to rebuild to his specifications.

In Tirana, Albania, is a bizarre glass pyramid that the tyrant Enver Hoxha constructed as a museum about his life, and in which he is now buried. In Rome’s EUR, Mussolini and his city planners designed a neighborhood scaled for the race of Fascist giants to which they imagined they belonged.

So it comes as no surprise that Donald Trump has invested so much energy, sacrificed so much popular goodwill, and already spent a fortune on the East Wing ballroom, a project that will occupy an astonishing 90,000 square feet.

I can’t have been the only person who was shocked and saddened by photos of the jagged, gaping hole in the White House’s east facade, ripped apart by a wrecking crew, and by pictures of the historic rose garden remade to resemble an outdoor food court at a mall.

Also unsurprisingly, estimates of the building’s costs have varied widely, though it’s clear that the price tag will be in the hundreds of millions of dollars. ($400m is the most recent figure to have been mentioned.) Though the president has promised that the American tax payer will not be stuck with all or part of the bill, some of the money has already been diverted from publicly funded agencies such as the Secret Service.

And there’s been precious little reporting about who is getting contracts for all this expensive work and about the nature of the contractors’ relationships (financial and personal) with the president.

We were supposed to have been relieved and mollified to learn that the ballroom – a pleasure palace for the president and his most loyal and generous supporters – would occupy a mere 22,000 of those square feet. The remaining 68,000 would provide, as the president wrote in a post, “one big, expensive, and very complex unit, which is vital for National Security and Military Operations!” Beneath the ballroom, a six-story underground bunker, designed to protect our leaders in the event of an attack, will make Hitler’s roomy 3,000 square feet look, by comparison, like a studio apartment. Most recently, Trump discussed using the new building’s roof as a launching pad for killer drones and other lethal weapons.

This clever use of the air space seems to have occurred to the president even after the US court of appeals for the District of Columbia circuit ruled, in a 2-1 decision, that he could not continue renovating the East Wing without congressional approval.

According to Judges Patricia Millet and Bradley Garcia, “each president is a temporary tenant, not the owner, of the White House and its Executive Residence”. Brought by the National Trust for Historic Preservation, the reasoning behind the suit seems obvious: a temporary tenant cannot change a rental cottage into a skyscraper without the landlord’s permission.

The legal requirement to ask permission from Congress has already been ignored by a president who has levied tariffs, attacked Venezuela, gutted government institutions and started a war with Iran – all without bothering to consult the elected senators and representatives.

Now Trump has vowed to take the case before the US supreme court which, as we have seen, is inclined to support him, though there have been a few happy surprises when the majority of chief justices decided to uphold the constitution.

Much of what Trump does seems pointless, illogical, a response to some passing whim. But let’s not forget that he has the instincts of an autocrat, that he is the heir to a playbook, a series of actions designed to attain and consolidate increasing power and control.

This story has always been about more than a building, more than about the intentional desecration of a national symbol, of a handsome structure with a history and a meaning. We’ve seen how Trump feels about history when he accused the Smithsonian Institution of being anti-American for telling the truth about our past.

Though the case was initially brought by Alison Hoagland, an architectural historian and a board member of the National Trust, who claimed that the ballroom was an “aesthetic, cultural and historical” affront, it was never as simple as asking a town board to rule that a neighbor can’t build a view-blocking three-story vertical addition on to his house. We know, as the judges must have known, that the whole thing is about control, about the reckless grab for absolute power and about the necessity to resist it.

It might seem like a distraction – a relatively trivial concern – to worry about a building when our government agents are shooting and kidnapping innocent people on our streets. But it’s part of the same story, a narrative about the subversion – at once reckless and calculated – of our ethical and democratic norms.

Like our response to the ruined reflecting pool and the proposed Trump triumphal arch, our reaction to the proposed ballroom is partly about our wanting our grandchildren and great-grandchildren to be able to visit our nation’s capital without having to think about Donald Trump and about how much he did to deface it.

Francine Prose is a former president of PEN American Center and a member of the American Academy of Arts and Letters and the American Academy of Arts and Sciences

Russia frees ex-US marine four years after sentence disputed by his family

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 15:58:14 GMT

Robert Gilman had been deteriorating rapidly in prison and was said to be in a catatonic state

Russia has freed a former US marine who had been in jail since 2022, after Vladimir Putin granted the ailing American a pardon on humanitarian grounds.

Robert Gilman, 32, an English teacher who briefly served in the US Marines, was travelling by train when he was arrested in the southern Russian city of Voronezh in January 2022 for allegedly kicking a police officer while drunk, according to Russian state media. He was later sentenced to four and a half years in prison, though his family has consistently denied the allegations against him.

His father, Vladimir Gilman, who emigrated to Massachusetts from Russia, wrote in an October 2024 Boston Globe op-ed that his son had fallen ill when the incident occurred and had accidentally kicked the police officer.

Concerns over Gilman’s health had grown sharply in recent weeks, and the White House and the state department had urged Moscow to release him so he could receive medical treatment in the US.

Eric Lebson, chief strategy officer at Global Reach, an advocacy group representing Gilman’s family, told Reuters last week that he had been transferred in late June from a prison hospital to the psychiatric ward of a civilian emergency hospital in a catatonic-like state that doctors assessed as a “dissociative stupor”.

US president Donald Trump claimed he had spoken directly to his Russian counterpart Vladimir Putin about the issue, leading to Gilman’s release.

“I am proud to announce that American citizen Robert Gilman, a former United States Marine, is coming HOME!” Trump wrote on Truth Social.

“After my discussions with President Vladimir Putin, Russia has agreed to release him, very much on a humanitarian basis,” Trump added.

US officials told Reuters that Gilman was already on a state department plane carrying him ‌from Russia to Washington Dulles airport, adding that Gilman would be greeted on arrival by US envoy Steve Witkoff and Sebastian Gorka, a deputy assistant to President Trump.

The official who spoke to Gilman said he was walking and talking.

“All things considered, he seems to be in good ‌shape,” the official said.

Trump stressed on social media that Russia had not sought the release of anyone in return. “No exchange took place,” he wrote.

It is highly unusual for Russia to free a jailed US citizen without securing a prisoner in return.

The decision to release Gilman is therefore likely to be seen as a signal of Moscow’s eagerness to improve relations with the Trump administration, after several weeks in which the US president has appeared to shift towards a more supportive position on Ukraine.

Shortly after Trump returned to office, Washington secured the release of American schoolteacher Marc Fogel from a Russian prison in exchange for a Russian national jailed in the US on money-laundering charges.

Gilman was one of at least 10 Americans known to be imprisoned in ​Russia.

‘Real struggle’ to tame New Forest blaze as UK set for hottest summer on record

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 15:49:18 GMT

Key tourist route closed and residents rallying round as battle against wildfire expected to go on for several days

The battle to contain a large wildfire spreading across the New Forest will go on for several days, experts have said, as wind changes and a desiccated landscape fuel the flames.

Smoke billowed above the deserted A31 road, the main tourist route into the New Forest national park where tens of thousands of families are on holiday, late on Tuesday while a helicopter flew above the blaze. Firefighters at the scene said they were trying to contain several hotspots across the more than 100 hectares (247 acres) of dried heathland as winds swirled over the area.

Roads, including the A31 dual carriageway, will remain shut until at least Thursday, Hampshire’s fire service said, adding that “significant” repairs were needed to the surface, barriers and fencing.

One source at the scene said: “They are really worried that as they dampen down the top, the peat underneath is still alight and spreading the fire. It is too dry, too windy, and it is a real struggle.”

July was the busiest month on record for wildfires, the National Fire Chiefs Council said on Tuesday. Its services have responded to 966 wildfires this year, including 458 in July alone.

Parts of England are bracing for a fifth heatwave of the summer this week as climate breakdown makes extreme heat, drought and wildfires more likely. The Met Office said on Tuesday that the UK was on course for its hottest summer on record by average temperature. On Monday, authorities declared almost three-quarters of England officially in drought.

Hampshire fire and rescue service said it was facing many challenges, including maintaining a water supply and dealing with difficult terrain and changes in wind direction, but it was trying to mount a larger-scale attack on the blaze. Britain owns no dedicated water-bombing aircraft of the kind used by fire crews in Europe.

“Thanks to the efforts of firefighters, we are managing to contain the fire,” it said. “We have been using a mix of fire engines and off-road vehicles to damp down and cool the edges of the area … to prevent spread and escalation. The police drone is being used to enable us to prioritise hotspot areas and manage flare-ups.”

As the emergency services worked through the night, a spokesperson for the Fire Brigades Union said it was calling for the recruitment of 5,000 more firefighters to cope with the extreme climate conditions.

Firefighters have been at the scene since Sunday evening when a lorry caught fire on the A31 at Ringwood at about 5.21pm, sparking the blaze.

Each summer, millions of tourists camp, hike and cycle in the New Forest national park. This year the human-caused climate crisis, which is driving extreme heat, has turned the green English heartland of Hampshire – which has not had rain for 59 days – into a landscape more akin to Arizona in the US.

The A31 dual carriageway, which in August is normally nose to tail with tourist traffic, remained closed on Tuesday with no timetable for when it would reopen. Along the abandoned route, two Shell petrol stations stood empty and closed outside the village of Burley.

A yellow high-pressure water pipe stretched 5 miles along the empty highway from a stream near the village to the blaze, where more than 120 firefighters from stations across the country continued to try to contain the blaze.

Debbie Tann, the chief executive of Hampshire and Isle of Wight Wildlife Trust, said: “It is devastating. It is just dry tinder across the whole forest. It has spread really quickly because of these dry conditions. The New Forest is one of the most important habitats in Europe. It has so many rare species in the heathland – reptiles, adders, lizards, and then the birds like nightjars. This is going to have a devastating impact.”

Sue Oatway, who lives in Burley, said people had come together to support the firefighters, many of whom are volunteers, collecting food and drink for them. On a community WhatsApp group, firefighters thanked the “amazing community” for the support.

Outside Oatway’s home, New Forest ponies sheltered from the heat under brown, dried-out trees amid withered bracken. Oatway said: “I have never seen it so dry here. Someone posted a world map today on a social media group. I knew about the wildfires in Canada and Europe, but the scale of them in the United States I didn’t know. They are everywhere, and now here on our doorstep.”

With the A31 closed, tourist traffic was forced on to back roads through the forest, causing jams on narrow lanes. Holidaymakers were continuing to try to enjoy their break but as the battle to contain the wildfire entered its fourth day, the reality of the threat was becoming clearer.

Jason Bunsell had been camping with a friend at Ashurst on Sunday night when the fire began. “We were coming back on our bikes and saw this big thick black cloud above, we thought it was a storm,” he said. “Then we got back to our campsite and my wife sent a message to me saying: ‘How far are you from the fires, are you all right?’”

Ian Buckman, a district lead volunteer for Arundel and Littlehampton Scouts, who was guiding 26 scouts and explorers on a cycle ride through the forest outside Burley, said: “I have never seen the forest so dry. The environmental impact is massive.”

Tourism is the largest single industry in the local economy. Within the national park boundaries, leisure and recreation trips account for £107.6m in income each year. But the threat of fire spreading and the impact of air pollution have prompted appeals for tourists to stay away.

Forestry England, which manages about half of the national park, said the operation to contain the fire could last for several days. Its spokesperson Esta Mion said: “We are very much asking people to stay away … it’s unpredictable. We just don’t know how long this incident could go on for. We’re certainly thinking it could be the next few days, for sure.”

Emma Howard Boyd, the chair of the independent National Heat Risk Commission and a professor in practice at the Grantham Research Institute on Climate Change and the Environment, said: “As the prime minister tours every region of the UK, it will be hard to miss the impacts of this extraordinary summer: wildfires, drought, parched farmland and people struggling to work in extreme heat. The government must now act with urgency to prepare the country for a hotter climate, cutting emissions while investing in the resilience of our homes, infrastructure, farms and public services.”

‘There is no age-limit on love!’: Lesley Manville and Peter Mullan on their heart-melting TV romance

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 15:07:57 GMT

Ten years later, the actors are stopped in the street to talk about their chemistry in the fantastic BBC sitcom Mum. The pair open up about joy, naughtiness and Tinder swiping

More from our series Making Love: ‘We met and two minutes later we were kissing’ – how Gavin and Stacey became Britain’s most bang tidy TV couple

Ten years ago, Stefan Golaszewski introduced us to 59-year-old Cathy in his sitcom Mum. It was the day of the funeral of her husband, Dave, in the chaos of her suburban semi-detached before setting off. Her dopey son, Jason, was there with his goofy new girlfriend, Kelly, as was Cathy’s pushover brother, Derek, and his snobby partner, Pauline. Dave’s parents were picking at nibbles, too, making borderline problematic comments. And there was a quiet, kind man, Michael, trying to reach Cathy in this mayhem to give her a bouquet of flowers.

Michael was an old friend of Cathy and Dave. Over the next three seasons, he slowly revealed his true feelings to Cathy. It started with a little hand grab. Then came the admission that, after Michael’s mum died, the last thing he talked to her about was, “You.” Garden centre trips followed (“We’ve been to three and you still haven’t bought any chairs!”) as did Cathy’s doubts (“What if I just miss Dave?”). Then, one day, she gave in to her feelings: “It sort of crept up on me.” After a final declaration of love in the rain, the pair walked off into the sunset with a bag of crisps and bottle of bubbly.

Fresh from his first hit relationship sitcom, Him & Her, only Golaszewski could make the line “Thanks for helping me scrape shit off my drive” sound romantic. Peter Mullan as Michael was the master of longing, while Lesley Manville captured Cathy as a woman at long last putting her own happiness first. The show picked up multiple Bafta nominations, and won the hearts of viewers who rooted for this quieter love story between two ordinary people in their 60s. After all, how often does TV celebrate that?

“Lesley, are you hiding?” Mullan says on an early morning video call, as we meet to mark this decade anniversary.

“I was on stage last night and was on until very late, I’ve only just got up!” Manville says. “I was told it was audio only, so I’m still in my jim-jams.”

It’s true that there has been some miscommunication, but the one blank square, somehow, stirs the chemistry.

“You’re the sex siren of the West End!” he tells her. Laughter erupts.

“You wouldn’t think that if you saw me now,” she insists, later adding: “Just look at the poster for Les Liaisons Dangereuses. That’s how I want you to remember me.”

LM: We’d met at Dinard film festival years before and then I guess we next met at the read-through for Mum? We’d just said “hello, nice to meet you” blah blah, then the next thing you know we’re on a round table with all these amazing people.

[I ask how they feel about being Golaszewski’s first choices.]

LM: Well, it would have been terrible if you’d started this interview by asking: “So, Lesley, I hear you were the 15th choice for this.”

LM: You’ve got these three people and they’re so close and one of them dies, and the other two are still mourning the loss of this person. Cathy and Michael’s friendship is as strong as it always was. What a great basis for a romance! It’s an amazing friendship; it’s crucial. So, I can kind of understand that. Can you, Peter?

PM: Yeah! I’ve got friends to whom that has happened in real life.

LM: Oh really?

PM: Yeah, there were three – and very, very close – then one died and the other two got together.

LM: Wow.

PM: That’s since Mum … but not because of Mum!

LM: Jason kind of frowns on it, partly because he doesn’t want his mum to be with someone other than his dad. But also because there’s a kind of stigma: “You can’t be with dad’s best friend.” But he had to get over that.

PM: For me, it was the declaration of love in the rain.

LM: Oh God, Peter, yes!

PM: Yeah, that was quite special. Not least because it’s the only time Stefan didn’t pull me up on the fucking lines.

LM: He is a stickler for the lines being absolutely spot on … I’ve just remembered what you said, Peter: “You melt me like butter!”

PM: It was the only time Michael had ever brought all of this forth. He’d held all of that in. So from an actor’s point of view it was great to finally allow that.

LM: There’s also the little lovely moment with the fireworks and you just see their fingers connecting. I think that’s the first time there’s ever any acknowledgment that there’s something going on between them that’s quietly gone beyond friendship. Hands touching is a gorgeous thing. It reminded me of The Age of Innocence: the scene where Daniel Day-Lewis takes Michelle Pfeiffer’s glove off really slowly and it’s the most erotic thing.

LM: I think it ended pretty perfectly because it was a little anarchic sweet moment, kind of childlike, of them just prancing off into the woods with a bottle of champagne and leaving the whole bonkers family behind. I wouldn’t have changed a thing!

PM: I agree completely.

LM: It was joy and naughtiness. People tend to equate middle-aged people with not being naughty any more. But these two are and it’s just delightful.

PM: I was really surprised. I wasn’t expecting the kind of reactions we got. I knew it was a really lovely show and beautifully written and acted. But I was very taken by people [asking] “Will they/won’t they?”, which was really sweet. I’ve never been in anything else like that!

LM: From my experience of strangers talking to me about it, they were really pleased to see a love story about people who weren’t in their 20s or 30s.

PM: Oh totally!

LM: People at that age can fall in love, Jesus Christ!

PM: What was interesting, too, was that young [people] weren’t disgusted by it. As many youngsters came up to me and talked about it as did people of our vintage. Which was really interesting because, quite rightly, when you’re young, Jesus, who wants to see two old fogies get together? I mean, remember, when you’re young, 40 is ancient!

LM: It’s the thing that people stop me to talk about the most. Just this week, someone stopped me and said: “Oh I love Mum, it was my favourite!” And they’re always asking, “Are you gonna do any more?”

PM: I’m the same, I get it at least every couple of weeks.

LM: And 10 years on! How many shows can you say that about from 10 years ago?

PM: Absolutely.

LM: We’re bigging ourselves up here, aren’t we? We’re marvellous!

LM: Oh, that’s a bit personal. You can start this one, Peter.

PM: I’m a romantic at heart, yes.

LM: Yeah, me too.

PM: We all are. I don’t believe there’s a human being on earth who’s not a romantic. Whether they show it or not is a different issue, but we all share that gene.

LM: I mean, who wouldn’t want romance? Who wouldn’t want somebody sharing, receiving, giving … Who wouldn’t want that in this messed-up world we’re living in? Keep it simple, just a little bit of good, honest, basic to and fro, love, romance.

PM: More!

LM: Yes, more of it please. I’m not on Tinder, in case anyone is wondering.

PM: At least not at this time in the morning …

LM: Not in my PJs, I’m not! I wouldn’t think Tinder is very romantic anyway. Scroll right, scroll right, scroll right. No that’s not my idea of romance.

LM: When we finished, Lisa McGrillis, who played Kelly, had somebody make us these little dolls of our characters. I’ve got a little Cathy doll with a striped T-shirt, which Cathy wore quite a lot. I certainly didn’t steal any of Cathy’s costumes. [Manville previously said Cathy’s “terrible jeans” made her bum “look as if it goes on for ever”.]

Did you steal any of Michael’s costumes, Peter?

PM: No, and I didn’t steal any of Cathy’s either. But I’ve got a cup and we’re all in a selfie on it. I do have the utterly bizarre little model: mine looks like Beetlejuice.

But in all seriousness, most actors wouldn’t take props because they would jump on you. You can get arrested for it. I can say this now, I guess: I nicked a nose and a pair of ears from Lord of the Rings. I’ll not say who gave them to me but they were terrified.

LM: The nose police are going to be after you now.

PM: Quickly followed by the acting police.

LM: We do lots of work and people come and go in our lives but you can’t put them all in your phonebook. But Mum was utterly unique. The core of it was the eight of us. We have a WhatsApp group and it’s still going. We were all close.

PM: Baby, I’m with you 1,000%. It’s the only show I’ve ever done where we’ve all kept in touch after. It’s very rare. And in my case, it’s the only one where [we] still keep in touch with that little band of brothers and sisters. It’s beautiful.

LM: And sometimes if we haven’t heard from somebody for a while, somebody will check in and just say: “Has anybody heard from whoever?”

PM: It comes in all shapes and sizes and we should have more of it.

LM: Yeah. And for Cathy, at the most awful time of her life, you could never have imagined that this little acorn of love was brewing right there in front of her.

There’s this amazing bit in series one, when – oh God yes, Peter it was amazing what you did – Cathy’s ordering some takeaway for them both and Michael’s just watching her and that’s all he needs to do. It’s just sublime. Cathy is dealing with this grief and Michael is just allowing her to deal with it. You see the love is already there for him and it’s been there for years. The scene of him just watching her open the cupboard looking for a menu … it’s gorgeous.

It’s a beautiful, slow-burning love story. I guess the big thing is, there is no age limit on falling in love.

I thought having a second baby would be easier – but, from food to teeth to poos, I’ve forgotten so much

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 15:01:02 GMT

My mum has been a huge help with the practical stuff. She’s also stirred up memories of my own blissfully bored childhood

I gave birth to a second child a few months ago. I don’t think I will ever get over the fact that this is the way people come into the world. It’s a ridiculous endeavour, from start to finish.

My first pregnancy was almost comically miserable for the first 20 weeks, then glorious for 10 weeks, then scary and stressful for the remainder. And this time? More intense in every way, but with added toddler. Which is to say: no breaks, suck it up, keep on moving.

The soothing bedtime story I told myself whenever pregnancy insomnia let me sleep was: “At least you know what you’re doing this time.” It’s what I told myself as I made a spartan list of things people could buy for the baby, which I then mostly bought myself. It’s what I murmured under my breath as I counted and washed the previous baby’s hand-me-downs. When pregnancy-induced carpal tunnel syndrome showed up and the crib still hadn’t been assembled and the new pushchair hadn’t arrived, I comforted myself with deep breathing. Inhale – atleastyouknowwhatyou’redoing – and exhale – thistime.

And you know what? I did! The new baby bore an uncanny resemblance to the old baby, which seemed to unlock something. That loud hum of postpartum inexperience and anxiety from the first time around was quieted. I knew that babies don’t expire from crying while you eat, or have a quick shower, or run downstairs to answer the door. I knew that the postnatal stink would dissipate and that the night sweats would stop. I anticipated the steep learning curve, for me and the baby, of breastfeeding (God bless silver nursing cups).

But alongside this sublime, bone-deep knowledge has been the often panic-provoking realisation that I have forgotten everything I once swore would be indelible. My Google searches shamed me with how basic they were: what is the right temperature for a baby’s bath? How long can a breastfed baby safely go without doing a poo? Really? I no longer recall the order of teeth appearing, nor first solid foods. What were that baby’s first words? I simply couldn’t tell you.

All that information has dribbled out of my head over the years. It turns out your brain holds on only to the pertinent information and the most important version of your kid is the one in front of you. The one with teeth – not previous years’ sweet gummy model.

My mum has been helping out a lot these past few months and I’ve found myself thinking about the nature of memory. She remembers the most urgent task of all: she still has the knack for soothing a wailing baby. We talk a lot in the wee hours, often about my childhood. Her reminiscences are forcing me to remember, too.

In my mind, my youth was halcyon – long summer holidays of adventures with cousins, travel and movie marathons. But a little reflection offers a new perspective: I was so bored, so often! There were stretches of nothingness: waking, eating, napping, reading the same book for the seventh time, eating again and going to bed. For weeks.

I was 90s free range and my parents weren’t doing anything unique. But it brings up something I thought with my first and still think about. What kind of childhood do I want for my kids?

Boredom was ultimately a positive and formative force in my life – it allowed me to seek what I found truly interesting. I discovered that I liked writing, that I had no painting talent, that I could sing pretty high. I learned how to sit inside my head without wallowing. I think it has made me resilient. My middle-class kids are growing up in the age of scheduled enrichment: parents hoping to imbue tiny minds with sparkly bits of culture, the stuff they will reach for when they are trying to make the world better one day. Being idle feels akin to brain rot and severe parental deficiency.

Part of the reason I didn’t feel neglected as a child is that everybody was bored. What my parents and their working-class peers were doing out of necessity feels to me, now middle-class, like radical counterculture. And yet I think I will persist. Boredom is a gift. And maybe, like me, they will remember only the highlights when they’re grown. And if they don’t – hey, there’s always therapy.

Bim Adewunmi is a freelance journalist

Copernicus! Tony Soprano! The inventor of the semicolon! The heroic obsessions of artist Isabel Nolan

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 14:27:05 GMT

Ireland’s artist at the Venice Biennale steals religious imagery for her secular celebrations of scientists and humanists, including an Italian punctuation master – and even TV’s famous mobster

It’s a long way to the Irish pavilion at the Venice Biennale. First, you pass through the biennale’s colossal central exhibition, housed in a sequence of cavernous medieval warehouses. After that, the route takes in a suite of national pavilions (the Philippines, Latvia, Timor-Leste, Morocco, Malta, Oman). By the time art-lovers reach Ireland’s presentation, they are frequently exhausted, if not delirious.

This year, Dublin-based artist Isabel Nolan has transformed the pavilion into a space of respite, both physical and intellectual – a secular chapel titled Dreamshook. Visitors emerge from darkness into a pale-yellow chamber flooded with daylight and face a radiant tapestry arched like a stained-glass window in a gothic cathedral. A cluster of archangel’s wings bearing books hovers before the vast orb of the sun above a fish-filled sea. Nolan works across sculpture, textile, painting, drawing and text, and Dreamshook is accordingly rich in colour and pictorial detail, including bent metal sculptures, tapestries and drawings. For the footsore and weary – ie everyone – she has even designed benches. Glory be.

“I think the history of Christianity has proven beyond doubt that they’re really good at weaponising aesthetics,” says Nolan, conspiratorially. She is frequently inspired to steal ecclesiastical flourishes in her exhibition-making, from archways to coloured light and iconography. “To use that kind of language to largely secular ends feels satisfying.”

Underpinning Dreamshook is the story of Aldo Manuzio, a humanist and printer working in Venice at the turn of the 16th century. One tapestry shows Manuzio asleep in his canopied bed, a little dog curled by his side, being visited by an angel in his dreams as the air teems with winged books and manuscripts. Manuzio had a vision in which books – until then, expensive and rarefied objects – might be portable and affordable. According to this vision, knowledge was no longer the purview of the church and noble families. In acting on it, Manuzio allowed the great texts of pre-Christian Europe to become common cultural currency.

Nolan tells me that Manuzio is credited with inventing italics, blank margins, the semicolon and the precursor to the paperback. “Of course, he was a Christian, but he was also a committed humanist, and he dedicated himself largely to publishing works of classical literature that for centuries had only survived in Arabic. He printed more first editions of classical Greek and Roman works than any other printer in history.” For Nolan, Manuzio is an emblem of thoughtfulness and scholarship in a turbulent era: Europe had been ravaged by famine and the Black Death, the papacy was in crisis and life was violent and unsettled. Even though he was a man of faith, Manuzio maintained his passion for disseminating secular knowledge.

Sign up to Art Weekly

Your weekly art world round-up, sketching out all the biggest stories, scandals and exhibitions

after newsletter promotion

Some Fundamental Forces, Nolan’s retrospective, is about to open at Southwark Park Galleries in London, bringing together works from the past 20 years. Aptly, part of the show occupies a former church space (the atmospheric Dilston Grove, now the Dilston Gallery). Like Dreamshook, the exhibition is a celebration of transformative ideas, including tributes to Copernicus, the philosopher Simone Weil and New Jersey’s Barthes of the Beretta, Tony Soprano.

As in Dreamshook, Nolan’s instinct is to celebrate scientists and innovators as fellow humans rather than venerating them as geniuses. One painting is titled, excitably, Copernicus! (Nolan admits to a weakness for exclamation marks). A drooping flag carries a beatific funerary portrait of the poet John Donne. In an accompanying text, Nolan writes of her fascination with the monument this portrait is based on in St Paul’s Cathedral in London, and the statue’s curiously wobbly knees. “It is,” she writes, “the conspicuousness of his knocked knees, the beguiling authenticity of that slight bend, that makes John Donne and his strange performance real to me.”

Visually, it’s a show full of objects that have attempted pompous swagger, but with only limited success. “It’s very hard to take grandeur seriously, largely because it’s been so weaponised to communicate powerful ideologies,” says Nolan. “Grandeur is attractive. It’s exciting. It’s rousing, but ultimately it’s often very hollow. There are works in the show that I think of as fallen and failed chandeliers, and flags that are bent and scrape the floor.”

As an appropriate spirit animal for all this bathos, Nolan turns to the donkey: a creature of contradictory associations, at once a lowly beast and an emblem of Christ. In the drawing Sisyphus, a donkey appears as a tiny, isolated figure – a bearer of impossible burdens. Donkeys also help slice through her own temptations toward grandeur. She has created magnificent tapestries, including one featuring the oldest surviving figure sculpture – a diminutive carving known as The Lion Man – and another of a turbulent and mysterious skyscape. A wall-sized photograph of two donkeys in a graveyard look over her work like guardian spirits. As ever, her instinct is to bring people down to earth, herself included.

Isabel Nolan: Some Fundamental Forces is at Southwark Park Galleries, London, from 15 August to 1 November. The Venice Biennale runs until 22 November

Hard work or humour? Take the British values quiz

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 13:00:01 GMT

Poll finds consensus on what makes people proud to be British but deep divisions on issues such as immigration

You may think it’s a commitment to buying a round, the ability to chat at length about the weather, or making a proper cup of tea. But when it comes down to what makes people proud to be British, there is a consensus across all ages, demographics and political groups – and tea, pints and the weather don’t get a look-in.

Hard work, fair play and community are the values that voters – no matter which political party, age group, social class or region they come from – instinctively believe capture what it means to be British, according to a large-scale study of British values by Public First.

“When you talk to people across Britain it’s clear they have a shared set of values – if fairness is Britain’s moral compass, hard work is its character and community the thing that binds us,” said Olivia Walsham, the director of social policy at the policy and polling research firm.

But the research found that while most voters could agree on this core set of values, they were deeply pessimistic about finding common ground: more than 65% of respondents felt that in a post Brexit-era, Britain is more divided than ever.

As Andy Burnham embarks on a summer “listening” tour of the UK and vows to “bring back hope”, the report suggests he has his work cut out, with fewer than one in three people feeling hopeful about the UK’s future, and seven in 10 feeling that things have become worse in the last five years.

But three-quarters of the public believed British people shared a common set of values – ranging from 70% of Reform UK voters to 83% of Labour-leaning voters.

In the study, which polled 6,000 adults and spoke to 300 people in focus groups, participants were asked to choose which values they thought were the most important – forcing them to make trade-offs.

“Hard-working” topped the list of qualities the public associated with British people, with 70% describing hard work as a British value. “Community” was also key, with 80% of people agreeing Britain was at its best when neighbours looked out for one another. Contributing to society boosted positive views: 62% of those who had volunteered in the last month said they were optimistic about their local area’s future, compared with 25% of those who had never volunteered.

Unsurprisingly for a population that can’t pass a queue without joining it, the notion of “fairness” or waiting one’s turn was the clearest shared value. Asked for examples of what described British life at its best, citizens referenced queueing, the NHS, fair play, taking turns, treating people equally, and the expectation that everyone should play by the rules.

The report also exposes deep-seated division, especially on immigration. Half of respondents said immigration had had a negative impact on the UK, while only one-fifth said a diversity of people and cultures made them proud to live in the UK.

Sunder Katwala, the director of British Future, said the report showed that British people had become not only more divided, but more “discomfited by how divided we feel”.

Sign up to First Edition

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

after newsletter promotion

In a political landscape in which no political party was polling at higher than 25%, politicians were more likely to appeal to their base using divisive language, he added.

“Politics should be about articulating how people feel about the world, but also the peaceful resolution of differences,” Katwala said. “There are some glimmers of hope in this research, people are looking for a unifying sense of what we share, but actually appealing across the board to those people, it’s quite challenging.”

Burnham’s advisers may take heart in the report’s findings that messaging around shared values had been better received when framed positively, rather than through grievances, with “messages that talked about British values in affirmative terms consistently outperforming those that were rooted in a sense of loss”, according to the report.

“The values that unite us haven’t disappeared but no one is speaking to them,” said Walsham. “Politicians should start by recognising people’s everyday values – fairness, hard work and community. Then they might fan the flames of a hopeful, shared patriotism that the country so desperately needs.”

Trevor Pears, the chair of the Pears Foundation, which funded the report, said people were crying out for more initiatives like the annual Big Lunch and Big Help Out projects. “We need many more opportunities for people to come together, help one another and strengthen the communities they’re part of,” he said.
