‘I feel motherly towards them’: the nature reserve manager sharing her office with a wasp nest

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 12:00:01 GMT

Eilish Rothney’s first instinct was to get rid of the wasps entering her converted shipping container in Norfolk. Now she enjoys their constant hum

When wasps began streaming through a rusted gap in her office door one June morning, Eilish Rothney’s first instinct was the same most people would have: get rid of them. The reserve manager for the Trinity Broads, part of the Norfolk Wildlife Trust, had discovered a wasp colony taking up residence in her office, a converted shipping container.

“They just built the nest inside my door,” she says. “My first thought was: ‘Oh no, I have to get rid of that now.’ But then I realised: ‘Wait, why? Why do I have to do that?’”

Two months on, Rothney is in a co-working arrangement with the nest, which is now more than a metre tall and swallows an entire bookshelf of ring binders. It has papered the inside of her office in what she calls “lovely layers of colours”, with thousands of chewed wood pulp cells stacked like the world’s strangest filing system.

Rather than evict her new colleagues, Rothney adapted. She opened the window to give the wasps a clear flight path. “I just stroll past like I’m a browsing cow, calmly going about my day,” she says. “The wasps were fine with me being there. The longer I was there, the more I felt relaxed being alongside them.”

The queen lays eggs continuously, while worker wasps forage and bring food back to feed the larvae. That, in turn, exudes a sugary liquid that the adult workers drink. Rothney describes a low purring sound rising off the nest, which she believes comes from thousands of wings beating to keep the colony cool. “It’s almost like the colony in itself is a living organism,” she says.

She says she isn’t worried about being stung – “it wouldn’t be a whole lot worse than mosquito bites” – and her advice for anyone less enthusiastic than her is: “Insects learn that certain individuals won’t harm them. Just keep calm, they will sense that and won’t feel threatened.”

Rothney traces her convictions about wildlife back to an ecology field trip at university, and to what she describes as “a kind of poetry and beauty in understanding the intricate relationships between everything”. She says she has become increasingly frustrated by how easily the label “pest” becomes a green light for extermination. “It’s fair enough if people don’t like wasps,” she says, “but we need to move away from things just being killed out of convenience. We cause a lot of damage with that approach.”

She is quick to defend the wasps which interfere with picnics every August, too. By late summer, the larvae stop producing their sugary secretion, leaving the adult workers suddenly cut off from their main food source, she says. “The poor things are starving. It’s not a battle between them and me. They’re desperate. Let’s provide them with that sugar and maybe draw them away from someone’s real ale in the evening.

“All I want is for people to be more tolerant of wasps, and of nature generally. I’m proud to be working where I am, and it’s our 100th anniversary this year – so maybe the wasps are celebrating too!”

As the weather turns this autumn, Rothney says, the queen will stop laying, the males and worker wasps will die off, and next year’s queens will leave to hibernate alone. By winter, the nest will be empty.

“It’ll be sad when they leave. I do feel motherly towards them,” she says. “It’ll be odd when they’re gone, because even the noise itself is amazing.”

And when the nest is empty? “I’m going to try to take it apart very carefully when they leave so I can see what it looks like inside.”

Until then, the shelves belong to the wasps.

‘Living fossil’: the blue-blood arthropod that has changed little in 445m years

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 11:00:02 GMT

Tenacious and confounding, the horseshoe crab pre-dates dinosaurs and has copper-based blood vital to vaccine safety

They pre-date dinosaurs, eat food using their legs and contain blue blood that we use to keep us healthy. Horseshoe crabs are the supreme, if offbeat, survivors of the invertebrate world.

Often called “living fossils” for visually obvious reasons – horseshoe crabs resemble a sort of prehistoric helmet (their Japanese name translates as “helmet crab”) – these animals have barely changed appearance along a lineage that stretches back 445m years.

But even for a species that looks like it is stuck in a sort of Jurassic period costume party, horseshoe crabs still have the ability to confound people. For starters, they are not even crabs (they are arthropods but are more closely related to scorpions than crustaceans).

They have nine eyes scattered about their bodies and six pairs of legs, but only five of them are for walking – the last pair are used to crush up food such as clams and worms and shovel them into their mouths. They have a sharp, deadly looking tail called a telson which is, in fact, completely harmless and only used to flip them the right way up if they are turned over.

When situated dome-up, horseshoe crabs have a formidable defence against predators because of their shells. Draw their blood, though, and you may be surprised to see it is blue, not red.

This copper-based blood provides an incredible link in time between the era before stegosauruses and your routine vaccination appointment at the doctor or pharmacy, as it is used by the biomedical industry to help determine if there are any harmful toxins in vaccines.

Horseshoe crabs are found in North America along shorelines from Maine to Mexico, while a related species, the mangrove horseshoe crab (Carcinoscorpius rotundicauda, above), is found in India, Bangladesh and south-east Asia.

The animals use beaches as a sort of nursery, much like sea turtles. Female horseshoe crabs will deposit about 4,000 eggs in clusters in the sand, usually at high tide and during a full moon. Many of these eggs become essential food for shorebirds.

While the species has survived several mass extinctions so far, the horseshoe crab now face a novel challenge due to the impact of humanity. Sea level rises and coastal development are squeezing out suitable habitat for the creatures, leading to some concerns over their long-term future.

“There’s a worry that rising sea levels are eroding sandy beach habitat, and once that spawning area on the beach is gone then it’s gone,” said Prof Mark Botton, who has studied horseshoe crabs for nearly 50 years.

Sign up to Down to Earth

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

after newsletter promotion

Botton, whose interest in these creatures as a young man was initially piqued by witnessing a documentary crew from David Attenborough’s BBC Life on Earth series filming horseshoe crabs in the Delaware Bay, has found, though, that the species is unusually hardy.

As well as having an internal cocktail of chemicals that helps them survive hostile conditions, horseshoe crabs are adept at eking out a place to survive even if the odds are against them. You do not get to hang around for 445m years without a certain tenacity, after all.

“They are very good at finding small patches of sandy habitat even if a coastline has been almost completely concreted over,” said Botton, an ecologist at Fordham University. “Don’t expect them to go extinct any time soon. We shouldn’t sell them short or write them off just yet.”

Don’t despair about what’s happening to our planet. That’s what they want you to feel

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 11:00:00 GMT

There are social tipping points when the seemingly impossible suddenly becomes inevitable. Good journalism plays an essential role in helping drive these shifts and shows us how real change can happen

Climate campaign 2026: help us reach 20,000 acts of support

There are many reasons to despair: our green and pleasant land is parched and brown, reservoirs are depleted, and wildfires are racking the UK, Europe and the US. But there are many more reasons not to despair: despair is self-fulfilling; despair is disempowering; despair is what the bastards want you to feel. But above all, despair is irrational. Let me explain why.

However sophisticated our forecasting tools may be, we simply have no means of knowing what comes next. Like the climate, like every ecosystem, society is a complex system. Its properties and adaptive responses cannot be predicted by studying its components in isolation, regardless of the brilliance of social scientists and futurologists. Things happen - unexpected things - sometimes at astonishing speed. In other words, society has tipping points. It can quickly flip from one equilibrium state to another.

Take Ireland, to give just one example. Until a few decades ago, the Republic of Ireland was a Catholic theocracy. It had been that way since the 1920s. If anything, for most of the 20th century, the Catholic church seemed to tighten its grip. Breaking that grip was almost unimaginable. But then a number of events combined in unexpected ways. A series of appalling scandals came to light: systematic sexual violence, the kidnapping of women and children, slave labour and mass mortality in church institutions. Travel and mass media allowed Irish people to see what social and sexual liberation looked like in other countries. The media and international forums allowed brave activists, whose voices had long been suppressed, to be heard more widely. The walls fell faster than anyone could have guessed. Before long, equal marriage and the right to abortion were both legally sanctioned: a shift that had been almost unimaginable.

The same can be said for the collapse of state communism, and the end of just about every empire and monarchy that once looked unassailable. The fossil fuel empire is not exempt.

The complex system we call society has two equilibrium states: impossible and inevitable. The change we want always looks impossible. Votes for women? You must be off your rocker. Independence for India? Britain will never let it go. Civil rights? Ridiculous! But with sustained pressure, a bit of luck, a change in circumstance, bang!, the impossible happens. Then everyone says, “Well, that was inevitable, wasn’t it?”

I think we could be approaching a tipping point on climate breakdown. For years we’ve been told, “You’re asking for the moon. Fossil fuels are here to stay.” Part of me has believed it. But now, as the Guardian’s reporting has shown, suddenly fossil fuels are far more expensive than renewables. Suddenly it is green technologies that are generating vast employment and income. Suddenly, technological barriers that looked insuperable have come tumbling down. Suddenly, fossil-funded politicians look outmoded, ridiculous, corrupt. Suddenly, we are waking up to the need for urgent action, as heat shocks hammer the message home.

Climate campaign 2026: help us reach 20,000 acts of support

The trouble with a tipping point, whether positive or negative, is that you never know in advance where it might be. You can see it only in retrospect. If we have already caused so much global heating that the Atlantic current system is destined to collapse, or cascading failure of the Amazon rainforest is locked in, we don’t yet know. But similarly, if the conditions required for a major social shift have already lined up, we won’t know until the system flips.

This kind of rapid change requires a public informed by independent media sources, journalists in hock to the facts rather than corporate interests. The Guardian’s open, reader-funded model is a clear antidote to other outlets – both the billionaire media and public service broadcasters - who are actively shirking this challenge, cutting their climate coverage at a time when it has never been more critical.

So, at the Guardian and beyond, we keep pushing, keep exposing, keep demonstrating how change might happen. And, for wholly rational reasons, keep hoping. Please consider supporting our reporting today.

The greatest question we confront, possibly the greatest question humanity has ever confronted, is whether we can hit the social tipping points before we hit the environmental tipping points.

This year’s Guardian climate support campaign is highlighting our unrivalled coverage of the climate emergency as well as our reporting on the ideas and initiatives that can help us tackle it. Please consider supporting it today.

Hotel prices in Europe jump before total solar eclipse

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 10:50:12 GMT

Rooms in Icelandic capital Reykjavík cost double usual rate and Spain also enjoying strong bookings

Europe’s total solar eclipse: the best place to see it

Hotel prices have surged in key European cities as hundreds of thousands of tourists flock to see the first total eclipse visible from the continent this century.

Iceland’s capital, Reykjavík, has seen one of the biggest jumps in hotel room prices, being one of the few accessible locations to see the full eclipse before evening on Wednesday.

Tourists there have been shelling out $793 (£587) on average to secure a room on Wednesday, a 98% jump compared with the same day last year, according to data from the travel and hospitality data group Lighthouse Intelligence.

Spain is also steeling itself for an influx of tourists, as one of the best places on the European continent to view the total solar eclipse. The finance ministry said on Monday that it expected 460,000 overseas tourists to visit specifically to see the eclipse.

The rate for a room in A Coruña in the north of Spain was up 135% compared with last year to $451 on average, while prices in Mallorca, where the eclipse is expected to be visible on the horizon at sunset, were up by 24% at about $448. In Santiago de Compostela and Bilbao, hotel room prices have risen 34% and 67% respectively.

Accommodation in small towns and villages in the eclipse’s path, as well as makeshift observatories set up by local enthusiasts, have been booked for months. There have been reports of US travellers taking part in house exchanges with people living in Spain’s Castilla y León, which is one of the best regions to view the eclipse.

However, authorities in Spain are worried about an increased risk of fires, with police expecting 1.5m more car journeys than usual on Wednesday. They have warned that extraordinarily dry temperatures mean the smallest spark could lead to devastating forest fires, while traffic could end up blocking access for emergency vehicles if fires break out in rural areas.

Authorities will undertake out a massive police operation and safety campaign, with plans to deploy 35,000 officers to help minimise risks. Virginia Barcones, the secretary general for civil protection and emergencies at Spain’s interior ministry, said: “We’ve had an extremely tough summer in Spain, with yet another series of major forest fires. That’s why we must restrict any kind of activity that could generate a spark.”

Parking on hard shoulders or close to dry vegetation has been banned in Spain, as have barbecues and fireworks. The València region has closed its natural parks and banned walking on rural footpaths.

after newsletter promotion

As of Monday evening, three large wildfires were burning in the provinces of Huelva, Segovia and Huesca. Fires have already scorched about 200,000 hectares this year – six times more than by the same date in 2025, according to the environment ministry.

For those unable to make the journey, the US space agency Nasa has installed four high-powered telescopes in the small village of Villalibado in north-west Spain in order to livestream the eclipse to more than 10 million people.

A partial solar eclipse will be visible across the UK on Wednesday, where it will reach its maximum coverage at 7.12pm in London.

Good luck if you’re waiting for A-level results. Just don’t ask me what to do next

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 10:00:57 GMT

I’m all in favour of university – but I don’t have to pay the enormous fees

In one of the most poignant vox pops I ever did, I wasn’t even looking for Neets, AKA young people who aren’t in education, employment or training. I was just looking for people on the streets near Liverpool with enough time to speak to me. I ran into a young man who had dropped out of university, and he explained why: he’d been doing a business studies degree, and had realised during his second term that a degree didn’t make sound business sense. Everything he was learning, he could have found in a book, and the nine grand of annual fees was only a fraction of the total debt he was incurring. He went on to tell me quite a lot about upfront cost and hidden cost, and ratios and leverage, but the bottom line was that it didn’t represent value for money.

So he left, but that gave him three problems: first, he still had a load of debt, just nothing to show for it; second, he had nothing to do; third, a lot of the value of a degree cannot be counted – you only begin to comprehend it by its absence.

As A-level results loom this week, I constantly have my beak in teenagers’ business. Friends of my kids, work-experience students I’ve known and loved: I can’t stop myself asking them all the standard questions – what university they want to go to, what their offer is, what their predictions are, how their exams went. The emotional rhythms are irresistible: you strive for a thing, you get close to the thing, conditionally, you strive harder and you hopefully hit the jackpot. Even if I were to engage my critical brain, and think: “How much additional earning potential will an English and history degree from Exeter deliver?”, I don’t believe in that as a metric. I’m not willing to surrender to a normal in which the only point of reading and thinking is converting them into a definable employment status. At the same time, I’m not going to be the one paying for that idealism – they are. So I carry on contributing to the social fantasy that student debt is an inevitability and a solid investment, just to avoid dealing with the world as it is.

That guilt and vague foreboding must be even worse for teachers. No wonder they’re so into apprenticeships.

Zoe Williams is a Guardian columnist

‘A horrible nightmare’: the shocking story of the eBay harassment scandal

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 09:00:09 GMT

Jaw-dropping documentary Whatever It Takes details an aggressive stalking campaign targeted towards a couple whose reporting criticized the tech company

When Massachusetts husband-and-wife team Ina and David Steiner launched their reporting careers covering Silicon Valley at the turn of the century, the tech beat was still cozy enough to feel personal. The startups were still emerging from garages, and the journalists covering them were often filing stories from their own kitchen tables. Ebay was still a young online auction company founded on the principle that “people are basically good”.

As digital retail took off, the Steiners launched an e-zine called EcommerceBytes, catering to a growing community of small business owners who were increasingly using platforms like eBay to sell their wares. “We talked about this being an opportunity for just average people, disabled people, elderly people to make extra income,” says David of a community that has grown alongside EcommerceBytes, which now has more than 600,000 readers. “We were really excited about that.”

Their first 19 years on the beat were a dream, the kind of happily-ever-after usually reserved for Hallmark Christmas movie couples. But like any successful partnership, it depended on a division of labor. Ina handled what got published, and David handled how it got done. “Back when we started there was no WordPress,” Ina says. “He had to somehow conjure up his own system where we could still publish within budget.”

But they never expected the dream to turn into a nightmare, with them as the targets of a relentless cross-country harassment campaign orchestrated by the very company behind their livelihood. “This was a Stephen King nightmare, a horrible nightmare,” Ina says. “And then to find out the company that I had covered for 20 years was doing this is surreal. It’s taken an enormous toll on us physically and mentally.”

The Steiners have become a startling example of the modern dangers reporters can face simply for doing their jobs. Last month, the Steiners obtained a $55.7m settlement from eBay after six years of criminal and civil proceedings against the company in connection with its former employees’ efforts to intimidate the couple into silence. The macabre campaign unfolded over several weeks in 2019, with the Steiners receiving online threats and disturbing deliveries at their suburban Boston home: live spiders, a bloody pig mask modeled after the one worn by the killer in the Saw franchise, a book on surviving the death of a spouse. Pornographic magazines addressed to David were deliberately sent to the Steiners’ neighbors, and their home was listed online as a destination for pop-up yard sales and swingers’ parties.

The Steiners had scant idea who was behind the threats or what they had done to deserve them. “I thought it was some lone psychopath,” Ina said. “I reached out to my sister and said: ‘I have a stalker. Please tell everyone to disengage and disconnect from me on social media.’ We were so terrified and afraid for our family. I was feeling this sort of shame.”

They went into self-preservation mode, jury-rigging noisemakers from pots and pans to alert them of potential home intruders, installing cameras throughout their house and sleeping in separate beds to ensure one had enough time to call for help if the other was attacked. All the while, local police were left dumbfounded by the case. They didn’t really start figuring it out until the Steiners provided a license plate number from a car that had been monitoring them, which they traced back to an eBay employee who had rented the vehicle with a corporate credit card.

From there, the mystery quickly unraveled: eBay was under pressure – struggling to keep pace with competitors, facing activist investors and embarrassed by Ina’s matter-of-fact reporting on then CEO Devin Wenig’s bloated compensation package. Text message exchanges between executives suggested how far they were willing to go to make the Steiners go away, and just how personal the vendetta had apparently become – a far cry from the cozy Silicon Valley of yesteryear:

“Take her down,” Wenig wrote.

“I want to see ashes,” wrote chief communications officer Steve Wymer. “As long as it takes. Whatever it takes.”

According to investigators, the company’s security chief, James Baugh, acting on orders from on high, then directed a group of young women who worked for him to target the Steiners – even as he was also harassing them and, in one case, was extramaritally involved. In 2020, the Department of Justice charged seven eBay executives and employees with cyberstalking. In 2022, Baugh was sentenced to 57 months in prison. In 2024, federal prosecutors entered into a deferred-prosecution agreement with eBay itself. The company admitted to a detailed statement of facts, was charged with six felony counts (including interstate-travel and electronic-communications stalking) and agreed to pay the maximum $3m criminal penalty.

Meanwhile, the Steiners’ $55.7m civil settlement – in which eBay admitted fault and apologized but disclaimed legal liability – brought a nearly two-year legal battle to a close. Wenig, who stepped down as eBay’s CEO in 2019, agreed to pay $2m to the Steiners and $1m to a first amendment rights charity in Ina’s name (he was not charged in connection with the harassment campaign). But the formal resolutions haven’t exactly brought an end to the Steiners’ personal and professional nightmare. Since their 2019 ordeal, they have each been diagnosed with PTSD, see therapists and take medication to manage anxiety. A simple grocery run still feels like a fraught proposition after all the stalking. David is still haunted by the memory of celebrating his 31st wedding anniversary with Ina over tuna and dry spaghetti, as their provisions ran dry. “Every time we went out or I tried to shop, something would happen and I’d have to run home,” he says.

The Prime Video documentary Whatever It Takes, directed by Jenny Carchman, retraces the Steiners’ ordeal with the pace of a paperback thriller – among other things, revealing an eBay corporate security operation that scans like a cross between an NSA counter-terrorism strike force and a frat house. The couple was vehemently against the idea of participating at first. But the documentary has helped their friends and neighbors understand the gravity of their situation, and why they were so cagey about accepting help. “We felt toxic,” David says. “We certainly didn’t want anybody else involved in something that was directed at us – so we just stayed in our bunker.”

Somehow, though, the Steiners managed to summon the resolve to continue publishing – partly to keep the scandal from swallowing the brand they built from scratch. “When people were writing about eBay targeting us, it was as if my 20 years of being a reporter didn’t exist,” Ina says. “I was reduced to: that lady who wrote the story about executive compensation.”

Given their own experience of being targeted, I wondered if they could empathize with the female employees who also found themselves caught up in the scandal.

“The New York Times did this interview with [one of the female eBay employees] and it was very sympathetic to her,” Ina says, reaching for empathy. “But from my point of view, it was like: she was harassing and terrifying me.”

David feels more guarded. “Ina, she’s a very forgiving person. But I’m just not there yet.”

The biggest victory in the Steiners’ civil settlement might be the absence of an NDA. They can speak publicly about the case and their ordeal and keep reporting on unfolding developments. While pursuing the civil case, they received a trove of more than 68,000 documents that have given them a far fuller picture of the internal effort to harass them and the executive failures that essentially let it happen. It’s tempting to describe the whole sordid affair as an early form of corporate terrorism. But given Wenig’s past experience as CEO of Thomson Reuters, home of one of the world’s largest newswire services, it reads more like news-on-news crime.

It’s a reminder of how much Silicon Valley has changed since the Steiners started on the tech beat, and how much their plucky operation stands apart from the industry-friendly coverage that dominates the mainstream. For the Steiners, the $55.7m settlement is not the payoff for a fight they wanted to win and leave behind for another chance at a happily-ever-after – on a tropical island where eBay is blocked, maybe. Rather, it underscores the honor of their fight, the value of their integrity and that principled journalism is still well worth doing.

Not surprisingly, EcommerceBytes’ loyal readers understood the value of that work already. “We get emails and comments that are like: ‘We know you guys deserve a break, the compensation and the security, but please don’t stop publishing,’” Ina says. “That is really meaningful to find.”

Whatever It Takes is now available on Amazon Prime

MPs shouldn’t face abuse. Neither should their staff – but here’s what we deal with every day

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 09:00:09 GMT

Some constituents are traumatised, others violent. It’s time the House of Commons protected the people who work at the grassroots of our democracy

Before most people have finished their morning coffee, an MP’s staffer might have already dealt with a suicidal caller, a victim of domestic abuse or a threat of violence. We’ve been yelled at, cried at and sometimes even physically attacked, all in the course of managing a heavy caseload of constituents’ most intractable problems. So, when I read the results of the latest survey by the wellness working group (WWG) I was not surprised to learn that half of us are experiencing clinical levels of psychological distress.

Psychological distress is a range of emotional or cognitive suffering, such as anxiety or depression, that impairs a person’s ability to cope with ordinary daily life and functioning. It makes you wonder why urgent action is not in place to support these staff members, who are holding together the day-to-day functioning of democracy as well as supporting constituents throughout the UK.

As the office manager for the Labour MP for Preston, and an executive committee member for the WWG, I encounter this distress every day. In an MP’s email inbox are images that no one should see. These include murdered children, flayed animals, nude images and graphic pictures of war zones, all sent by constituents to try to strengthen their points or illustrate the reality of the situations they face. While photos are often triggering, so is some written communication from constituents who urgently need their MP’s help. Vivid descriptions of child abuse, domestic violence, physical ill health or the thoughts of people who are severely mentally ill or suicidal take a huge toll. The reality is that usually it is not the MP who opens these communications but a member of staff in their constituency or parliamentary office – often an office manager, sometimes a junior colleague who has just left university and hasn’t received any training at all. These teams also receive the racist or sexist language and the rape and death threats that are levelled at some members of parliament.

Sometimes these communications are sent directly to the staff themselves, or staff are abused personally on social media. In my role at the WWG I speak weekly to colleagues who have been physically attacked, doxed (sharing someone’s person data online) and stalked. One told me how she lived in a small village, where a constituent found her address and knocked on her door to shout abuse at her. We all remember our colleague Andrew Pennington, who was killed in 2000 while protecting the MP he worked for from a constituent with a sword. This kind of abuse seems to be increasing, yet support and protection for staff has not kept pace. It’s little wonder that we are overwhelmed and at breaking point. A third of staff are considering leaving their jobs.

Staff members are now at their lowest psychological point since the WWG began the survey in 2000, including the year of the pandemic – and I think that’s because the nature of the work is changing. The pressure for MPs and their staff to be always on is increasing with the expectation by some constituents that we should respond immediately to their emails and posts on social media. What many people do not understand is that an MP is supported by a very small number of staff and that each year our casework is increasing, both in volume and complexity. This has resulted in some offices having hundreds of emails backlogged. Staff are fighting to reassure people for whom we are struggling to find solutions. Obviously to each constituent, theirs is the most important case, but offices have to triage. Is there an immediate threat of death, destitution or disaster? These cases must be brought to the fore, pushing others further back in the never-ending list of work to be done.

The survey too points to how many staff are working until late at night, logging on early in the morning and not taking lunch breaks, or their annual leave, to try to get on top of the workload. I know of staff who go into their offices at weekends to check the answerphone to see if their vulnerable constituent is still alive, and those who buy weekly shopping and deliver it to constituents who don’t receive any other support. Inappropriate? Most of us do this work because we care, we want to help people and we find it hard to see others in extreme distress.

Contrary to popular opinion, the vast majority of members of parliament are honest, decent people who come into public service because they also care and want to make the country a better place. They care about their constituents and hire people who want to help them. However, like any large group of people, there are always a few who don’t have the right intentions. Some of those few expect their staff to work all hours and a small percentage expect their staff to work in unsanitary conditions, without running water or even toilet roll.

In response to the annual survey the WWG provides a series of recommendations, and this year they are more urgent than ever. The House of Commons should mandate specific training for staff, taking the decision for this out of the hands of the individual MPs who employ us. This would equip them with the skills to help constituents, and to protect themselves mentally and physically. Similarly, the House should make its recommended safety measures mandatory in all MPs’ offices – these include providing alarms and security counters, and ensuring that all surgeries are by pre-booked appointment only. The House of Commons has a duty of care to the staff who work in MPs offices – it should ensure that basic security measures are provided as a standard minimum requirement away from the parliamentary estate.

If we expect MPs’ staff to absorb trauma, safeguard vulnerable people and keep constituency services functioning, then we also have a duty to equip and protect them. If we do not do this, the public ultimately feels the consequences, and we further erode faith in democracy.

Estelle Warhurst is the office manager for a Labour MP and an executive committee member for the parliamentary wellness working group

Does Sophie Cunningham benefit from white privilege? Of course she does

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 08:30:08 GMT

One of the player’s WNBA rivals said the Fever guard is treated differently from her Black peers. There’s plenty of evidence to back that statement up

White privilege. An accusation that triggers multitudes of white people in America as if you had insulted their mother. Mention racism, and people may debate you. Mention discrimination, and people may even listen. But say the words “white privilege”, and suddenly defenses go up, outrage erupts, and people start explaining why privilege couldn’t possibly have anything to do with what everyone just witnessed with their own eyes.

Which brings us to DiJonai Carrington and Sophie Cunningham.

In Saturday’s WNBA game between Chicago Sky and Indiana Fever, Carrington was ejected after being assessed a Flagrant 2 for making contact with Cunningham’s head and neck area while attempting to defend her in transition. Carrington, who is Black, maintained she was making a play on the ball and didn’t intentionally hit Cunningham, who is white. Even Cunningham’s coach at the Fever, Stephanie White, said she did not believe Carrington intentionally went out to hurt Cunningham.

Carrington acknowledged she should have been penalized for the foul. But after her ejection, she went on social media and posted those two words many white people seem to fear so much: “WHITE PRIVILEGE”.

Predictably, Carrington’s words became as controversial as the foul itself. But instead of dismissing Carrington’s accusation, we should ask why she posted the words that she did – she has said they had nothing to do with the officiating decision itself. What happens when Cunningham, a white woman, is the aggressor, and what happens when she is the “victim”?

We’ve already seen the first scenario. In June 2025, Cunningham committed a Flagrant 2 foul against Connecticut Sun guard Jacy Sheldon. Cunningham wrapped Sheldon up as she drove toward the basket, sent her to the floor, and ignited an altercation. Cunningham was correctly ejected and fined by the WNBA.

If we are going to discuss racial double standards, we shouldn’t manipulate facts. Cunningham was ejected in 2025, just as Carrington was ejected in 2026. The referees treated both players the same – as Carrington herself appeared to suggest. Instead, the white privilege is found in what happened next.

As my co-host on The Collision podcast, Chuck Modi, pointed out after Cunningham’s 2025 foul, a significant portion of the public didn’t transform her into a dangerous menace threatening the integrity of women’s basketball. Instead, she was lauded as an enforcer. She was the teammate willing to protect Caitlin Clark, a player whom many on the right see as a delicate white flower who needs protecting at all costs. Cunningham also became immensely marketable. Her TikTok following exploded from 300,000 to over one million, and she was flooded with lucrative marketing opportunities.

Ring partnered with Cunningham in an advertisement that explicitly played off her image as the Fever’s “protector”. The campaign converted the symbolism of her retaliatory foul into a branding opportunity: Cunningham protects her teammates; Ring protects her home.

Think about that. A white player is ejected, and emerges with exponentially more followers, an “enforcer” identity, and lucrative endorsements.

Now imagine if Carrington had been flooded with endorsement deals built around being an “enforcer” after her foul on Cunningham. Would America think that was cute? Would television personalities laugh and celebrate her toughness? Or would she be described with words like “dangerous”, “dirty” and “thug”?

That question isn’t hypothetical, either. Look at what happened when Carrington fouled Cunningham.

Carrington’s foul was immediately framed by commentators as something larger than basketball. Cunningham was portrayed not simply as the recipient of a Flagrant 2, but as another white WNBA player being victimized by an increasingly dangerous culture within the league. We’ve heard similar rhetoric surrounding Clark for years: opponents aren’t merely fouling her; they are targeting her, jealous of her, and trying to hurt her. The racial subtext matters because the villains in this narrative are disproportionately Black women, while the white players like Clark and Cunningham are portrayed as in need of protection.

Then the Carrington-Cunningham incident went somewhere very strange: Florida’s attorney general, James Uthmeier, got involved. Uthmeier said he would be willing to charge players with assault and battery if they committed hard fouls in Florida.

This is, of course, ridiculous, and Uthmeier – no doubt grandstanding for a certain demographic of conservative voters – knows it. Quite apart from the fact that Florida doesn’t have an WNBA team, we are talking about professional basketball. Hard fouls have existed since Dr James Naismith hung a peach basket on a wall. Players have been clotheslined, elbowed, shoved, and involved in fights. Flagrant fouls exist precisely because sports already have mechanisms to distinguish basketball contact from excessive contact. Yet suddenly, we are discussing criminal prosecution.

Sign up to WNBA 30

A limited run women’s basketball newsletter dedicated to the league’s historic 30th season.

after newsletter promotion

Would we be having that conversation if Cunningham had fouled Carrington? We don’t have to guess. When Cunningham committed her retaliatory Flagrant 2 against Jacy Sheldon, where was Uthmeier? Where were the threats of assault charges, the politicians warning the WNBA, or the television segments asking if Black women were safe from prowling white women?

That’s what privilege looks like. When Cunningham fouls an opponent, she’s an enforcer. When Carrington does the same, she’s a danger.

This isn’t the first time Cunningham has become a cultural phenomenon. In June, she pointed at Phoenix Mercury’s DeWanna Bonner during an on-court confrontation. The image exploded across social media, accumulating tens of millions of views as Cunningham’s point became a meme. People described it as hilarious, petty, and iconic – and there’s no denying it was entertaining.

But in considering white privilege, I posted an image on social media of Cunningham as a Black WNBA player and asked: How would it be viewed if the roles were reversed? If a Black player followed a white opponent around the court, staring her down and pointing in her face for 20 seconds. Would she still be called adorable? Or would she be described as threatening, aggressive, classless, and hostile?

That’s why conversations about white privilege cannot stop at whether two players received the same foul classification on the scorecard.

We could test this. Take every comparable WNBA flagrant foul over the last several seasons involving contact to the head or neck. Separate the incidents by the race of the offending player and the race of the recipient. Examine the headlines and social media posts. How frequently do the words “hard foul” come up versus “cheap shot”, “brutal”, “assault”, “targeted”, or “clobbered”?

That would be a far more meaningful investigation of white privilege than screaming about playing “the race card.”

Privilege isn’t always receiving something someone else didn’t. Sometimes privilege is being granted innocence of interpretation. It’s having your aggression interpreted as toughness, your confrontation as personality, your retaliation as loyalty, your defiance as humor, and your anger as passion. Meanwhile, someone else’s aggression becomes violence, confrontation becomes hostility, retaliation becomes assault, and anger becomes evidence that she is dangerous.

White privilege doesn’t require Cunningham to ask for it or be racist. It doesn’t require every referee, journalist, or politician to consciously think: “She’s white, so I’m going to treat her differently”. Privilege operates through assumptions, sympathy, and framing – through whose aggression gets contextualized and whose gets criminalized.

So when Carrington talks about white privilege, instead of becoming enraged, America should examine why those two words make people angrier than the double standard she’s asking them to examine. The question isn’t simply whether white privilege exists in the WNBA. The real question is: How much more evidence do people need before they’re willing to admit that it does?

Read original article

Could you predict which children will be Neets? No – and it’s unfair to ask that of teachers

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 05:00:04 GMT

The idea of government adviser Alan Milburn sounds laudable. But it’s asking too much of a system already struggling to meet society’s demands

When you go to the dentist, what do you expect? That they will check your teeth, see if you need any fillings, give you some advice on oral hygiene? You probably don’t expect that they will teach your child to read, tackle youth unemployment or solve poverty.

But when it comes to education, politicians are remarkably relaxed about loading wider social responsibilities on to schools. The suggestion from Alan Milburn, a former minister and now a senior government adviser, is that primary schools – that is, schools that educate children aged four to 11 – should identify children who might not be in education, employment or training at the age of 16. “The warning signs are there from a very early, early age. You know, frankly from preschool,” he said. “So, definitely primary schools should be identifying children who are at risk of being Neet. And secondary schools and other services should be intercepting, intervening, supporting and helping those young people to get on a different path.”

Laudable in theory, but we need a bit of realism about what it is fair to make schools responsible for, and if making them responsible for so many things is going to cause more problems than it solves.

Defenders of this approach will argue that big problems have many causes, so we need a holistic response that breaks down institutional silos. I’d argue that by making people responsible for outcomes they have very little control over, you risk demoralising them and distracting them from what they can control.

Of course, it is true that primaries, and secondaries as well, have some influence over students’ later career prospects. There are numerous studies showing the importance of numeracy and literacy to economic outcomes. Getting GCSE English and maths means a student will be much less likely to be Neet. In fact, if a primary did want to create a “Neet risk register”, the simplest thing to do would be to list the pupils struggling to read.

But that just shows the futility of this kind of policy. The main way a primary school can contribute to a pupil’s future work success is by teaching them maths and English. Requiring a separate Neet strategy would add another layer of bureaucracy to a system that is already too full of them. A boy who is struggling to read shouldn’t need a “potentially unemployed in 2034” database tag before a school can act.

We also need to recognise that while schools have influence over students’ later success, they are just one small part of a wider system. Having better basic skills and more qualifications will help individuals succeed in the labour market. But if there are persistent structural problems with the labour market, improving individuals’ skills will not necessarily create more or better opportunities overall.

Our new government disagrees. They see rising youth unemployment rates as largely education’s problem. It isn’t just Milburn: the new education secretary, Lucy Powell, has suggested a causal link between schools policy and Neet rates, and this analysis is being used to justify radical reform, or as she puts it “rewiring the entire education system to give young people from all backgrounds the chance to thrive”. But is that link true? Neet rates have risen in England and Wales, despite them having very different education systems. The tax and regulatory burden of employing young people has increased in the past few years, and this may have contributed to the sudden rise in Neets over and above what goes on in schools.

More broadly, we need to look at systemic issues with the British economy. A recent analysis by John Burn-Murdoch in the Financial Times showed that US workers who are functionally illiterate earn as much as the average British worker. “The same proportion of US workers who score stunningly low on literacy earn an average of almost $30 per hour, and two-thirds of them are in work,” he said. “Their British counterparts make the equivalent of $20 and fewer than half are employed.”

Schools can make sure students have the skills they need to thrive in a modern economy, but the economy has to be set up to take advantage of those skills. Increasingly, ours is not. We need to look at all the factors other than skills that might be contributing to this: housing, energy, transport, tax, regulation, health.

And we need to be careful: if we keep improving skills without increasing opportunities, we risk creating a generation of smart but disillusioned young people, youngsters who worked hard at school and university but still ended up with worse living standards than their parents and their peers in other countries.

Sign up to Matters of Opinion

Guardian columnists and writers on what they’ve been debating, thinking about, reading, and more

after newsletter promotion

Over the past 20 years, the UK has barely seen any per capita GDP growth, or any growth in median wages. In the same period, primary schools in England have risen up the rankings on international tests: according to the most recent rankings in 2021, England is one of the best-performing countries in the west on Pirls, a test of primary literacy. Primaries are a rare example of an English public policy success. We risk undermining that success by making them compensate for state failures elsewhere.

More broadly, why is it that a holistic approach to a social problem generally requires schools to take on extra responsibilities? “Joined-up government” always seems to involve schools helping the Treasury and Home Office achieve their objectives, not Treasury officials and police officers teaching eight-year-olds to read and count.

Schools are already doing a good job. Some other parts of the British state need to get better at doing theirs.

Daisy Christodoulou is an education writer and director of education at No More Marking, an educational technology startup

What does it say about the UK’s pro-Europeans that they hide their message behind a bin?

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 04:00:04 GMT

Voters disillusioned by Brexit should have more options than Nigel Farage or the novelty candidate Count Binface

The UK could be forgiven if, like Brenda from Bristol, it feels it has had too much politics of late. The long death rattle of the Starmer government against a backdrop of relentless excitement from the White House has left everyone suffering a severe case of overwhelm. We all deserve a holiday – even Andy Burnham, exhausted after a few weeks in the job.

So the political and media class may think they are doing us a favour by turning the forthcoming byelection prompted by the resignation of the Reform UK leader, Nigel Farage, into a joke. But it is not very fair on the people of Clacton, who are being told they face a binary choice on 13 August, between Farage and the novelty candidate Count Binface.

Most people outside the Essex constituency have already picked a side. Unless you are a diehard Reform supporter, everyone seems to be backing the bin. A huge media operation is in full swing, in the hope that Clacton voters will oblige by embarrassing Farage. A speaker on BBC Radio 4’s Thought for the Day piously informed listeners that Binface’s candidacy belongs to a rich tradition in Britain of political satire that has its origins in the parables of Jesus Christ. The new high priests of political broadcasting, Alastair Campbell and Rory Stewart, bestowed their blessing on Binface with the sacrament of a podcast interview.

Yet the Clacton byelection is not a two-way contest. There are, in fact, 34 candidates on the ballot – but there is one other candidate who, unlike Binface, is running a highly professional, constituency-wide campaign: John Stevens, of the Rejoin EU party.

Stevens, a former Conservative member of the European parliament and ardent pro-European, insists that there are many voters in Clacton who dislike Farage and are no fans of Brexit, and he is determined to give them a voice – so I went to see for myself.

Based on my observations of Stevens and his enthusiastic team of volunteers, marshalled by a formidably well-organised agent, as they delivered leaflets across three different parts of the constituency, he is right. The places we visited may have been chosen because data gathered via Facebook suggested they were fertile territory. But in the quieter streets of genteel Frinton-on-Sea and the tidy new-build estates of the surrounding villages, there was striking hostility to Nigel Farage, and an overwhelming view that Brexit had failed.

True, not all thought it had failed because it was a bad idea; nor did they necessarily blame Farage as opposed to the Tories for what most thought had been a terrible mistake; nor were they all convinced that the cost of rejoining would be worth it. But what was also clear was that many people were uncomfortable about expressing their opposition to Farage by voting for Binface. Unlike Radio 4’s sermoniser, they did not feel they had divine permission to turn a sacred democratic right into a national punchline. Nor did they think it would reflect well on Clacton, or the country.

An Ipsos poll suggests they are not alone. It found that just 25% of voters nationally have a positive view of joke candidates, falling to just 13% of Conservative voters – and Clacton pre-Farage was one of the safest Tory seats in Britain. All of which suggests that pro-Europeans relying on Binface to give Farage a bloody nose may be in for a disappointment.

Besides, it is one thing to tell a pollster that you are inclined to cast a protest vote. It is quite another to head to the polling station to cast it in person – particularly when Binface, unlike Stevens, has little by way of a ground campaign to get you out to vote.

And yet, ironically, the strongest opposition to Stevens comes from fellow pro-Europeans, who accuse him of splitting the anti-Farage vote. They assume that a vote for Rejoin EU is one vote lost for Binface, rather than a positive choice by someone who otherwise would not vote at all. Prominent pro-Europeans have not only ignored his candidacy but privately admonished him for campaigning for the cause he has championed all his career – in an election that, thanks to the boycott of mainstream parties, is a golden opportunity for voters to make a statement.

Sign up to Headlines Europe

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

after newsletter promotion

This seems remarkably shortsighted. What does it say about the strength of the pro-European cause in Britain that it dare not make its case in public? Many pro-Europeans complain about the timidity of politicians. Andy Burnham’s EU policy is a blank sheet, perhaps to be filled in on the beach. The Liberal Democrat position is an unworkable fudge. But how are those who earnestly wish to reverse Brexit ever going to shift the Overton window if they are so afraid of making an argument that they hide behind a bin?

It is clear from the number of Reform placards plastered around Clacton that Farage will win his byelection handily. A show of support for Rejoin EU in Farage’s heartland would at least send a signal that Westminster could not ignore. But a vote for Binface fuelled by nothing more than media vibes would be devoid of any useful information about Farage, Clacton or Britain. The joke would last about 24 hours. And it would be on all of us.

Simon Nixon is a journalist and economics commentator
