‘We are still here’: a day in the life of 12 Afghan women

The Guardian Tue, 11 Aug 2026 04:00:03 GMT

In the five years since the Taliban’s return to power, the walls have been closing in on the women and girls of Afghanistan.

As the regime has tightened its grip, it has banned girls from education after the age of 12, ejected many women from the workplace and created legal frameworks for child marriage and husbands beating their wives. Taliban rule has ushered in a new era of women being whipped for minor infractions and constant inspections by ‘vice and morality’ officials, with men compelled to become enforcers in their own homes. Even the borders of the online world have been creeping inward, with a new spate of smartphone bans and internet restrictions.

Marking the grim anniversary, a dozen women and girls have, at great risk to their safety, documented their lives for the world to see

Herat provinceEvery day, I wake up to the silence of dawn. I feel as though I am still holding my weapon. I lie on my bed for a few moments, taking deep breaths. With every awakening, I make a promise to myself: if my husband manages to return home safely today, I will thank God even more.

My name is Parasto*, and I live in Herat. For six years I served here as a soldier. Within a few days of taking the city, the first thing the Taliban did to me and my female colleagues was hold a meeting and demand that we hand over our uniforms. When they took that uniform, I felt as though I had lost something grand that belonged to me; it was a profound pain.

Because my husband and I served with the previous government, the Taliban have summoned him several times to question him about how many Taliban he killed, or how many I killed. We have been threatened with death many times, through phone calls and messages. In the early days, many of my colleagues fled. The Taliban arrested two women whose names I do not wish to mention, and nothing is known of their fate to this day. Where are they? Are they alive or not? We tried to smuggle ourselves over the border to Iran, but were turned back by border guards. Today, we still keep our bags packed and waiting, in case we have to flee.

Now, my morning begins with chores. I wake, light the fire, make breakfast, prepare my children for school and my husband so that he can search for a day’s work. I stand by the house gate until they leave. After the gate closes, the house sinks back into silence. I pull the curtains closed again so no one knows who lives here.

I spend the days alone in the heat of this house. Sometimes, to keep my mind off the past, I sit behind the sewing machine, thread the needle, stitch the fabric slowly. It is a way to pass long hours of loneliness. The weather is also getting hotter every day, as if fire is raining from the sky.

At night, before my eyes close, I think of the days when I would prepare myself for the next day’s shift.

I had so many dreams of serving this country more, but the times do not allow me to. I still dream that I am wearing my uniform and going to duty. When I wake up, it takes a few moments to realise that none of it exists any more.

As told to Goharshad

Northern provincesThe fear of being exposed is constant. Every time someone knocks on our gate, I panic. Whenever I hear the sound, I immediately hide my laptop under my mattress because of what is held on its screen.

I am an undercover journalist in one of Afghanistan’s northern provinces, working under an alias. My writing days unfold in a 12-sq-metre room, with a small desk, a mobile phone and a laptop.

Coffee is my therapy. Every morning, as soon as I get out of bed, the first thing I reach for is a cup of coffee. I pick up my phone and check the latest news in Afghanistan. I open my pseudonymous email account to see what assignment my editor has left.

I have already lived through two house searches by Taliban officers. Since then, every knock at the gate fills me with dread. What if it is the Taliban? What if they enter the house while I am working on a report about a killing, interviewing a transgender person or writing the account of a survivor of violence? A thousand scenarios race through my mind and fear consumes me.

The job of a journalist is to meet people and record their stories – but every time I do so, it holds the risk that I could be betrayed. Last week, I went to interview a woman with a disability who had experienced domestic violence. She lived on the outskirts of the city, so a friend accompanied me. To reach her house, I had to change taxis twice. The trip was filled with anxiety because she lived in an area where restrictions are especially severe: women wear burqas, and it is unacceptable for a woman to move around without a male guardian.

We walked along dusty alleyways, winding through countless narrow turns. It was a small, impoverished house with barely any light. As I spoke to her, the entire time I was terrified. I had never met her before; she had only been introduced to me through someone I trusted. I did not know what kind of place I was walking into or whether I could trust the situation.

When I returned, I told my editor that we should not publish yet. I feared that, after publication, the Taliban could use the city’s expanding network of security cameras to trace my movements and identify her, or me. This is the reality of working under Taliban rule: reporting in a near-total suppression of press freedom is extraordinarily difficult.

By Sophia Malek

Kabul Every day, when most people are still asleep, my sister Marzia and I wake at 4am. We light the oven. This is how our day begins.

My name is Fatema*. I am 32 years old and a mother of six: four sons and two daughters. Me and Marzia, who herself has eight children, run a small home bakery in a corner of Kabul.

Women from all over come to us with dough they have prepared the night before, asking us to bake their bread. This work has been our only stable source of income during these five difficult years.

My eyebrows and eyelashes have disappeared because of constant exposure to the heat of the fire. My eyes burn. My knees ache at night, and I no longer feel the burns on my hands. Work is good, but I never wanted such a hard life and such difficult work for my daughters.

I pray my morning prayer while the oven is heating up. After the prayer, women begin arriving one by one. Some come from distant neighbourhoods, carrying with them fear and worry. They say: “The Taliban were in such and such alley today.” Sometimes they arrive silently, hand over their dough and wait.

We bake the bread, and they return home with fresh loaves.

As I work, I always remember my mother. She taught my sister and me the art of breadmaking, and was a wise woman. She encouraged me to attend school so I could learn the Qur’an, and she taught me sewing, baking and caring for my children. All these skills have helped me survive. I still light the oven the way my mother taught me. But the wood, which used to cost 60 afghanis per bundle (70p), has risen to 110 afghanis (£1.25). Now, we use almond shells as fuel – they’re cheaper, but their bitter smell stays with me until the end of the day. We charge six afghanis for each batch of bread. On good days, we might earn about 1,000 afghanis (£11), but usually we earn half of that. My family of eight and Marzia’s family of 10 survive on this.

My eldest daughter is now 12, and my youngest is eight. Beyond how difficult the work itself is, my biggest worry is that I cannot do anything for their education. I don’t have enough income to send them abroad to study, and I have no hope that conditions inside Afghanistan will improve soon. I wanted my daughters to study and have a more informed and comfortable life. What if they end up like me? A difficult life with very limited choices.

By mid-morning, I can take a break, take off my work clothes, wash my hands and face, eat breakfast and rest a while. In the afternoon, I light the oven again, and the work continues until 7pm.

When women come and share stories about their lives, I realise that the lives of women and girls in Afghanistan have never been easy. With the arrival of the Taliban, they have become a living hell.

As told to Tarana Mohammadi

Undisclosed I wake up to the sound of my parents’ laughter. In the living room, my mother spreads out her flower-patterned tablecloth and prepares breakfast. For me, she makes a boiled egg because she knows I love them. I am 12 years old, preparing for my sixth-grade exams – the last I will be allowed to take.

After breakfast, my sisters and I go to our room, where my older sister gets lost in old books and notebooks, studying and writing. She dreamed of becoming a writer whose books would be read around the world. But the Taliban buried that dream inside her. My other sister reached 10th grade before she had to leave. Now she sits for hours, searching social media for scholarships and journalism training programmes.

I know that soon the fate that came to my sisters will come for me. I will also remain trapped in this room, our forced refuge for years. Even at night we do not dare pull back the curtains for fresh air, fearing the Taliban or suspicious neighbours. What if someone says: “Three young girls live in this house and move around freely inside it”?

On the shelf where we keep our clothes, there is no colour left except black. I put on my black dress, scarf, mask and chador. I must wear my chador, even in this scorching heat – two classmates were arrested and expelled from school for failing to wear it correctly.

Finally, I reach the school gate, surrounded by girls. The teacher enters the classroom and places a preparatory exam paper in front of us. But I stare at the exam paper and ask myself: what should I write? What future am I studying for, when that path no longer exists?

Every day after school, I go straight to that same room and record my blog. I talk about life, recite poetry and speak about girls whose books have remained closed for years, women prevented from living an ordinary life. My mother brings her phone to record me. I receive encouraging messages from women and girls who relate to my words. But whenever I publish a video, relatives come to our home and fill my father’s ears with criticism. They say: “Your daughter has grown up. The honour and dignity of the family will be destroyed. Why is she blogging? Why is she speaking out against the Taliban?”

Every time I hear this, I feel like more walls are being built around my life. One greater fear stays with me: that someone might reveal my identity and location to the Taliban. Today, perhaps the Taliban see my videos and do not know who the voice behind them is. But what happens if they find me?

Sometimes I imagine that if one day I can return to seventh grade, if I can place my books on a desk again and hold my pen without fear, the first thing I will write will be the story of these days. I am a 12-year-old girl. Hear our voices, before our silent rooms become filled with buried dreams. We are still here.

As told to Awa Nikyar

UndisclosedTwenty-five years ago, when I was 10, I contracted polio. Because we were a poor family living through war in a remote village, we had no access to doctors or vaccines. My legs bent at the knees, and walking was difficult. My body and height did not grow like those of average people.

As a child, struggling with the muscle pain and paralysis, I realised my life would be different from other people’s. I saw my pace in this world was slow, and that I was viewed as a burden. The pain I saw in my mother’s eyes made me realise how difficult my life was going to be. She never said it out loud, but I read it in her gaze.

To escape the pain in my mind and body, I would take a needle and plastic scraps to make clothes for dolls. Gradually, I won my mother’s trust and she let me hand-sew a dress for her. Her faint smile was a stamp of approval. I still remember the first proper garment I ever sewed: a white cotton shirt and trousers for my father. He made my heart swell with joy, saying: “Bravo, my daughter!”

This talent was a gift from the creator: I had never had a teacher, but I found I could look at anything, replicate its pattern and sew it. One day, one of my sisters showed me a dress by a skilled tailor and asked if I could make one like it. When it was done, seeing I could work like a master tailor, she promised to buy me a sewing machine – and she kept her promise.

From that day on, my reputation spread by word of mouth, and clients began coming from more and more distant villages.

When clients arrive, their first look is often searching and sceptical. They ask: “Is the tailor here?” I know exactly what they are thinking. When I tell them it is me, they place their orders with looks of disbelief and curiosity. When they see me using a pen and paper to write down measurements, they ask: “How are you literate?” They have no idea how hard I worked to teach myself to read. I became a student of my own students; in exchange for teaching them tailoring, I had them teach me writing.

I sometimes have so many orders that I can earn 6-7,000 afghani (£68-80) a month, helping to support the family. Since my mother died and my father lost his sight due to old age, I run the household myself. I keep thanking God for giving me the strength to do so. Despite my legs and skeleton being ravaged by polio, I have never looked down on myself. My art has ensured that people do not look at me with pity or condescension; instead, they look at me as their trusted tailor.

As told to Hura Omar

Balkh province If you want to know me, do not start with my name, but with my room’s window. Every morning, before the city fully wakes up, I sit in front of it. The road in front of me is just an ordinary street, but to me it is filled with people, and each has a story that no one has yet written. An old man who slowly drags his cane across the asphalt; a girl who clutches her bag tightly to her chest and walks briskly; a child chasing his ball … Sometimes, before I write the first sentence, I just watch them for a few minutes.

I am Mahtab*. I am 23 years old and from Balkh province, and I am writing a novel.

I began writing on my own, but now I take online classes as well. One by one, students appear onscreen. Some could be my older sister or even my mother. Every time I see their faces, I think how remarkable it is: here, where the paths to learning grow narrower every day, there are still people who have not given up. Sometimes, the internet disconnects, sometimes the power goes out, but none of this stops the class.

Let me tell you about last week: I was missing going to the bookshop, so I adjusted my shawl and set off. The little bell above the door chimed as it opened, and the smell of paper and new books filled the shop. The bookseller smiled and said: “I haven’t seen you in a long time.” I went straight to the fiction shelf. My fingers slid over the spines of the books, and I was holding one in my hand when, through the glass, a few men in official attire passed by. Instinctively, I held my breath.

Through the glass, I saw girls passing the shop: one holding her little sister’s hand; one with a book hidden beneath her shawl; another walking so fast, as if she wanted to get away from the street’s gaze. When I stepped out, I held the book tight against my chest. In a time when so much can be taken from a person, the dream of reading, writing and creating cannot be stolen unless one gives it up. For me, writing is not just putting words together; it is a way to preserve what is being lost: dreams, memories, the voices of people who are still hopeful. Perhaps, one day, someone will read these writings and know there were people who, despite all the walls, still kept a window open to the future.

When night falls, I sit behind the same window again. I may not be able to change the pain of this city, but I can remember it, write it down and not let it be lost in silence.

As told to Sofia Malik

Jawzjan provinceMy husband used to be the breadwinner in our family. But in 2021, he was in injured in a traffic accident, leaving him unable to stand on his feet or work. From that moment, the responsibility of providing for the family fell on my shoulders.

My name is Leilma*. I am 33 years old. I am originally from Faryab province, but I now live in Jawzjan. I am the mother of four children – three daughters and one son.

Every day, I wake up before sunrise, pray and begin my household chores. I prepare breakfast for my children and husband, send my children to school, prepare food for our cow and goats and feed them, then tie them under the shade of trees so they can be protected from the scorching sun.

Then, I head to the hotel where I work as a waitress and cleaner. From 6.30am until 8 or 9pm, I work continuously.

On days when there are wedding ceremonies at the hotel, I arrive early, arrange tables and chairs, and serve guests food. Then I clean every part of the hotel with brushes, mops and brooms, and arrange the tables and chairs again. I wash the tablecloths, chair covers and dining sheets, and dry them on the hotel roof. Once they are dry, I collect them, spread them back on the tables and chairs and return home.

For all this work, I earn only 200 afghanis (£2) a day. Although this income is very little and does not cover all our needs, I have no choice but to continue. My children’s hopes are tied to my tired hands.

I live with my husband and four children in a very small house with only one room. To be able to build at least one more room, I make raw bricks whenever I have free time.

My brother used to do this work so I know how bricks are made: preparing the soil, mixing it with water to make mud. I spend hours pressing and kneading the mud with my feet until it is ready. After that, using a brick-making mould, I shape the raw bricks from the mud and leave them under the sun to dry.

Sometimes, there is a wedding ceremony at the hotel while the mud for making bricks is also ready at home, so I cover the mud with plastic and go to the hotel. When I return home at night, I start making bricks again.

My neighbours tell me that if I continue putting so much pressure on myself, I will collapse. But I do not know any other way. My children are all still young, and I cannot bring myself to send them to work.

I tell myself: I did not have the chance to study and became a cleaner; let them study and become educated.

As told to Simin Daneshjo

Day Kundi provinceMy day begins at four in the morning. After waking, I pray and read the Qur’an until the sky begins to brighten. Then I pick up empty water containers and walk to the nearby spring to collect fresh water.

I have breakfast with my son – tea, sugar and tandoori bread. We live in a remote village in Day Kundi province, but unlike many other villagers, our table is not filled with eggs or cheeses. My name is Sediqa*. I am 41 years old, and I have been teaching at my village’s girls’ school for 18 years. I studied at this same school. After the fall of the previous Taliban regime, when Hamid Karzai came to power in 2002 and girls’ schools reopened, I was among the first to return – although I was over 20 years old at the time, I started my education from first grade. Eventually, I became a teacher.

I married a man who promised to support my work and education, because I hoped to build an independent life. But after my son was born, he failed to support me, did not allow me to study at university and opposed my going to work. Then, when the government collapsed and the Taliban took power, my husband left Afghanistan. My son and I were displaced, moving from one person’s home to another. The house I built through years of hardship and with the money I earned from teaching was taken away from me by his family. We eventually settled in a mud house near the school.

In the morning, I prepare dough and go to an area among the trees near my house to gather firewood. I light the clay oven and bake our bread, then leave for school with my son. We each take separate mountain paths and walk to our schools alone. My son walks an hour on foot while my journey takes 45 minutes.

Eighteen years ago, many girls and I gathered at this school under a single tent. There were no professional teachers, our school had no building and the tent was unbearable in the heat and cold – yet we gathered beneath that roof with great enthusiasm and a deep desire to learn.

Today, our school has a building, and classrooms with desks and chairs. But our girls can no longer study beyond sixth grade. The school uniform has been replaced with large black prayer garments, chador namaz, which education officials come to inspect. The youngest girls are just six years old. Despite their age and the long distances they travel, they must carry these heavy garments through the difficult journey, enduring the heat and cold.

I teach first grade, and my day ends with the happy laughter of these young girls. Girls who are starting school for the first time are happy, energetic and full of dreams. Being around them gives me a sense of joy.

As told to Nahid Naji

Nangarhar provinceSometimes, when I catch a glimpse of myself in the mirror during the day and see the colour of my face and my hair, I fall silent. I see how much I have truly changed over these past few years. My skin has become pale and lifeless, as if I am fading away; the dark circles under my eyes look frightening. Sometimes, I think the girl I see in the mirror is not me.

My name is Leima*. I am 24 years old, and I was studying computer science at a private university in Nangarhar when the Taliban announced the closure of universities. I was in my room, working on a course project. It was 9pm when I finished studying and saw the news on my phone.

I fell into a state of shock. When my mother and sisters saw me they became silent. They had wanted to hide the news from me, but I already knew. For days, my family and relatives tried to reassure me that the universities would reopen.

When the universities stayed shut, I was left hopeless. My father – who has always supported our education – told me not to worry, that he would find every possible way for me to continue my studies. Eventually, he decided that we should leave Afghanistan and go to Pakistan or Turkey so I could resume my education. But because my younger brother has a physical disability and is paralysed, we were unable to leave.

Now, life has reached a point where no ordinary day is truly ordinary. My mind is so filled with thoughts and worries that even when I’m cooking, tears fall from my eyes without me realising.

This morning I woke up at 2am, recited prayers and sat on my prayer mat, crying. Every night, I ask God to make everything right. In the darkness, I go into the courtyard and look at the silent sky, the moon and the stars. It gives me a sense of comfort. I tell myself that everything will change one day.

There was a time when I would dress up every day. I would lovingly dye and style my hair, apply my favourite bright lipstick, listen to my favourite music at full volume and dance happily in my room. This morning, after brushing my teeth, I wanted to comb my hair. But my worries are making it fall out: so much hair gathered on the brush that I felt as if every strand had died.

As I sat there, my eyes fell on my old perfumes sitting beside the mirror. I picked one up and sprayed it on myself; for a moment, my mood changed. I felt like the person I used to be years ago.

As told to Hosai Ismail Khan

Faryab province My workday revolves around plaster, moulds, plastic prosthetics, scissors, screws, rubber, glue and other tools.

My name is Nazanin*. I am 42 years old and the mother of four children – two daughters and two sons. For the past 21 years, I have worked as a technician in a hospital, making orthopaedic assistive devices, casts and plastic prosthetic limbs for women and children with physical disabilities.

I begin each day with prayer, then wake my two sons, who leave for their classes at 6am. My younger daughter is in second grade, so I take her to school in my rickshaw. My older daughter, however, is 13 and no longer allowed to attend school.

Now, she stays home alone. She does all the housework, prepares meals for her father and siblings. She has been at home for two years, and it breaks my heart. No one truly understands a mother’s fear. Every time I close the gate behind me, it feels as though my soul remains on the other side with my daughter.

I arrive at work at 8am and check how many orders we have. I produce several casts or artificial limbs most days.

I think about my daughter as I work and wonder what she is doing. How long can she keep herself busy with housework? How much television can she watch? I worry that she might harm herself. I worry that she might lose hope.

Then I tell myself that there are women who need me – who dream of walking independently and doing everyday tasks on their own. One young woman I worked with was the mother of seven children. Her husband was a farmer and they lived in a remote mountainous district. Before receiving the prosthesis, she had to crawl to do her daily tasks.

Now, she can walk powerfully on those uneven fields. Later, when she visited us to have her prosthetic leg repaired, she told us with great happiness: “I went to the market – I saw the market up close. I work on my land now, and I no longer think about the fact that I once did not have a leg.”

In the evening, when my husband returns from work, he comforts me about our daughter. He says: “Are you anxious? Well, this is something that has happened to all girls.” He takes our daughters out for a walk and buys them ice-cream or something small. He says: “At least let their faces light up a little.”

As told to Tomiris Tamga

Jawzjan provinceGetting to my place of work is never easy. On my way, I pass several mosques and a Taliban-run religious school. When the young men come out, the way they look at me sometimes makes me fearful. Every time I walk that route, I wonder whether I could end up like Farkhunda – a young woman killed by a mob for no reason. The men’s stares reveal how deeply they resent women leaving their homes.

My name is Marwa*. I am 23 years old, and once I dreamed of being a brain surgeon. When women were banned from universities, I found I could study an online course in dental prosthetics. Today, I work as a dental assistant in a small private clinic run by a friend of my father’s.

When I arrive at the clinic, I push fear aside and focus on my work. Taliban officials and soldiers all come to the clinic. One commander came to have a tooth extracted and asked the dentist about the anaesthetic injection. The soldier with him laughed and said: “Doctor, don’t numb the commander. Our commander has spilled so much blood – let him lose a little blood from his tooth as well.”

Hearing that, I wondered how many innocent people this man had helped kill, how many families he had left without loved ones, how many women and children had suffered because of him. Every moment, I worried that he might suddenly become angry.

Officials from the Ministry for the Propagation of Virtue and the Prevention of Vice visit the clinic to carry out inspections. They always ask: “Who is this young woman’s guardian and why is she working alone?” Every time, I worry they might shut down the clinic or create problems for us. Whenever they arrive, I hide so they do not see me.

This is not the future I imagined for myself. When I was 17 and began preparing for Afghanistan’s national university entrance exam, I told myself I would earn the highest score in my province. I had dreamed of becoming a doctor, but by the time exam results were announced, the political system had changed and I wasn’t able to pursue that. Once, a man who had been my teacher came in for treatment. When he saw me, he said: “You are capable of much more than being a nurse or an assistant. If circumstances had been different, you could have become a better doctor than many practising today.” His kind words meant a great deal.

There are months when I feel hopeless. But then, a small sense of hope returns. Maybe the circumstances are not right today, but no one knows what tomorrow will bring. Some girls have lost hope and are searching for a refuge in life – they think if they get married, they can find that refuge in their husbands. But I want to be my own refuge.

As told to Atousa Azar

Samangan province People often say that the beginning of the week starts with motivation and energy, but since the day Afghanistan fell to the Taliban and my studies were left unfinished, I have had no motivation to live. Morning and evening, day and night, every day of the week feels the same: dark and heavy.

My name is Fatana*. I am 25 years old and live in one of Afghanistan’s northern provinces. When the Taliban seized control of Afghanistan, I was a third-year student of law and political science, living in a government-run dormitory. Since then, I have been prevented from continuing my education. My six-year-old sister and I get into a rickshaw and go to school – a private school where I have worked as a teacher for nearly two years and earn a monthly salary of 4,000 afghanis (£45). Alongside teaching, I try to encourage my students. I tell them that to achieve success, they often have to work several times harder than boys.

One of my students lost her father to cancer last year, and her family is struggling financially. She is a quiet and sorrowful girl who often keeps to herself. In addition to her regular lessons, I teach her English for one hour every day through WhatsApp. Sometimes, I cannot escape worrying about her future. I fear that poverty and family pressure could force her into child marriage – a fate that still shapes the lives of many children.

The most painful part of my work at the school is seeing my students graduate from sixth grade. Their sad and hopeless faces on their final days are unbearable for me. Every time I think about those girls, and wonder what conditions they are living in today, my heart aches. I ask myself: how many of them are still holding on to their dreams of education? How many have been forced into marriages they did not choose?

As told to Raha Malik

* Names have been changed

With El-Sayed’s Victory Comes a Wave of Islamophobia

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

As Muslim politicians like Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan have become more prominent, they have experienced considerable bigotry. President Trump said Democrats were electing “jihadists.”

By Jennifer Medina

Senator Tommy Tuberville called Dr. Abdul El-Sayed a “terrorist.”

Representative Nancy Mace described “every single Muslim holding public office in America” as “a threat to both national security and our republic.”

And on Monday, President Trump said that there were “jihadists being elected all over the place.”

In the days since Dr. El-Sayed’s victory in Michigan’s Democratic Senate primary, Republican leaders have embraced Islamophobic attacks and falsehoods like those three comments to portray him as an anti-American extremist. The onslaught is being fueled in part by Republican Party officials and has quickly become a central aspect of the strategy to beat Dr. El-Sayed in the fall. Few officials in the Republican Party have publicly tried to tamp down such messaging, though Dr. El-Sayed has not hesitated to parry the attacks.

“They’re going to point at me, they’re going to point at my name, like, ‘Wow, he’s different,’” he said the morning after his win. “My name is not raising your gas prices,” he added. “My name is not taking us to war. My name is not the reason you can’t afford your groceries.”

Muslim and Arab American political power is growing across the country, led by prominent figures including Zohran Mamdani, the mayor of New York City, and Dr. El-Sayed, who is the son of Egyptian immigrants and was born in Michigan. The number of Muslim American running for — and winning — local, state and federal elections has steadily increased over the past decade. Today, there are more than 230 elected Muslim officials, according to the Council on American-Islamic Relations, including four Muslim Americans in Congress, the highest number ever.

Three more could be elected to the House this fall. And in Michigan last week, several Arab Americans won contested primaries, including for the state legislature and mayor of Ann Arbor.

But the increase in power has also drawn considerable repercussions. Muslim officials say that their increased visibility has brought with it a wave of bigotry and threats. In recent years, Mr. Trump has repeatedly attacked Representative Ilhan Omar, an immigrant, mocking her hijab and declaring that she should be “sent back” to Somalia. Multiple Republicans have falsely accused Mr. Mamdani of being a jihadist.

“There has been remarkable political progress for Muslims in American, but the backlash to that has come in the form of anti-Muslim hate that seems to be the worst we have seen yet,” said Edward Ahmed Mitchell, the national deputy director for CAIR, one of the largest Muslim advocacy groups in the United States. Mr. Mitchell said that much of the negative response has stemmed from candidates’ criticism of Israel over the past several years.

“We have a generation who became very forceful at speaking for themselves,” Mr. Mitchell said. “They essentially said we are not going to take this anymore, we are not going to be begging for scraps.”

Many of these new leaders have made Palestinian rights and an end to military aid to Israel central to their platforms, arguing that such assistance directly undercuts spending on U. S. health care, housing and public schools. Their wins have helped shift the politics within the Democratic Party, where support for Israel now amounts to a liability in some quarters.

That change has helped fuel Republicans claims that the party is electing extremist candidates. Mike Pompeo, the former secretary of state, accused Dr. El-Sayed of being a “Hamas supporter.” Dr. El-Sayed has condemned the group.

The attacks have not been limited to Muslim and Arab American politicians’ views on Israel. More broadly, they have tried to depict candidates as out of step with the country, sometimes dangerously so.

The first advertisement against Dr. El-Sayed from the National Republican Senatorial Committee last week called him “the most radical Senate candidate in America” and pointedly referred to him by his full name, Abdulrahman Mohamed El-Sayed.

Mike Rogers, the Republican Senate candidate in Michigan, accused Dr. El Sayed of believing that the United States “deserved” the Sept. 11, 2001, attacks — a sentiment he has denied. Hasan Piker, the left-wing podcaster Dr. El-Sayed has campaigned with, voiced that view but later called it “inappropriate.”

Days later, Mr. Trump juxtaposed two photographs in a social media post, one of himself standing next to Melania Trump in a strapless dress, and the other of Dr. El-Sayed sitting next to his wife, who was wearing a hijab. “Two VERY DIFFERENT America’s,” the president wrote.

Last week, Senator Ted Cruz, Republican of Texas, held a hearing on what he called the threat of the Muslim Brotherhood network in America, after saying that Dr. El-Sayed and other Democrats “hate America.”

In a House primary in Michigan last week, a little-known Republican who had suspended his own campaign beat Amir Hassan, a Navy veteran who had Mr. Trump’s endorsement and is Christian. A third candidate in the race acknowledged that his campaign created advertisements that “didn’t so much appeal to bigotry against Muslims as kind of counted on it.”

Asked about such tactics in a television interview over the weekend, Senator Bill Cassidy of Louisiana, who recently lost a bid for re-election, said he condemned the bigoted comments from his fellow Republicans. “Everybody should be treated as an individual,” he said. But few other high-profile Republicans have spoken out against such language.

It’s not clear what impact such speech has with voters. Representative Andy Ogles of Tennessee, who has made statements like “Muslims don’t belong in American society,” lost his Republican primary last week.

On Sunday, Dr. El-Sayed pushed back against Mr. Trump’s notion of two Americas by attacking the president’s marriage while praising his own.

“Yes, you’ve got two different visions of America,” he said in an interview on CNN. “One in which your overlords are two people who don’t like each other but joined in the interest of making billions of dollars off of you, or two people who genuinely love each other, enjoyed some pancakes together, want to come together to build the kind of America where they can raise a family, and know that that family is going to have the good things.”

CAIR estimates that there are more than 2.5 million Muslims registered to vote across the country, with many concentrated in key battleground states.

Nowhere is the rise to power among Muslim political leaders and activists more consequential than in Michigan, a swing state with the highest concentration of Arab Americans in the country. In 2020, President Joseph R. Biden Jr. carried the state with overwhelming support from Dearborn, a city where Arab Americans make up a majority of the population. But in 2024, as the Biden administration continued to support Israel during its bombardment in Gaza, many of those voters were disillusioned. Mr. Trump won Dearborn by six percentage points and helped to flip Michigan, as Democratic turnout in Muslim-majority precincts plummeted.

“As Arab Americans, we have more power than we’ve ever had,” said Abbas Alawieh, a former congressional staffer and longtime Democratic organizer. Last week, Mr. Alawieh easily won a Democratic primary in a newly drawn State Senate district that includes the highest concentration of Muslim and Arab American voters in Michigan.

In 2024, Mr. Alawieh helped create the “Uncommitted” campaign, encouraging Democratic voters not to cast a ballot for Mr. Biden to show their opposition to the war in Gaza. He called Dr. El-Sayed’s primary victory a “monumental shift in our politics.”

“We are reaching disillusioned voters and changing the conception of what is possible,” he said.

In the days leading up to Michigan’s primary election, organizers from Emgage, which focuses on empowering Muslim voters, knocked on more than 15,000 doors and sent nearly a million text messages targeting registered voters. Organizers from the Council on American-Islamic Relations called more than 48,000 Michigan voters to encourage them to cast their ballots.

Dr. El-Sayed performed strongly in significant Arab American populations. In Dearborn, he won nearly 80 percent of the vote and 70 percent of the vote in nearby Dearborn Heights.

Yet for all of the history-making potential of his campaign, Dr. El-Sayed and his supporters often hesitate to frame his platform around identity. In an interview last week with The New York Times, he resisted a question about the potential of becoming the country’s first Muslim senator.

“I think this is a moment for people who want to bring integrity back to our politics,” he said. “I’m just interested in being the next U. S. senator from Michigan. Yes, I have an uncommon name, which they’re going to point to a lot. But I know America is big enough for people with names like mine. People are asking less about how you pray than what you pray for.”

And he has repeatedly emphasized his optimism.

“This is the best place in the world to be Abdul,” he said the morning after his win.

Tim Balk contributed reporting.

Jennifer Medina is a Los Angeles-based political reporter for The Times, focused on political attitudes and demographic change.

How Iran Adapted Its Attacks as U. S. War Munitions Dwindled

The New York Times August 11, 2026

War in the Middle East

Advertisement

Supported by

The Iranian strikes that killed three U. S. soldiers in Jordan show how quickly Iran’s war-fighting skills have evolved, as the Pentagon runs low on interceptors.

By Eric Schmitt and Helene Cooper

Reporting from Washington

During five intense days last month, Iran launched waves of drones and missiles at U. S. troops on three bases in Jordan, hoping to break through the formidable American air defenses.

Using advanced missiles that can suddenly change course, Iran pummeled the bases’ defensive systems, forcing the Americans to burn through scarce supplies of Patriot interceptors.

On the fifth day, the Iranians broke through.

A missile struck prefabricated housing units at one of the bases on July 17, killing three U. S. soldiers and wounding more than 100. Pentagon officials later acknowledged that scores of other troops were injured and several aircraft damaged in attacks in Jordan that week.

“We shot down almost all the missiles,” Secretary of State Marco Rubio said when asked about the fatal attack. “One leaked through.”

The strikes reveal a deadly confluence of two important trends: First, Iran has become a more skilled adversary as the war has progressed, learning how to evade U. S. air defenses as it widened the battlefield to include most of the Middle East and enlisted allies like the Houthis in Yemen and militias in Iraq.

Second, the Pentagon’s supply of interceptors has quickly dwindled, with the weapons becoming more precious each day of the off-and-on conflict.

The two trends drive home a point that has slowly emerged in the months since the war started, that a global superpower with a mighty military footprint can still be held at bay by a lesser adversary willing to wage asymmetric warfare to find any advantage it can.

The Pentagon has used more than 1,500 Patriot interceptor missiles in the war and has fewer than 1,700 interceptors left, according to two people briefed on the stockpile levels who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss sensitive military details. The United States produced about 600 interceptors in all of 2025.

The rapid depletion is occurring as Iranian forces study another war. In Ukraine, Russia has used combinations of ballistic and cruise missiles to distract Ukrainian air defenses, and then rushed in drones.

“The Iranians are making it more difficult on air defenses when you have a range of both missiles and drones coming in,” said Seth G. Jones, the president of the defense and security department at the Center for Strategic and International Studies.

“They have effectively leveraged the Russian tactic,” he said, “to make it more difficult to protect across locations in the Gulf.”

On a single day during that deadly week last month, according to two U. S. officials, American forces in the Middle East fired about 50 Patriot interceptors, which cost about $4 million each.

The Pentagon has continued to face a powerful threat, which it has tried to minimize, in the months since major combat ended in early April.

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth told reporters on April 8 that Iran’s missile program was “functionally destroyed,” that its launchers and missiles were “depleted and decimated and almost completely ineffective.”

Most of Iran’s missiles and launchers were stored in vast underground facilities that U. S. and Israeli forces heavily bombed during the first five weeks of the war. But Iran dug out many of those sites and resumed firing, bringing shipping in the Strait of Hormuz to a near standstill and threatening American troops in the region.

President Trump and his national security advisers assumed the fight would be short and would not jeopardize the Pentagon’s stockpiles of Patriot interceptors, said Vali Nasr, a professor at the Johns Hopkins School of Advanced International Studies.

But Tehran, he and other Iran experts say, had been waiting for this moment since the conflict began more than five months ago. During a 12-day war in June 2025, Iran’s leaders had seen how quickly Israeli and American forces burned through ballistic missile interceptors to knock down barrages of Iranian drones and missiles.

Iran’s deadliest strikes against U. S. forces in Jordan targeted Muwaffaq Salti Air Base in Azraq, a Jordanian military installation that the U. S. Air Force has increasingly used as a major hub for dozens of aircraft and several thousand service members.

Many U. S. troops previously stationed in Kuwait, Bahrain, Qatar and the United Arab Emirates were moved before and during the war to bases like Muwaffaq Salti in Jordan that were farther from Iran and deemed safer from drone and ballistic attacks.

In the days immediately after the strike on July 17, American military commanders strengthened air defenses in Jordan and elsewhere in the region, said a senior U. S. military official, who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss operational matters but declined to provide more details.

U. S. military specialists who have also studied drone and missile attacks in Ukraine have been working with the military’s Central Command for months, sharing lessons learned from that campaign. Two of the three soldiers killed in Jordan were assigned to air defense units based in Germany that became part of that effort.

This new reality is having geopolitical consequences.

In late July, Mr. Trump asked Mr. Hegseth about the dwindling American weapons supplies, U. S. officials said, confirming reporting by The Washington Post.

The Pentagon has maintained publicly that it does not have a shortfall. “Claims of U. S. munition shortages are false,” the chief Pentagon spokesman Sean Parnell said in a statement. “When the fake news media irresponsibly amplifies these lies, the real-world consequence is clear: Our enemies are emboldened to provoke — or attack — American troops.”

Two weeks ago, Mr. Trump called off bombing raids against Iran after he said the leaders of Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates and Qatar had asked him to pursue diplomatic talks to end the war, which has killed an estimated 1,700 Iranian civilians.

In a phone call, Mohammed bin Salman, the de facto ruler of Saudi Arabia, told Mr. Trump that major airstrikes would probably prompt Iran to retaliate against energy sites and other infrastructure in the kingdom and its neighbors, according to a Saudi official, who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss confidential discussions.

With the United States running low on interceptors and Iran’s demonstrating that it could pierce those defenses, the threat of serious damage was very real, the Saudi official said. Prince Mohammed urged Mr. Trump to de-escalate, the official said.

The Jordan attack was not the first time Iran has adapted its skills and tactics in this war.

In April, Iran shot down an American F-15E fighter jet over southern Iran with a shoulder-fired surface-to-air missile. Both the pilot and the weapons officer ejected. The pilot was rescued quickly, but the weapons officer was missing for nearly two days, hiding from Iranian security forces until U. S. Special Operations forces rescued him in a daring operation.

In this case, Iran learned from Ukraine and deployed a swarm of drones in an area American fighter jets had penetrated, a senior U. S. military official said. In interviews with Pentagon officials, the crew said that the F-15E’s defensive countermeasures provided only half a second of warning before Iran hit the $60 million advanced fighter jet with a shoulder-fired missile.

American military officials concluded that the drones fed three critical pieces of information to Iranian commanders that helped them target the F-15E: its GPS location, speed and direction, one senior military official said.

Mr. Trump announced a “pause” in the fighting shortly after that incident. Military officials needed time to adjust to Iran’s new tactics, two military officials said.

Even during breaks in fighting, those tactics have continued to evolve.

When the Houthi militia in Yemen entered the war last month, experts said it signaled Iran’s intention to force the United States and its allies to defend themselves from aerial attacks from all directions. Yemen is hundreds of miles south of Iran.

International inspectors also believe that Iran is storing elements of its nuclear program in a heavily fortified underground complex in central Iran. The site, which the United States refers to as Pickaxe Mountain, is buried more than 300 feet beneath a mountain.

And Iran has sent its troops and leaders to hide-outs scattered across the country, experts said.

“Where are they?” asked Mr. Nasr, of Johns Hopkins. “It’s not like they’re sitting in a place where you can bomb them. You don’t find them.”

Jonathan Swan contributed reporting.

Eric Schmitt is a national security correspondent for The Times. He has reported on U. S. military affairs and counterterrorism for more than three decades. Contact him securely on Signal: @ericschmitt.36.

Helene Cooper is a Pentagon correspondent for The Times. She was previously an editor, diplomatic correspondent and White House correspondent.

In Minnesota, a Moderate Democrat Has a Big Cash Edge Over Her Liberal Rival

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

Representative Angie Craig has benefited from nearly $10 million in spending from groups whose donors remain hidden, giving her an advantage in her Senate primary against Lt. Gov. Peggy Flanagan.

By Theodore Schleifer

Theodore Schleifer covers campaign finance.

Over and over this year, moderate Democrats have had big financial advantages over their progressive rivals in primary races. Over and over, the progressives have won.

Representative Angie Craig is hoping the outcome is different on Tuesday in Minnesota.

Ms. Craig, a centrist running for Senate, has a significant edge on advertising spending over her left-wing Democratic primary opponent, Lt. Gov. Peggy Flanagan. Ms. Craig has outspent her by more than four to one, $19.9 million versus $4.7 million, including money from allied outside groups, according to data from media tracking firm AdImpact.

Ms. Craig has been bolstered in particular by about $10 million in ad spending since late May from a low-profile group called North Star Dawn PAC.

That group — like an increasing number of super PACs with murky origins — has been funded almost entirely by a so-called dark-money nonprofit group, which allows the super PAC to avoid ever disclosing the true provenance of the cash. That nonprofit group, the Civic Progress Fund Inc., was incorporated last December in Delaware. It has also aired pro-Craig ads.

Ms. Flanagan has also sought to use Ms. Craig’s past support from the American Israel Public Affairs Committee — a major pro-Israel lobbying group that has proved highly divisive in Democratic primaries this cycle — against her. AIPAC has not endorsed Ms. Craig or directly spent money on her behalf in this race.

Ms. Flanagan has attacked Ms. Craig as the candidate of dark money. But the lieutenant governor has had her own outside spending, primarily from a group affiliated with Democratic lieutenant governors that she once led. Some of its money comes from groups with undisclosed donors.

Ads from both the lieutenant governors group and North Star Dawn have sought to portray the opposing candidate as in the pocket of special interests.

Ms. Craig’s campaign raised more money than Ms. Flanagan’s by a margin of about two to one over the course of the race, allowing the congresswoman to outspend the lieutenant governor.

Theodore Schleifer is a Times reporter covering billionaires and their impact on the world.

Mamdani to Abandon Plan to Replace Elizabeth Street Garden With Housing

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Mamdani Administration

Advertisement

Supported by

After more than a decade, the fight over an effort to build affordable housing on the site of a community garden in Manhattan is winding down.

By Mihir Zaveri and Dana Rubinstein

Mayor Zohran Mamdani is expected to walk away from a plan to build affordable housing on the site of a community garden in Lower Manhattan, according to two people familiar with the decision, handing a victory to some celebrities and other neighbors who had vigorously opposed the development.

The move is the latest twist in the protracted conflict over the site, known as the Elizabeth Street Garden, which over more than a decade has become a symbol of the tension between development and preservation and of the difficulties inherent to building housing in wealthy New York City neighborhoods.

Mr. Mamdani’s predecessor, Eric Adams, had been a stalwart supporter of the development on the one-acre site in NoLIta, which would have included 123 affordable homes for older people, along with green space. About 50 of those apartments were slated for homeless New Yorkers.

But the developers’ plans ran up against a groundswell of pro-garden sentiment from celebrities such as Patti Smith, Martin Scorsese and Robert De Niro, who called the space, with its stone sculptures and shade trees, a unique public oasis. Last summer, Mr. Adams abruptly said he would abandon the plan and build housing elsewhere.

Last fall, shortly after Mr. Mamdani’s election, Mr. Adams’s first deputy mayor, Randy Mastro, had the property designated as “parkland,” a move that blocked the city’s development plans and could be reversed only by the State Legislature.

The developers on the project — Pennrose, Habitat for Humanity and RiseBoro — sued the city, calling Mr. Mastro’s move unlawful and saying they wanted to “prevent an outgoing administration from destroying an urgently needed affordable-housing project through an act of naked executive power.”

Mr. Mamdani’s administration has been in talks with the developers for months, and is expected to settle the lawsuit in the coming days, Gothamist first reported on Tuesday. As part of the settlement, the city would agree to no longer pursue the development, according to the people, who spoke on the condition of anonymity to describe confidential settlement discussions.

Pennrose said in a statement that it would “continue to look for opportunities to partner with the city” to deliver affordable housing. The other developers did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

As a candidate, and as mayor, Mr. Mamdani has adopted a pro-density stance, arguing that only by building vast amounts of affordable apartments can New York City ease its chronic housing shortage. During his campaign, he told the news site Hell Gate that he would evict the Elizabeth Street Garden, the nonprofit tenant that occupies the site, during his first year in office. Days later, Mr. Mastro had the land designated as parkland.

It is not clear if Mr. Mamdani tried to get the state to reverse Mr. Mastro’s move.

Matthew Rauschenbach, a spokesman for the mayor, said the city was “continuing to work toward a resolution of the lawsuit initiated under the previous administration concerning the Elizabeth Street Garden site.” He added that the city was moving forward with another project to build 180 affordable homes nearby.

That site, at 22 Suffolk Street, had been presented by the Adams administration as an alternative to the Elizabeth Street Garden development.

Housing advocates lamented the impending settlement. “More than a decade has been lost,” said Annemarie Gray, executive director of Open New York, a nonprofit group that supports more development. “Seniors who should have been living in these homes years ago have died waiting. That is the deep injustice at the center of this story, and no settlement erases it.”

But Joseph Reiver, executive director of the Elizabeth Street Garden nonprofit, hailed the likely outcome as “the kind of resolution all New Yorkers deserve.”

And Christopher Marte, the city councilman who represents the district that contains the garden and who has fought against the development, thanked the Mamdani administration for working toward an agreement that prevented housing from being built on the site.

The councilman said in an interview that he supported other housing developments in his district, including at the Suffolk Street site.

“It’s an area that really needs true affordable housing,” Mr. Marte said.

Mr. Mastro, a frequent critic of Mr. Mamdani, was similarly happy with the outcome.

“If whatever settlement of this lawsuit the Mamdani administration is contemplating recognizes and preserves Elizabeth Street Garden as dedicated parkland, that is the right thing to do, and I applaud it,” Mr. Mastro said.

Mihir Zaveri covers housing in the New York City region for The Times.

Dana Rubinstein covers New York City politics and government for The Times.

Ocasio-Cortez Opened Up About Freezing Her Eggs. She Got Women Talking.

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

Many women say being open about the fertility treatment is a form of empowerment. But critics say all the talk can be misleading.

By Juliet Macur and Anemona Hartocollis

Eight long years ago, Meg Quinn decided not to share the details of a big decision.

She was 37 and just out of a long-term relationship when she started panicking. Was she running out of time to have children? Feeling helpless, she decided to do something that was expensive but might help her chances of becoming a mother.

She froze her eggs.

“It was hard to talk about,” Ms. Quinn, a food content creator who’s now 45, said on Monday in an interview from her home in Los Angeles. “There were so many different ways that it could go. Like, you could spend all this money and it could be for nothing.”

So when Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, 36, announced last weekend that she would be freezing her eggs — and then posted video on Instagram injecting herself with medication just before a TV interview — Ms. Quinn was stunned by the boldness of her actions.

“I just love that she’s making it public,” she said. “I think women, the more we share, the better it is.”

She added: “It’s hard on women. We have to do all these things, and, like, no guy has to do these things.”

Until about 2012, freezing eggs to preserve fertility was considered an experimental intervention for urgent cases like cancer patients whose fertility would be compromised by treatment.

But it’s only now, as thousands more go through a process, that women are freely talking about it.

Like breast cancer, menopause and menstruation, egg freezing has become yet another women’s health issue in which open discussion is seen as a form of empowerment. Ms. Ocasio-Cortez herself said that it was “badass that I’m going to be giving myself shots in the green room” of a TV studio.

Her very personal disclosure, however, also had a political overtones, as she linked her decision to freeze her eggs to the debates over reproductive choice.

“There is very strangely a political risk, or a political tax, that comes with sharing this info,” she said, adding that she wanted “to show more depictions of women having full lives in every context and every choice.”

Indeed, her announcement also brought disquiet over the medical messaging and outright condemnation of her choices.

Though some people refer to freezing eggs as an insurance policy, doctors said that it was not always a smooth process and hardly guaranteed fertility later in life. Conservative commentators, especially those who have decried the country’s dropping birthrate, said that in vitro fertilization and other reproductive technologies give women the false impression that they can wait to have children.

Allie Beth Stuckey, the popular conservative writer and commentator, posted: “She’s glamorizing the lie that women can have it all. You can’t. Biology is real and how you order your life makes or breaks your future.”

Whatever the risks or the politics, the rate of egg freezing has soared: In the decade between 2014 and 2024, the number of egg freezing cycles grew to 38,930 from 6,090, according to Dr. Jennifer Eaton, president of the Society for Assisted Reproductive Technology.

Many women see egg freezing as a reflection of the times, as they search for ways to navigate their education, careers and relationships. In interviews, they described a personal decision, which could be both empowering, but also defensive, in case their hopes of finding suitable life partners do not materialize.

Egg freezing seems natural, said Karina Martin, who is 34 and lives in Sacramento. She underwent the procedure two years ago, before studying to be a physician assistant. She said she initially thought she would be ready to use the eggs in about five years. If she has to have a baby alone, she said, she will do it.

She sympathizes with Ms. Ocasio-Cortez’s decision to preserve her options. “She is a hard-working woman who has put her career first,” she said. “It’s a really smart decision, and I don’t think it’s political.”

Avni Barman, the chief executive of the media company Gen She, froze her eggs last year, even though she was in a serious relationship and didn’t expect to need them.

Ms. Barman, 29, posted videos of the process, hoping to show that it is not difficult, especially for younger women. “I feel so powerful,” she said, because she was taking control of something she didn’t want to leave up to chance.

On her videos, she explained that the cost to freeze her eggs in New York City was about $7,000 and that she easily squeezed the procedure into her busy day, which included a conference and an investor dinner.

“I’m friends with a lot of really successful seven, eight, nine-figure female founders — they all do it,” Ms. Barman said in an interview. “To them, it’s like getting a designer bag — freezing your eggs, they cost the same — and that’s kind of the mentality around it.”

Doctors and experts, however, say the process is often not that easy. Egg freezing and other fertility treatments can be as stressful as cancer treatments, said Rena Gower, a fertility therapist based in Manhattan.

A woman must inject a cocktail of hormonal medications into her abdomen over a period of eight to 12 days. A doctor then retrieves the eggs by a needle inserted through the vaginal wall while the patient is sedated.

Mature eggs are flash frozen in liquid nitrogen, potentially preserving them for many years. It is essentially the first step of in vitro fertilization, which involves thawing the eggs, fertilizing them in the lab and creating embryos.

Many infertility doctors said they hope that Ms. Ocasio-Cortez’s transparency will lead to a bigger, clearer conversation about supporting a woman’s desire to have children.

Right now, fertility is poorly understood, they said.

“I’ve had patients come in at age 48, and I’m ready to start talking about menopause, and they’re ready to start talking about fertility,” said Dr. Audrey Buxbaum, a gynecologist at Downtown Women in Manhattan.

Ms. Gower, the fertility therapist, said that she has encountered many women who were afraid to tell anyone that they have frozen their eggs.

Sometimes, she said, women feel forced into secrecy by their families, who believe it is morally wrong. Or they feel guilt or shame that their body might be seen as broken, or that their romantic relationships aren’t working out.

Some women are nervous that motherhood — even the prospect of it — would negatively influence their jobs.

Ms. Quinn, the content creator who was surprised by Ms. Ocasio-Cortez’s openness, said freezing her eggs left her so drained that she felt numb.

She dove into her savings to pay about $16,000 for the initial cycle, which produced only seven of the 18 frozen eggs her doctor had hoped for.

She was devastated. The doctor suggested she complete one or two more cycles, but she couldn’t bear even the idea of it, emotionally or financially.

Two years later, in 2020, she met her life partner, Greg Gomes. And on Feb. 14, 2023, when Ms. Quinn was 42, a doctor implanted the embryo that became their daughter, Rory.

On Valentine’s Day of this year, Ms. Quinn felt brave enough to post the details of her motherhood journey on social media, and the lessons she learned. She wanted to give people hope “because there’s so much unknown before you get to this place.”

The title was, “I froze my eggs at 37.”

“I feel more confident to talk about it,” she said, “now that it has worked out.”

Annie Karni contributed reporting. Alain Delaquérière and Kirsten Noyes contributed research.

Juliet Macur is a national reporter at The Times, based in Washington, D. C., who often writes about America through the lens of sports.

Anemona Hartocollis is a national reporter for The Times, writing about public health.

How Abdul El-Sayed Can Win Michigan

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

Thomas L. Friedman

By Thomas L. Friedman

Opinion Columnist

I confess that I never expected to see an American election where the choice of candidates in more than a few districts will be a democratic socialist versus a Republican monarchist — but live long enough and you’ll see everything.

As I listen to these democratic socialists and King-Trump-right-or-wrong Republicans, one of my favorite movie lines keeps coming to mind. It’s from the 1997 film “As Good as It Gets,” when Jack Nicholson, playing a misanthropic writer, answers his door to find a neighbor asking him to walk a dog and offering cheery advice about opening window curtains to see “God’s beautiful work.”

Nicholson says to her, “Where do they teach you to talk like this?” He adds: “Sell crazy someplace else. We’re all stocked up here.”

How I wish I could say that to both these extremes: We’re all stocked up on crazy in American politics. Sell it someplace else.

But because this is the only country I have, because the midterms will be razor close and because I am convinced that two more years of Donald Trump completely unchained — controlling the House, the Senate and tacitly the Supreme Court — could bring about the end of America as we have known it, I have to think about how to make the best of some not great choices.

The case most deserving of attention is in Michigan, so here is some free advice for Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic Senate candidate there. Why him? Because his victory or defeat could determine whether Trump continues to be able to rule as a monarch without any congressional oversight.

Sir, if you want to win over Jewish and centrist voters in Michigan, increase your odds of victory, be true to your pro-Palestinian, Arab American roots and stick it to AIPAC, the pro-Israel organization whose super PAC spent $32 million trying to defeat you in the primary, you need to thread your political needle very carefully. There is a way.

El-Sayed says Michigan Jews should not be worried about him — insisting that his commitment “to Jewish safety is the same commitment that I have to the safety of my own daughters.” That’s commendable, but many American Jewish voters are not just looking for stronger police protection from their elected representatives. They are looking for recognition of the right of Jews to self-determination, like any other people.

Yes, many American Jews, especially young ones, now agree — as I do — with Israel’s former Mossad chief Tamir Pardo, who in 2023 denounced Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu for bringing “horrible racist parties” into the country’s highest cabinet offices, describing them as “a lot worse” than the Ku Klux Klan.

But don’t confuse that with the belief in the right of Israel to exist as a Jewish state: That is still something very dear to most American Jews — just as much as Palestinian statehood beats in the heart of most Arabs and Muslims. Israel is part of their identity, and El-Sayed is not swaying American Jews when he simply tells them he will protect their synagogues.

He needs to stress his even more commendable and constructive position — which I just heard him describe to CNN’s Kaitlan Collins — that he believes “in equal rights to peace, dignity and self-determination for Jewish Israelis and Palestinians alike.”

That is a very good perspective and it is different from that of the democratic socialist mayor of New York City, Zohran Mamdani, who has argued that Jews are not entitled to self-determination in a state of their own — a Jewish state in their biblical homeland.

As Jay Michaelson, a contributing columnist to The Forward, has pointed out: “Already, El-Sayed’s statement is a useful contrast to Mamdani’s. He is not pontificating about the abstract legitimacy of Israel or Palestine; on the contrary, he is insisting on the pragmatic view, that I share, that ‘what the ultimate peace should be’ must be decided by both Palestinians and Israelis — a position rejected by both the Zionism-is-colonialism crowd on the left and the Jewish supremacists on the right.”

But when El-Sayed campaigns with the far-left streamer Hasan Piker, who has said that “Hamas is a thousand times better” than Israel, it is not surprising that Jewish voters wonder what his real position is on their right to a state of their own like every other people.

Let’s be very clear: Hamas is a terrible organization, first and foremost for Palestinians. The free pass that it has been given by some pro-Palestinian progressives — who criticize Israel and never Hamas, as if it has no agency — is shameful.

I know something about Hamas’s agency. In February 2002, in this column, the Saudi crown prince at the time, Abdullah bin Abdul Aziz, for the first time, called on the Arab League to offer peace and normalization of relations with Israel in exchange for Israel’s full withdrawal from Palestinian land to the 1967 lines. That call led the Arab League to hold a peace conference the next month, on March 27 and 28, in Beirut to do just that. It issued what was called the Arab Peace Initiative. Do you know what Hamas’s response was to that first Pan-Arab peace initiative laying the groundwork for a two-state solution?

The night that Arab summit opened, a Hamas suicide bomber walked into a Passover Seder at an Israeli hotel in Netanya and detonated, killing 30 people and injuring more than 140. That’s Hamas — two decades before the Gaza war.

I normally would not even get into these weeds, but it is too distressing to watch how the democratic-socialist left has pulled so many young Democrats away from America’s longstanding Middle East position of two states for two people — self-determination for both Jews and Palestinians. Any other position is a geopolitical loser for America and a loser for Palestinians, Israelis, Arab Americans, Jewish Americans and Muslim Americans, and every other American.

El-Sayed should just keep stressing what he told CNN — that he believes “in equal rights to peace, dignity and self-determination for Jewish Israelis and Palestinians alike” and he’d support any peace agreement they mutually agreed to.

And then go back to talking about the Midwest and leave the Mideast alone.

Being for any other exclusivist position — that only Palestinians or Jews are entitled to a national homeland between the Mediterranean Sea and the Jordan River — is a prescription for a forever war, because approximately seven million Palestinians and seven million Jews now live in that area and neither group is leaving. Anyone advocating either of the above is an enemy of peace and will alienate the centrist voters who will decide this election.

That said, I can understand that El-Sayed is bitter about AIPAC spending $32 million to defeat him in the Michigan primary and will no doubt spend more to try to defeat him in the Senate race. But he can’t let it get the best of him and cost Democrats a Senate seat, leaving the country with two more years of Trumpian monarchy.

It is not only El-Sayed, though, who needs to draw clear redlines. American Jews have let AIPAC drive them over a cliff.

As readers of this column know, I have been a longtime AIPAC critic. (The true pro-Israel and pro-American lobby is J Street.) Drunk on its own power, AIPAC lost its way years ago when it had a choice regarding how to be pro-Israel — to put American interests and values first or Netanyahu’s interests and values first. AIPAC chose to be pro-Netanyahu.

When Netanyahu walked away from the two-state solution, AIPAC went along for the ride. When Netanyahu abandoned Israel’s standard bipartisan approach to American politics, stiff-armed Democrats and aligned Israel exclusively with Republicans and evangelical Christians, AIPAC went along for the ride.

When Netanyahu urged Republicans to try to block President Barack Obama’s Iran nuclear deal, AIPAC saluted and went along for the ride. AIPAC chose to ignore the fact that, as a onetime head of the Israeli Atomic Energy Commission, Uzi Eilam, wrote: “While the P. M. made his opinions known loud and clear, many senior Israeli military and intelligence officers, nuclear experts and strategic analysts were quietly arguing that this was a good deal for Israel that would actually make us much safer.”

Sure, AIPAC’s website states that it seeks to “broker Israeli-Palestinian peace, including a credible pathway to a two-state agreement.” Show me one statement AIPAC has made criticizing the Netanyahu government’s settlement expansion project, the most aggressive in Israel’s history, that seeks to make it physically impossible on the ground in the West Bank to build “a credible pathway to a two-state agreement.”

I repeat — this is not complicated. El-Sayed can be pro-American, pro-Michigan, pro-Palestinian, pro-Muslim, pro-Israel, pro-Jewish people and expose AIPAC for the Netanyahu front organization that it has become: Just keep being for two states for two peoples. Then just talk about Michigan.

Trust me, his Republican opponent will not like that. And if it upsets the far lefties in his own party, that’s a bonus for him and the rest of us.

Thomas L. Friedman is the foreign affairs Opinion columnist. He joined the paper in 1981 and has won three Pulitzer Prizes. He is the author of seven books, including “From Beirut to Jerusalem,” which won the National Book Award. @tomfriedman • Facebook

Late-Night Hunt for Survivors After Deadly Colombian Quake

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Colombia Earthquake

Advertisement

Supported by

“We heard them screaming,” said one rescuer in Cali, where rescuers face a race against time to find the living.

By Genevieve Glatsky and Julie Turkewitz

Genevieve Glatsky reported from Cali, Colombia, and Julie Turkewitz from Bogotá, Colombia.

Desperate searches for survivors of a deadly Colombian earthquake continued throughout the night Monday and into Tuesday, with firefighters and other emergency workers using pickaxes and other tools to hack through the rubble in affected cities.

The death toll had risen to at least 181 people by Tuesday morning, according to Asocapitales, an association of Colombian cities. At least 1,310 people were injured and 153 buildings have collapsed.

The governor of Chocó, the region in western Colombia that was the epicenter of the quake, said Tuesday morning on national radio that the full extent of the devastation was not yet known, in part because communications were still down in eight municipalities in her department.

“What we don’t have is a complete tally of the physical damage,” said the governor, Nubia Carolina Córdoba Curi, “because it is truly massive.”

Late Monday the U. S. State Department said that it was committing $15.5 million in aid for emergency shelter, food, protection and earthquake assessments.

“We are united in prayer with the Colombian people and stand ready to support President @ABDELAESPRIELLA,” the department said in a statement on X, referring to Colombia’s new president, Abelardo De La Espriella.

The 7.4-magnitude earthquake, which struck Monday morning, was the strongest recorded in Colombia in the last decade, according to the Colombian Geological Survey.

In Cali, Colombia’s third-largest city, known as a hub of culture, music and salsa dance, rescue workers spent a grim night scrambling over the remains of collapsed buildings, trying to hear the taps and calls of those trapped inside. Cellphones, headlamps and lights powered by generators showed the way. The city has had the highest death toll from the quake, with 95 people dead, according to Asocapitales.

At one collapsed residential building in the Tequendama neighborhood, firefighters, paramedics and other rescuers clawed through the debris to try to find a family of four they believed was trapped inside, while civilians formed human chains to haul away buckets of dust and rubble.

“We believe they’re there because we heard them screaming,” said Daniel Leguizamo, a 16-year-old paramedic with a private rescue squad who rushed to Cali from the city of Popayán shortly after the quake. “Right now they’re going to lift this concrete slab to try to free that section underneath.”

Katerín Carrillo, 32, a local business owner, said she and friends handed out water, electrolyte drinks and homemade sandwiches, taking them to rescue sites after learning about the damage online.

“We washed our hands with alcohol and made sandwiches right in the vehicle,” Ms. Carrillo said. “The people who are hauling rocks over there should at least have something to eat.”

Amid the debris lay remnants of lives disrupted: a scrapbook called “my 15 years” filled with old photographs, a “my first year” poster, a shoe, a piece of zebra wallpaper. Collected items pulled from the building were placed in bins, which people searched to recover what they could.

As the night wore on, firefighters remained in control of the search-and-rescue effort, with support from the police and city government, while civilians worked on rubble removal. An ambulance stood at the ready. A yellow construction machine was used to haul away some heavy concrete.

When rescuers needed quiet, people raised their hands and the signal rippled through the crowd.

By midnight, rescuers had still not reached the family of four.

But just a few hours later, at a different building, shouts went up. “One, two, three!” Firefighters who had come in from Bogotá, the capital, had found a man alive in the debris. “One, two, three!” they shouted as they pull him from the space between a column and a beam. “One, two, three!”

Genevieve Glatsky is a reporter for The Times, based in Bogotá, Colombia.

Julie Turkewitz is the Andes Bureau Chief for The Times, based in Bogotá, Colombia, covering Colombia, Venezuela, Bolivia, Ecuador and Peru.
