First-Semester Jitters? This College Has a Solution: No Letter Grades.

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

The University of Michigan will make first-semester grades pass or fail, to encourage students to try different classes and lessen fears of what a low grade could mean.

By Mark Arsenault

The University of Michigan will replace traditional letter grades for some first-semester freshmen with a pass/fail-style grade, to more gently ease new students into academic life.

The plan will go into effect in the fall of 2027 at the university’s College of Literature, Science and the Arts. It is intended to relieve pressure on freshmen and help them “start strong and curb the mental health crisis unfolding among college-aged individuals,” the university said in a statement.

Under the plan, first semester letter grades will still be recorded internally by the school for things like compliance reporting and eligibility for academic honors, the university said. Student transcripts, however, will reflect either “pass” for each course or “no credit.” First-semester grades will not factor into grade-point averages.

The new grading plan, reported earlier by The Chronicle of Higher Education, is similar to how the Massachusetts Institute of Technology records grades. First-semester freshman grades at M. I. T. are reported as either pass or no record, to allow students time to get accustomed to an increased college workload and different teaching methods, the M. I. T. Registrar’s Office explains on its website.

In many courses at Brown University, a student may choose between two grading options, according to the university’s Office of the Registrar. One is a grade of A, B, C or no credit. The other is a grade of satisfactory or no credit.

The University of Michigan said the point of the change “is to help first-year students acclimate to the demands of college,” and to allow students to set their early academic path with less grade pressure. “For example, they can explore a subject that may not come easily to them or take a course in a new field without the fear that a low grade may have long-term consequences,” the school said in its statement.

The policy faced immediate criticism from people who have argued that making academics easier for students does not ultimately help them. Rick Hess, a senior fellow and director of education policy studies at the American Enterprise Institute, said the policy is “exactly the wrong diagnosis.”

“It turns out that swaddling college students does not actually improve their mental health,” he said. “It creates entitlement. It creates a lack of productive tension.”

Mr. Hess urges the opposite approach. He called for “ratcheting up rigor” in coursework and “ratcheting up expectations” on students, with things like robust minimum reading requirements and curbs on cellphone use on campus.

Jonathan Alpert, a psychotherapist and author, agreed that getting a bad grade can feel awful and make students anxious. “But feeling bad isn’t the same thing as being unwell,” he said. “I worry that therapists and institutions have blurred that distinction.”

“College should be one of the places where young people learn that evaluation isn’t always pleasant,” he said. Students might get a C or worse, hear criticism from a professor or fail to get the internship they wanted. “Those experiences can hurt, but learning that you can tolerate them and move forward prepares you for adulthood,” he added.

Other prominent schools, such as Harvard University, have taken the opposite tack with grades — making them more competitive. The Harvard Faculty of Arts and Sciences voted overwhelmingly in May to restrict the number of A-grades professors may award in undergraduate classes. The school capped A’s at roughly 20 percent of the grades in a class after extensive study and debate over how to address grade inflation.

Mark Arsenault covers higher education for The Times.

What We Know About the Earthquake in Colombia

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Colombia Earthquake

Advertisement

Supported by

The 7.4-magnitude quake, which killed more than 100 people and damaged hundreds of buildings, was the strongest recorded in Colombia in a decade.

By John Yoon

A major earthquake struck Colombia on Monday, killing more than 100 people, damaging hundreds of buildings in the country’s west and prompting a frantic search for survivors.

The 7.4-magnitude earthquake was the most powerful recorded in the South American nation in the past decade, the Colombian Geological Survey said.

President Abelardo De La Espriella of Colombia, who had been sworn into office just three days before the quake, declared a state of emergency and said that he would directly lead the disaster response.

Here’s what to know.

The epicenter of the quake, according to the U. S. Geological Survey, was about three miles east of San José del Palmar — a town of about 5,000 people in the Chocó region, one of the poorest parts of Colombia.

The temblor, which struck about 7:34 a. m. local time, rattled much of the country, though the impact was worst in cities and towns in the foothills of the Andes.

Major damage was reported in Cali, the country’s third-largest city, which has about two million residents. The cities of Pereira and Manizales, hubs of Colombia’s coffee-growing region that are each home to about 500,000 people, were also badly hit.

The country’s two largest cities — Bogotá, the capital, and Medellín — appeared to have escaped the worst of the damage. But residents there and across the country reported intense shaking.

The quake was also felt in neighboring countries including Panama, Ecuador and Venezuela, though there were no reports of serious damage.

Note: Shake data as of 1:45 p. m. Eastern. Sources: U. S. G. S.; The LandScan Program; Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL); Global Earthquake Model Foundation. The New York Times

The death toll had risen to at least 181 people by Tuesday morning, according to Asocapitales, an association of Colombian cities. At least 1,310 people were injured and 153 buildings have collapsed.

Rescue efforts were underway across western Colombia. In Cali, Mayor Alejando Eder said that emergency workers were focusing on finding people in debris from 10 collapsed buildings. Mayor Carlos Fernando Galán of Bogotá said the city was preparing to send rescue crews to hard-hit cities.

It is unclear how long people can survive trapped in rubble after an earthquake, researchers say. But some experts say that there is a 72-hour “golden” window in which the most lives can be saved.

The quake damaged nearly 5,000 homes, with 387 destroyed, the U. N. humanitarian aid agency said, and 61 buildings collapsed.

Mr. De La Espriella said 18 health centers and 52 schools were also damaged.

In Cali, at least 26 buildings collapsed, trapping people inside, according to its mayor. In Calima El Darién, a town north of Cali, a hospital collapsed and another was overwhelmed with victims.

There was also widespread damage, including piles of rubble from collapsed buildings, in Pereira and Manizales. Several airports in western Colombia, including in Pereira and Manizales, were among the structures damaged, according to the Colombian Civil Aviation Authority.

The agency said it had suspended operations at some airports until structural damage could be assessed.

The earthquake is the first big test for Mr. De La Espriella, a right-wing former lawyer with no previous political experience who was sworn in on Friday after winning the presidential election in June.

He said in a video posted on social media on Monday that he had canceled his travel plans to head to Bogotá, where he said he would “personally assume leadership” of the emergency response.

“You’re not alone,” he wrote, addressing people affected by the disaster. “The state is present and taking action.”

Nick Cumming-Bruce contributed reporting.

John Yoon is a Times reporter based in Seoul who covers breaking and trending news.

Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

VHS Dates advertises itself as resurfacing lost dating service videotapes from the 1980s. The account’s followers have been genuinely touched.

The creators of the Instagram account VHS Dates describe its lore as such: “The box of VHS tapes was found in the basement of our office in Downtown L. A.”Credit...

Supported by

Written by Sopan Deb

Visuals by Maggie Shannon

Reporting from Los Angeles

The footage has the graininess of something recorded on a 1980s video camera. The man captured by the lens appears middle-aged and his name is Doug. He is wearing a striped sweater and wide glasses.

He speaks in halting sentences and looks uncomfortable to be on camera.

“How would it feel to be in love?” he says, with a deep sigh and pause. “Well, that — how would it feel to be in love is like — I really — I would really, really, really like to know.”

The clip comes from VHS Dates, an Instagram account with nearly 900,000 followers.

The people behind the account describe the origin story for the undated footage: “The box of VHS tapes was found in the basement of our office in Downtown L. A.” They are estimated to be from the “mid 80s to the early 90s” and from a company called “VHS Dates” that “existed in Van Nuys, California.”

The clips show the kind of videotape dating service some people used in an era when no one had imagined swiping left.

Viewers, including some celebrities, were touched by the onscreen vulnerability, as well as the outfits and hairstyles that looked like something out of an old Sears catalog.

I, too, found it compelling. I scheduled an interview with the two filmmakers who said they had been digitizing the footage. Their publicist explicitly told me these were “real” dating tapes.

But when I showed up to meet them at a cavernous studio near downtown Los Angeles, they said, “This is where we shoot the videos.”

The clips, they revealed, are not found footage from a lost era. The videos are staged.

VHS Dates is the creation of Lee Citron and Trevor Worley, both 39, who met at the American Film Institute and have collaborated on comedy projects. They run a production company, Podhead Studios.

For the videos, they hired actors, who were asked to keep their participation secret. The performers read bits conceived by Citron and Worley, and the actors themselves. “Doug,” for example, is portrayed by the actor Ryan Gaul, a member of the Groundlings improvisational theater company.

“I had actually been tricked by it and thought they were real,” Gaul said about getting approached by Citron and Worley.

The posts come across as a sort of museum exhibit of one way of finding love in the days when Blockbuster was ascendant and people wanted their MTV. In some ways, such videos were a prototype of contemporary online dating. In others, they are an unfamiliar time capsule.

The videos are shot against a green screen. A 1980s Ikegami tube camera helps give the videos a retro look.

“There’s a lot of people out there who have never touched a VHS tape in their entire life that are still drawn to this,” said Charisse L’Pree Corsbie-Massay, an associate professor of communications at Syracuse University who studies how media affects identity. “So it is not even their nostalgia. It’s cultural nostalgia. Because it’s not just the tape, it’s also the hair. It’s all of the clothes. It also the slang. It’s a desire for what we envisioned as a simpler time.”

In recent months, the duo have begun dropping the facade, in part because some actors — mostly Los Angeles-based sketch comedians — began to be recognized. At a Tribeca Festival event in New York in June, they dropped the pretense and even invited some of the actors to appear with them.

“We kind of think of it like kayfabe,” said Worley, using a term that describes how pro wrestling audiences suspend their disbelief.

At a time when everything is “potentially fake online, it was interesting from an analog perspective to create something in that world,” he added in an interview at the studio where the tapes are filmed.

“We’re all so comfortable to be on camera now and perform,” Citron said. “But,” he added, “back in the day, you were awkward.”

Citron and Worley said they never expected the project to take off. While Citron said they have made about $10,000 selling VHS Dates merchandise, including VHS tapes with the filmed sketches, the account is a “labor of love.”

The meticulous costuming — featuring the wide range of ’80s looks, including larger-than-life curls, wide-frame glasses and horrifying sweaters — is mostly from Western Costume, a Los Angeles warehouse that supplies television and film productions. To research the looks, Citron and Worley in fact turned to old Sears catalogs.

“What people connect to with these characters is that they are lonely,” Citron said. “They are looking for love, and people looking for love is very relatable, very primal and very human.”

One of the characters was performed by Allison Dunbar, who, like Gaul, is a Groundlings cast member with credits that include the streaming series “For All Mankind” and “Crazy Ex-Girlfriend.” She portrayed “Tina Terranova.”

Tina describes herself as a “M-80 firecracker with generous hips.”

When Dunbar first discovered VHS Dates on social media, she, too, thought the tapes were of real people. Then she spotted a friend, the actor David Crabb, as one of the characters. She wanted in, and even had a character in mind. The shoot took 45 minutes, and included improvised bits.

“There’s a vulnerability to putting yourself out there, and sharing your deepest wants and desires on a friggin’ VHS tape and hoping that someone sees it,” Dunbar said.

The actress Ashley Bell, who starred in the 2010 horror film “The Last Exorcism,” played “Marlene,” a woman who dresses conservatively, but who took up karate as a way to “get out some aggression.”

“If excellent is a choice, why would you want to wake up and be anything else but that?” Marlene says.

Enough people found the character so convincing that the top autocomplete suggestion when her name is typed into Google is “Marlene Dubois now.”

“Whether you currently found the love of your life or you’re still looking for somebody, we’ve all been at that awkward moment of searching,” Bell said.

Many followers of the VHS Dates account have long thought the videos were real, or wondered at the very least.

That includes a number of celebrities. Citron and Worley said the musician John Mayer once messaged the account. “You gotta tell me,” he wrote. “Are these: new fake, old fake, old real (though I believe this the least) I won’t tell a soul. Entertainer to entertainer…”

Citron and Worley — entertainer to entertainer — told Mayer the truth. Through a representative, Mayer declined to comment.

If the videos are not real, will it matter on social media? Using Worley’s professional wrestling analogy, audiences still tuned in to watch The Rock give Stone Cold Steve Austin a “People’s Elbow.”

“It’s true because it feels true. It’s real because it feels real,” Corsbie-Massay, the Syracuse professor, said, adding, “If you told fans, ‘Hey, this isn’t real, this is all recorded,’ I don’t think they would like it less.”

I decided to test that out by speaking with some people who had written directly to Citron and Worley, and were wondering if the supposed cache from the Los Angeles basement might connect with their own personal histories.

The parents of Sara Gauthier, 31, met on a video dating service in the 1980s. She explained in an interview that she has long hoped to find the tapes because they would be “validation that the story that they had told us about our lives wasn’t a lie.”

She was stunned by the truth.

“I hate that,” she said.

As the news set in, she said she was “heartbroken.”

Allen Davoren, 63, sent an email to the filmmakers, too. He recorded a tape in the mid-1980s in Sunnyvale, Calif., he said. The cost to take part was $1,500. In an interview, Davoren recalled making a joke during his recording.

“At the time, there was a commercial on TV about ‘It’s 10 o’clock. You know where your kids are?’” he said in an interview. “I remember it was my chance to say, ‘It’s 10 o’clock. Do you know where your parents are?’”

It never led to a successful match, and Davoren ended up getting billed for only $400.

“Once they saw that I asked, like, a couple dozen girls out and they all said no, I think they felt sorry for me and just gave up,” Davoren said.

Like Gauthier, he was also caught off guard by the truth.

“Really?” he said. “They look just like the real thing back then.”

Joshua Eisman, 43, wrote the creators to say his grandfather, Edward, had co-founded a video dating service and had videotaped some of the interviewers of the would-be daters featured in the tapes decades before.

At the start of our interview, he said he was convinced that he could hear his grandfather’s voice off-camera asking questions of the subjects in some of the VHS Dates videos, and that his brothers heard it, too.

He said he didn’t mind learning the truth.

“I’m kind of happy in a weird way,” he said. “I just like the truth. I mean, my grandpa would be, too. He wouldn’t want me going around telling a story that’s a little inaccurate.”

Sopan Deb is a Times reporter covering breaking news and culture.

The Bayeux Tapestry’s Long and Bumpy Road to London

The New York Times August 11, 2026

French art experts insisted the fragile 900-year-old masterpiece could never travel. Political will, cutting-edge engineering and unorthodox diplomacy proved them wrong.

French art experts insisted the fragile 900-year-old masterpiece could never travel. Political will, cutting-edge engineering and unorthodox diplomacy proved them wrong.

Credit... By Antoine Cazin/la Fabrique De Patrimoines En Normandie

Supported by

By Alex Marshall

Reporting from London

When Elizabeth II was about to be crowned in 1953, British officials decided on the perfect gift for the monarch-to-be: They would bring home the Bayeux Tapestry.

For over 900 years, the 220-foot-long embroidery depicting the 1066 invasion of England by William to Conqueror had resided in Bayeux, France. A celebratory loan of one of Britain’s most important cultural artifacts would have made an ideal coronation present, but there was a problem: French art experts wouldn’t let the tapestry travel. It was too fragile to move, they said.

French experts scuppered another British plan to borrow the tapestry in 1966 for the 900th anniversary of the invasion. Then again in 1980. Last year, too, the tapestry’s handlers insisted a loan was impossible.

And yet, against the odds, the tapestry is now in London. On July 9, at 6:15 p. m., a bright yellow truck carrying the masterpiece, sealed in a climate- and vibration-controlled case, left Bayeux in a secretive overnight operation. Accompanied by members of the French security services, the truck drove onto a Eurostar train that took it under the sea through the Channel Tunnel.

Once in England, the truck crawled through the night to the British Museum in London, where its cargo will be the star of a blockbuster exhibition that opens Sept. 10 and runs through July 11. Tickets are already sold out through Dec. 31, and the British Museum hopes attendance will rival the 1.6 million who flocked to its “Treasures of Tutankhamen” show in 1972.

When a decades-long no suddenly becomes a yes, something is clearly afoot. Since 2018, a legacy-seeking President Emmanuel Macron of France was among those leading the charge for the loan. King Charles III was a key encourager, according to Macron. So were several British prime ministers. The British government stepped in to insure the process for 800 million pounds, or over $1 billion.

Is the about-face over the tapestry a sign that political power can trump the concerns of curators and scholars? Or have conservation techniques developed so much that the impossible is now possible?

For those who oppose the loan, the answer is clear. Conservation teams may have done “everything possible to reduce risk,” said Didier Rykner, the founder of La Tribune de l’Art, a French art publication, but in the case of the tapestry, “everything is not enough.”

The loan had happened only because Macron wanted it to, Rykner said. “He thinks he knows better than experts.”

The list of reasons that the Bayeux Tapestry is so important is almost as long as the tapestry itself. Whereas many surviving medieval textiles are faded fragments, the Bayeux Tapestry is almost whole, and its red, blue and green threads still have an unexpected vibrancy.

Then there’s the story it tells: how William the Conqueror, a duke from Normandy in France, invaded England in 1066, deposing its last Anglo-Saxon monarch at the Battle of Hastings. Those events changed the English language, reshaped the country’s laws and upended its royal line. To many in England, the tapestry is a key symbol of British identity, like Stonehenge, Shakespeare’s plays or even the monarchy itself.

In 58 scenes that resemble plotlines from “Game of Thrones,” the tapestry’s narrative twists and turns, before climaxing in an epic battle between the armies of William the Conqueror and England’s king, Harold Godwinson. Calvary charge. Arrows fly. Swords cut through flesh, and heads tumble to the ground. In the tapestry’s best-known scene — depicting Godwinson’s death — the deposed monarch clasps an arrow that has thwacked into his eye.

It’s clearly a history told by the victor that establishes William the Conqueror as England’s rightful king. But it has also been used as a powerful symbol by the likes of Napoleon, the Nazis and now today’s political leaders.

The tapestry was likely embroidered in Canterbury, England, at some point in the 11th century, and historians aren’t sure how, when or why it made it to Bayeux, a city in northern France. It does not feature in historical records until 1476, when it appears in an inventory from Bayeux cathedral as “a very long and narrow hanging of linen, embroidered with figures and inscriptions representing the conquest of England.”

Although it was often stored in a cedar chest to guard against moths, the tapestry wasn’t always treated as a fragile masterpiece, even as, over the next few hundred years, its reputation grew.

Rocky, Reconsidered: The sculpture is at the center of a show by the Philadelphia Museum of Art that explores the meaning of monuments.

Route 66 Turns 100: Exhibitions recall the history of the neon corridor that helped define the American road trip.

A New Museum Comes With a D. J.: The space in Indianapolis, housed in a former dairy barn, aims to upend expectations of what an art museum can be.

The Fisher Collection in a New Light: A Bay Area family made a deal with SFMOMA that called for exhibitions of the collection’s works every 10 years.

More on Museums: Artists and institutions are adapting to changing times.

In 1803, Napoleon ordered it moved along bumpy roads from Bayeux to the Louvre museum in Paris to help drum up support for his planned invasion of Britain. It later returned to Bayeux, from where some 19th-century English visitors wrote that the tapestry was “already ragged” and predicted that it would “be wholly ruined in the course of half a century.”

When France was under German occupation during World War II, the Nazis brought the tapestry back to the Louvre on the orders of Heinrich Himmler, who ultimately wanted it in Germany. But the Allies liberated Paris before the tapestry disappeared into a Nazi treasure trove. It went back to Bayeux, and in the decades afterward, the tapestry’s guardians all seemed to agree on one thing: It shouldn’t leave town again.

The centuries of rough handling had an impact: A study from 2020 found that the tapestry had 24,204 stains, 16,445 wrinkles, 9,646 holes and 30 tears. By that time, ideas about art conservation had changed drastically.

Ella Hendriks, a professor of conservation at the University of Amsterdam, said that conservation had emerged as a profession in the 19th century, when there were two schools of thought. Some artists and philosophers, exemplified by the English critic John Ruskin, thought that artifacts should be maintained as they were or left to naturally decay, arguing that the “golden stain of time” added aesthetic value. Yet the majority of incipient conservators and restorers felt a duty to keep artworks looking brand-new.

If a painting had faded, these conservators “wouldn’t hesitate to over-paint an area,” Hendriks said. If a textile was worn, they would happily re-embroider it. That led to some dramatic interventions, maybe even for the Bayeux Tapestry. Many historians think the arrow thwacking into Godwinson’s eye isn’t an original detail but one of several 19th-century modifications.

It was only in the early 20th century, Hendriks said, that conservation became as much a science as a craft. Temperature and humidity controls became commonplace in museums, as did the practice of keeping artworks behind glass. Conservators refused loan requests from institutions that didn’t meet their standards, and although they developed techniques to let many artworks crisscross the globe safely, they considered some pieces too precious to ever move.

But political power could overcome that stance.

In 1962, when Jacqueline Kennedy asked André Malraux, France’s culture minister at the time, if he would allow the Mona Lisa to tour the United States, he agreed despite stiff opposition. A group of the Louvre’s curators threatened to resign, and French newspapers published articles aghast at the idea.

Malraux ignored their objections, and later that year, the Mona Lisa crossed the Atlantic in a climate-controlled container onboard an ocean liner. After a sojourn in Washington, it headed to the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York. It was on display there for only a few weeks, but over one million people caught a glimpse of the smiling beauty. It was the must-see show of the season — just as the British Museum hopes the tapestry will be.

For many of the tens of thousands of art lovers who signed a petition opposing the tapestry loan, its journey to Britain is reminiscent of the Mona Lisa’s 1960s travels — an event driven more by diplomatic posturing than by conservation expertise.

Macron first pledged to loan the tapestry to Britain as a gesture of friendship during a 2018 French-British political summit. As the two countries dealt with the fallout of Britain’s exit from the European Union, they could still make “a new tapestry together,” Macron told reporters at the time.

Isabelle Attard, the director of the Bayeux Tapestry Museum from 2005 to 2010, said in an interview that she had assumed it was an empty promise. When she joined the museum, she recalled, she had wanted to loan the tapestry to foreign institutions. “I was quite naïve and optimistic,” she said, but soon “everybody in Bayeux told me that was insane — that it was not possible for it to travel.”

Indeed, chatter around a loan soon died down after Macron’s 2018 pledge. Not long after the summit, relations between Britain and France soured over issues including illegal immigration and rival military deals.

By mid-2021, the Bayeux Tapestry loan was “in the deep freeze,” recalled Menna Rawlings, a former British ambassador to France. She said she had kept pushing for it, and recalled that Macron was supportive during a 2023 World War II commemoration event in Normandy. But by spring 2025, Rawlings said, a deal looked unlikely. She recalled a meeting at the French culture ministry that involved “a load of very senior civil servants saying, ‘This can’t happen. The tapestry is too fragile. The scientists say so.’”

Things suddenly changed at last year’s Cannes Film Festival. Chris Bryant, a senior British government culture official, traveled there for a half-hour hotel meeting with Rachida Dati, France’s culture minister at the time. Bryant said that the pair were supposed to discuss a loan, but Dati arrived 25 minutes late. It seemed like a bad sign, Bryant said, and he assumed that any deal was off.

Yet when Dati breezed in, she told Bryant she had some good news — but first he had to get on his knees and propose.

“I said, ‘I’ve already got a husband,’” Bryant recalled. “So do I,” Dati replied, according to Bryant: “What’s wrong with polyamory?” So Bryant went down on one knee. (A spokeswoman for Dati did not respond to requests for comment.)

A few weeks later, in July, Macron announced the loan during an event at the British Museum. France had blocked all previous attempts to borrow the tapestry by finding “the best experts in the world to explain in perfect details why it was totally impossible,” Marcon said. “We went beyond the experts,” he said, “and managed to find great solutions.”

A president who loves a grand gesture — particularly one achieved in the face of opposition — had secured the impossible loan. It would be part of his legacy.

Undoubtedly, one logistical factor played a role: The Bayeux Tapestry Museum was scheduled to close temporarily for a much-needed renovation.

Since 1983, the museum had displayed the tapestry in a U-shape, hanging from curtain hooks attached to a rail. That arrangement had put unnecessary strain on the embroidery, and conservators said it should be displayed laid out flat after undergoing a restoration. By the time the museum shut last fall, technicians — led by SMACH, a French conservation company — had spent years working on a plan to get the tapestry off its hooks and into storage.

Their operation played out one day last September, when about 100 technicians lined up along the tapestry’s length and carefully slid it along its rail, about two inches at a time, detaching it from the hooks and fastening it to a new railing on a series of panels that form a loose concertina. One of the French museum’s curators rhythmically intoned “Tac, tac, stop!” to guide each movement in a process that took six nerve-racking hours.

Hizkia, an art transportation company, working with experts in vibration engineering, then put the folded panels into a climate-controlled case suspended in a spring-mounted metal cage. Geert Verhoeff, an operations manager at Hizkia, said that the springs, known as “wire rope isolators,” functioned like “an old-school baby carriage” and had allowed his company to safely move incredibly fragile items like porcelain and paintings.

Once the tapestry was moved from the museum to a nearby storage location, the Hizkia team and other conservators undertook two trial runs in which they drove a mock tapestry to the British Museum, hoping to work out the route and collect vibration data so they could improve the design of the protective crate. (The British Museum declined to say how much the move had cost.)

When the tapestry set off for London on July 9, nothing had been left to chance.

For some critics of the loan, however, the move was still too risky.

Rykner, the Tribune de l’Art editor, said that the Bayeux authorities were taking a reckless gamble. A 2022 French government feasibility study said that moving it would “inevitably” subject the artifact to “further stress.” And a 2026 study said that too little was known about the tapestry’s “mechanical properties” to set a “scientifically robust” vibration limit for transportation.

Shirley A. Brown, an art history professor at York University in Toronto who is an expert on the tapestry, said in an email that she knew some French conservators “were dead set against the loan” but that, as employees of French state, they had to keep quiet.

Yet many conservators support the loan, not least because it means the tapestry will inspire thousands of people who otherwise wouldn’t see it. Hendriks, the conservation professor, said that many in the field now aimed to give artifacts the “best life” possible, rather than simply limiting damage. That could include allowing loans to museums with slightly worse temperature and humidity controls if it meant more public exposure and greater interaction.

On a Friday morning last month, as the British Museum prepared for its blockbuster show, the Bayeux Tapestry was lying flat on a 220-foot-long table, its tableaux of kingly warfare hidden beneath black protective sheets. To get ready for a visit from France’s culture minister, two conservators began unrolling the sheets one at a time, exposing scene after vivid scene.

At one end, a royal messenger raced on horseback, his hair flowing in the wind. In the center of the tapestry, two lumberjacks swung axes into trees to build William the Conqueror’s naval fleet. At the other end, the invading army clashed bloodily with its Anglo-Saxon rivals.

The tapestry had been removed from its transit case a day earlier, in an operation involving about 100 people that lasted from 7 a. m. until 3 the following morning — an intense 20 hours of focus. The team had connected the tapestry onto a rail, hoisted it skyward, then lowered it onto the specially constructed table.

It seemed like a success. Yet Cécilia Gauvin, one of the engineers involved, said that her team had found a “really small amount of threads” when unboxing the tapestry, although it was unclear if they were from the handlers’ clothes or from the embroidery itself.

Michael Lewis, the British Museum curator overseeing the show, didn’t seem worried about the threads. “Obviously you don’t want any damage,” he said, “but my colleagues seem very ‘Whatever’ about that.” His team and its counterparts from Bayeux had managed “a one-off,” he said, and set a new bar for how to move and display a historical treasure.

“To protect an object,” Lewis said, “sometimes you have to show it.”

I spoke with a lot of textile and other conservators for this story and the majority felt the benefit of boosting the profile and audience for the work (and so boosting the profile of all textile art in the process) outweighed the risk, especially given how much was spent on trying to make this move ok.

There’s so much more I wish I could have included and I highly recommend Carola Hicks’s history of the tapestry if you want to see bizarre images of it once kept on a spindle, which visitors turned to unroll the tapestry. That book also contains the full story of the Nazi’s attempts to reinvent the tapestry’s meaning and grab it for themselves.

Alex Marshall is a Times reporter covering European culture. He is based in London.

Trump Said to Have Secretly Used Military Jet to Leave Turkey Amid Threats

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Trump Administration

Advertisement

Supported by

President Trump hid in an airport catering container and was taken to a military jet for a secret flight out of country after the NATO summit last month.

By Maggie HabermanEric Schmitt and Zolan Kanno-Youngs

Top administration officials engaged in an elaborate deception to spirit President Trump out of Turkey during the NATO summit there last month, transporting him in a catering container to a military jet because of a threat against him and Air Force One from Iran, two senior U. S. officials said on Monday.

Mr. Trump and other officials had said publicly that he would be leaving Turkey on July 8 aboard one of the older versions of Air Force One rather than a luxury 747-8 donated by Qatar that he had flown in on. Mr. Trump told reporters at the time that he was making the switch for “old time’s sake.”

Mr. Trump boarded the older plane from the left-side door in view of cameras, and news organizations, including The New York Times, reported that he had traveled on it to Britain, where he switched back to the newer plane. The Times reported at the time that the decision was driven in part by concern about the security of the new plane donated by Qatar, which had not been equipped with all of the defensive capabilities of older versions.

But instead of flying out aboard that older, powder blue, modified 747, Mr. Trump was whisked off that plane via a catering container that had been lifted to the opposite side of the plane from where the journalists traveling with him boarded, one of the U. S. officials said. The president was then taken to a third plane, which he flew in secret to Britain, the official with knowledge of the matter said.

Reporters on the older plane were not told that Mr. Trump had not flown with them from Turkey to an air base in Britain, making them unwitting participants in the ruse and leaving them, along with some White House staff members, to serve as unknowing decoys.

After Mr. Trump arrived in Britain, he was secretly moved back to the older version of Air Force One. He then disembarked in view of reporters and walked across the tarmac to the plane donated by Qatar, which he flew to Washington.

The elaborate plan appears to have been developed in a matter of hours after officials determined that a threat against Mr. Trump from Iran was credible. It came after years of threats against Mr. Trump from Iran and amid the war he launched against that country. And it was an unusual instance of the public being deceived about a president’s whereabouts.

Officials have not previously acknowledged that Mr. Trump was not where they claimed he was. The furtive operation was reported earlier by the Washington Post, which identified the plane that carried the president to Britain from Turkey as a C-32A, a military variant of a Boeing 757.

It is rare for presidents to travel without the reporters who comprise the press pool designated to be with them, or in their vicinity, without some general awareness of the commander in chief’s whereabouts.

President George W. Bush kept a slimmed-down press pool with him as he flew first to a military base and then back to Washington in the chaotic hours after the Sept. 11, 2001, terrorist attacks. And presidents — including Mr. Trump — have traveled to war zones with reporters alongside them.

Donald Mihalek, a former Secret Service agent who worked in the protective details for Mr. Bush and President Barack Obama, said the Secret Service has used decoy motorcades and flights in the past to move presidents secretly.

“Anytime the president is in a theater where there’s potential combat danger, the Secret Service has employed protective counter measures including the use of a decoy,” Mr. Mihalek said.

He added that the agency has long prioritized the security of the president over transparency with the public.

“The agency is not the agency of public affairs,” he said. “The agency is the agency of the Secret Service. Their number one job is to keep the president safe.”

In this case, the threat led the White House to purposely mislead reporters assigned to cover Mr. Trump, leaving journalists to report that he was traveling on board a plane that was being used as a decoy.

After The Times reported last month on the security shortcomings of the Qatari-donated jet amid the concerns about the threat from Iran, Steven Cheung, the White House communications director, seemed to hint that subterfuge might have been involved in Mr. Trump’s departure from Turkey.

“As the president has said recently, there are many enemies of America who have their sights on him, and we use every tool at our disposal — including distraction and misdirection — to address those threats,” he said at the time.

The threat from Iran was not specific to the Qatari-donated jet, people familiar with the sequence of events said. Instead, Mr. Trump was being targeted on whatever airplane he was traveling on, as The Times has previously reported.

Mr. Trump was briefed on the threat while in Turkey, and a plan was soon put in place, with a small group of top government officials involved, including Gen. Dan Caine, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs, and Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, the official with knowledge of the deception plan said.

The Defense Department and a spokesman for General Caine referred all questions to the White House on Monday. The White House reissued Mr. Cheung’s statement from the week of the NATO summit, only without the clause mentioning “distraction and misdirection.” The U. S. officials with knowledge of the deception plan spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss sensitive security issues.

When Mr. Trump arrived at the airport to leave Turkey, a camera that had been positioned beforehand captured him getting on the old Air Force One, and journalists traveling with him, rushing to board the plane through lower steps in the back, had only a glimpse of him. A pool report noted as the plane took off, “We have been advised to keep our window shades in the press cabin closed.”

The older Air Force One landed at 10:16 p. m. at the Mildenhall military base in England, a pool report said, with Mr. Trump stepping off it 40 minutes later. Before that, unbeknown to reporters, Mr. Trump was brought by vehicle from the third plane that he had arrived on to the older Air Force One, boarded it through a different entrance and strode down the step from the upper left-side door as he would typically do, according to the U. S. official.

He walked from there to the Qatari jet, as the small group of reporters who had traveled aboard the older jet watched. The president and the press pool then boarded the new plane to fly back to Washington.

When asked by reporters during that flight back to the United States about why they had flown on the old Air Force One to England, Mr. Trump claimed he had wanted the troops stationed at the U. K. base to see the new plane. When pressed on why the window blinds had been directed to be shut on the flight from Turkey, Mr. Trump said that he knew of no specific threat, but described himself as under constant siege from Iran.

“I’m number one on their list, before you,” he told the reporters. “But if I go, you go. Right?

Mr. Trump and a number of his advisers have faced threats from Iran for years, especially after Mr. Trump, during his first term, ordered the killing of a powerful Iranian commander, Maj. Gen. Qassim Suleimani.

Mr. Trump’s advisers during the 2024 campaign privately told allies that they were given intelligence that Iran still hoped to harm Mr. Trump. That led to the occasional use of decoy planes during the campaign. His staff at times put the candidate aboard a secret jet, without alerting the media, while his staff flew his private plane. The Biden administration increased security for Mr. Trump that summer after new intelligence about plots to kill Mr. Trump; a short time later, he faced an unrelated assassination attempt in Butler, Pennsylvania at an outdoor rally. There was a second unrelated attempt in September 2024.

The collective threats, which ranged from a prosecuted murder-for-hire plot by an Iranian proxy to the hacking of Trump campaign officials’ email and social media accounts, created a siege climate for the candidate and his aides. It led to fear, insularity and, at times, paranoia, that shaped how officials interacted with the Biden administration, which was responsible for allocating Secret Service resources, as well as with the media, and with other government officials.

After the Times reported about the security concerns in Turkey, and the fact that the Qatari jet lacked the same defensive capabilities as the older presidential aircraft, Mr. Trump was enraged by the leaks. The Justice Department issued subpoenas for grand jury testimony to the reporters who had been on the bylines of the stories, as well as some of their family members. Those subpoenas were withdrawn after a federal judge admonished federal prosecutors for the process involved.

Mr. Trump has continued to rage about information coming from his government that contradicts what he is telling the public, particularly about the war in Iran that he began on Feb. 28, according to multiple officials.

Katie Rogers, Erica L. Green and Doug Mills contributed reporting.

Maggie Haberman is a White House correspondent for The Times, reporting on President Trump.

Eric Schmitt is a national security correspondent for The Times. He has reported on U. S. military affairs and counterterrorism for more than three decades. Contact him securely on Signal: @ericschmitt.36.

Zolan Kanno-Youngs is a White House correspondent for The Times, covering President Trump and his administration.

What to Know About the Vaccines Targeted by Trump’s Executive Order

The New York Times August 11, 2026

U. S. Health Policy

Advertisement

Supported by

The order, whose legal power is unclear, recommended narrowing the use of vaccinations against several diseases that can be life-threatening.

By Maggie AstorDani Blum and Teddy Rosenbluth

President Trump signed an executive order on Monday calling for narrowing government recommendations for vaccination against several diseases that can be serious or even deadly.

The executive order was the administration’s latest attempt to change the country’s childhood vaccine schedule. In January, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention tried to make the same changes, but a court blocked them, and legal proceedings are ongoing.

It is not clear whether the new executive order has any legal power. It suggests that vaccination against hepatitis A, hepatitis B, influenza, meningitis, respiratory syncytial virus and rotavirus should only be recommended for “high risk” children or under a framework called shared clinical decision making, which involves consultation with a medical provider. It also calls for the combined measles, mumps and rubella vaccine to be split.

Outside organizations, including the American Academy of Pediatrics, continue to broadly recommend all of these shots, including the combined M. M. R. vaccine. So does the C. D. C., whose proposed changes to the vaccine schedule are in limbo as the legal battle proceeds. The vaccines all remain available and covered by insurance.

The C. D. C.’s schedule was developed over decades, under presidential administrations of both parties, by a panel of experts who examined evidence, determined that the vaccines’ benefits outweighed any risks and recommended the timing of each dose based on when children’s immune systems could best benefit.

Here is a look at the vaccines Mr. Trump is targeting, and the diseases they prevent.

Hepatitis A is a highly contagious virus that can in rare cases cause liver failure and death. The virus lives in the stool and blood of infected people and often spreads through contaminated food or close contact.

Between the vaccine’s introduction in 1996 and 2011, the number of cases in the U. S. decreased by more than 95 percent.

The A. A. P. and the current C. D. C. schedule recommend two doses of the vaccine for all children. The executive order suggests it should be given only to “high-risk groups” or based on “shared clinical decision-making.”

Before it was routinely given, children played a major role in spreading hepatitis A: Most kids who become infected experience no symptoms and can shed it in their feces for months.

Hepatitis B is another very contagious virus that can lead to lifelong liver disease or injury. The disease can spread through sex or shared needles, but also through contact with household objects and from mother to child during birth. It can be up to 100 times as infectious as H. I. V.

Rates have fallen since the C. D. C. began widely recommending the vaccine for all newborns in 1991. Acute infections reported among children and teens dropped by 99 percent between 1990 and 2019.

A. A. P. and the C. D. C. recommend hepatitis B vaccination for all children, with the first dose being administered right after birth. But the executive order recommends it only for high-risk groups or under shared decision-making. The administration also previously tried to delay the birth dose.

The 2024-25 flu season was one of the deadliest on record for children, with the vast majority of deaths occurring among the unvaccinated, and the 2025-26 season was difficult as well. The flu can be particularly dangerous for children under 5 because they are susceptible to potentially fatal complications like pneumonia, brain issues and severe dehydration.

While the flu shot may not stop people from contracting the virus, it does help prevent people from getting very ill and dying. The C. D. C. normally recommends annual flu shots for everyone 6 months and older.

For years, a single shot has protected children against measles, mumps and rubella.

Measles is an extraordinarily contagious virus that can cause pneumonia, brain inflammation and loss of immunity to other illnesses. It was declared eliminated in the U. S. in 2000 but has roared back, with more cases in the past two years than in the previous 24 years combined.

Mumps affects the salivary glands, causing puffy cheeks and a swollen jaw; in rare cases, it can cause swelling of the brain, pancreas, testicles or ovaries, and lead to long-term hearing loss. Rubella is usually mild for patients themselves, but can cause serious birth defects if a pregnant woman is infected.

Mr. Trump’s order calls for three separate shots. While those exist in some countries, they aren’t available in the United States. Even if they were, experts say splitting them serves no medical purpose and would likely lead to more unvaccinated children because of the burden of making three appointments. And they note that the combined M. M. R. shot is safe; in rare cases, children may experience fever-induced seizures, which can be frightening for parents but don’t cause lasting harm.

The A. A. P. continues to strongly recommend two doses of the combined shot; the C. D. C. also still recommends both doses of the combined shot.

Vaccines protect against five groups of bacteria that can cause meningitis, a potentially fatal inflammation of membranes around the brain and spinal cord.

This inflammation causes fever, neck stiffness, headaches, vomiting and cognitive changes. With prompt treatment, many people recover, regardless of their vaccination status. But about one in six people with bacterial meningitis die from it, while one in five survivors have serious complications like long-term hearing loss, muscle weakness, speech difficulties or memory problems, according to the World Health Organization and the National Foundation for Infectious Diseases.

The A. A. P. and the C. D. C. recommend that all children receive a combination vaccine against the A, C, W and Y groups of meningococcal bacteria over two doses, the first at 11 or 12 and the second at 16. Mr. Trump wants those shots only under shared clinical decision-making or for “high-risk groups,” which could include people with H. I. V. and first-year college students living in dormitories, because the infection spreads easily in communal spaces.

A separate vaccine protects against the B group, but it has long been recommended only for some children, including those who lack a functioning spleen or who live in a place with an outbreak.

R. S. V. is the leading cause of infant hospitalization in the United States, sending up to 80,000 children younger than 5 to the hospital every year. The virus is more likely to cause serious complications in children with compromised immune systems or chronic lung disease. However, healthy babies may also develop a severe case.

Early infections have also been linked to long-term complications, including asthma.

The A. A. P. and C. D. C. both say that if a mother was vaccinated during pregnancy, her infant may not need a dose at birth, but recommend immunization for babies born to unvaccinated mothers during the respiratory virus season. Mr. Trump’s order recommends it only for “high-risk groups,” which it doesn’t define. Experts worry about excluding children born to women whose vaccination status is unknown or who may need additional protection.

Rotavirus is a diarrheal illness that most commonly affects children under 5, and killed hundreds of thousands per year worldwide before vaccines were available. While deaths are much less common in countries like the United States, which has access to quick treatment for dehydration, the virus used to cause tens of thousands of hospitalizations in American children.

Infection rates began to plummet after a vaccine was introduced in the late 2000s, and the National Foundation for Infectious Diseases estimates that vaccination prevents 40,000 to 50,000 rotavirus-related hospitalizations in the United States every year. An earlier vaccine was withdrawn in 1999 after it was linked to a serious type of intestinal blockage, but current versions are unlikely to cause that side effect.

The C. D. C. and A. A. P. advise all children to receive doses at 2 months and 4 months. (Depending on the brand of vaccine administered, some children are advised to get a third dose at 6 months.) The executive order suggests removing the recommendation, though parents could still seek the vaccine from a doctor.

Maggie Astor covers women’s health and the health effects of government policies for The Times.

Dani Blum is a health reporter for The Times.

Teddy Rosenbluth is a Times reporter covering health news, with a special focus on medical misinformation.

3 Presidents Before Trump Who Obscured Their Risky Travel Plans

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

President Trump was part of a particularly elaborate deception last month, boarding one jet before being spirited away to another.

By Claire Moses

In front of the cameras, President Trump boarded Air Force One after a NATO summit in Turkey last month. But he did not fly out on that plane.

Instead, he hid in an airport catering container and was taken to a military jet for a secret flight out of the country. He then re-boarded the original plane in time to be seen by the news media.

The elaborate plan, spurred by threats from Iran and apparently developed in a matter of hours, was an unusual instance of journalists and the public being deceived about the president’s whereabouts. Presidents have made clandestine trips before, but rarely with so complete a deception or with no apparent intention to make clear eventually what really took place.

The White House press pool, a group of journalists from various news organizations who cover the president and travel with him, typically receives guidance beforehand of his daily agenda. The guidance can be vague for security reasons and change on short notice. But Mr. Trump’s ruse was unusual in that it also left White House staff members and journalists on the original plane as unknowing decoys and in potential danger.

Here are some recent examples of when past presidents have made unannounced trips.

One piece of presidential subterfuge also involved three planes but came roughly a quarter-century earlier.

President Bill Clinton traveled to India and Pakistan in 2000 to try to defuse tensions in Kashmir, a Himalayan region claimed by both countries.

“Mr. Clinton, who decided to come to Pakistan after intense disagreement within the administration, spent barely six hours in the country,” The Times wrote at the time. The logistics of getting there included “some of the most elaborate security precautions ever devised for his travels.”

As Mr. Clinton was leaving India, he walked to the door of a military aircraft and shook hands with dignitaries as though he was going to board it.

But instead, The Times reported, he slipped around the front of the plane to board an unmarked, white jet that was parked nearby.

In total, three planes made the trip to Islamabad: the unmarked plane with Mr. Clinton aboard, the original military aircraft and a third decoy plane similar to Mr. Clinton’s but painted in the powder blue of Air Force One.

On Nov. 27, 2003, about eight months after the start of the Iraq war, President George W. Bush flew to Baghdad on Air Force One, in what was the first visit to the country by an American president.

The Times’s coverage of the trip called it a “stunning mission conducted under enormous secrecy.” It was so secret, the report said, that even the president’s wife and parents were not told of it in advance.

Mr. Bush’s entourage did, however, include “a few advisers and a small number of reporters sworn to secrecy.”

The president had been spending Thanksgiving at his ranch outside Crawford, Texas, and officials said he would be there all weekend.

Instead, he left the ranch in an unmarked car, flew to Andrews Air Force Base outside Washington and from there to Baghdad International Airport. He spent just over two-and-a-half hours in the country.

President Barack Obama made his first secret visit to Afghanistan in 2010 to meet American troops there and to speak with the country’s leader. Journalists had been told beforehand that he was spending the weekend at Camp David, The Guardian reported at the time.

That misdirection was one of several layers of security, including visiting at night, staying only briefly and giving limited notice to the Afghan government, according to The Guardian.

The trip did not, however, involve a physical charade that implied the president was in a different location or vehicle.

Claire Moses is a Times reporter in London, focused on coverage of breaking and trending news.

Europe Is Heating Up This Week. Here’s What to Know.

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Extreme Heat

Advertisement

Supported by

The latest heat wave is the fifth to batter European countries since May as scientists warn that the continent is warming faster than any other.

See temperatures as...

20

°C

Note: Data is as of 12:02 a. m. on Wednesday, Aug. 12 Central European Time. Forecast is for 24 hours beginning at 8 a. m. on Tuesday, Aug. 11 C. E. T. Source: European Centre for Medium-Range Weather. William B. Davis and Bea Malsky/The New York Times

By Nazaneen Ghaffar

Nazaneen Ghaffar is a reporter on The Times’s weather team. She is based in London.

Temperatures are set to soar this week across large swaths of Europe, offering little respite from heat waves that have battered the continent in quick succession this summer. Forecasters have warned that the relentless heat, combined with persistently dry conditions, could worsen the already severe threat of wildfires.

It is the fifth heat wave to hit Europe since May, with a high pressure system known as a heat dome intensifying across the continent. The extreme heat has fueled a string of dangerous wildfires, dried rivers and sent residents flocking to cooler spots as scientists warn that Europe is warming faster than any other continent.

The latest episode is expected to see temperatures rise to 40 degrees Celsius (104 degrees Fahrenheit) or more, initially across parts of France, Spain and Italy, then in Central Europe, where some of the highest temperatures are expected in the Czech Republic, Germany and Poland.

The European Forest Fire Information System has forecast that parts of southern Britain and northwestern France, along with a stretch of territory from the Alps to the Balkans, will remain under “very extreme” fire conditions — the highest category — through at least Wednesday.

High-level heat warnings were already in place across parts of western, central and southern Europe on Tuesday. The most severe alerts were in parts of Bosnia and Herzegovina, Croatia, Hungary, Italy, Serbia and Switzerland.

Météo-France, the French weather service, said that highs from 35 to 39 Celsius (95 to 102 Fahrenheit) were forecast across much of the country on Thursday and Friday, with 40 Celsius (104 Fahrenheit) expected in some locations. Cooler air and thunderstorms were expected to move in from the west by the weekend.

Spain is forecast to experience some of the most intense heat. AEMET, the country’s weather service, said that temperatures close to 40 Celsius (104 Fahrenheit) were expected on Wednesday in much of central, southern and northeastern Spain. On Thursday, temperatures could reach 43 Celsius (109 Fahrenheit) in the south.

In Britain, the Met Office, the country’s weather service, issued amber extreme heat warnings — the second-highest level in its three-tier warning system — for Thursday. Parts of eastern and southeastern England and the Midlands were expected to broil, and temperatures could reach 38 Celsius (around 100 Fahrenheit). That would match the high for Britain so far this year, and it would be the first time temperatures of 36 Celsius (nearly 97 Fahrenheit) or higher were recorded in August since 2020.

Italy has been sweltering since last week, when the country’s health ministry placed all 27 cities monitored by its heat surveillance system under red alert. The Italian Air Force Meteorological Service said that heat would continue to batter Italy through this week, with the highest temperatures reaching 40 Celsius (104 Fahrenheit) in parts of Basilicata, Calabria, Campania, Lazio, Puglia, Sardinia, Sicily and Umbria.

Determining how a single heat event was influenced by climate change requires specific attribution studies. But scientists are certain that heat waves around the world are generally becoming more intense, more frequent and longer.

In 2025, almost all of Europe was hotter than normal. Researchers estimate that in recent years the continent has suffered from tens of thousands of heat-related deaths annually.

Many homes, schools and businesses across Europe were built for a cooler climate. In Britain, many were designed to retain heat. Air-conditioning is not a quick fix. In France, installing such systems has become a political flashpoint. Some are deterred by high energy prices.

Even in Mediterranean cities more accustomed to heat, where old-fashioned courtyards, heavy shutters and white-stone facades can keep homes cool, many newer buildings trap heat.

See temperatures as...

Degrees above or below average for Aug. 11

Sources: University of Maine Climate Change Institute and the National Centers for Environmental Prediction Global Forecast System

Notes: Forecasts are for 8 p. m. Eastern on Aug. 10, 2026, to 8 p. m. on Aug. 11, 2026. Averages based on data from 1979 to 2000.

By Lazaro Gamio and Zach Levitt

Staying cool and hydrating often are the two most important things to do to avoid feeling sick and discomfort in extreme heat. Here are some other ways to keep cool:

Block out the windows in your home — especially those that get afternoon sun — with a blanket or a darker sheet during the day to keep the heat out. At night, keep windows open and run fans to circulate the air.

Spritz your skin with a mist of cool or room-temperature water or wipe your forehead with a cool cloth. Cold showers can also help you cool down.

If you need to be outdoors, put ice cubes in your water bottle and drink cool liquids. If you plan to exercise, douse your head in cold water. Swimming is also a great way to exercise and keep cool.

When exploring or sightseeing, seek out cooler attractions such as museums, cathedrals or even subterranean exhibitions. Some cities have created public spaces to cool down: Barcelona has climate shelters, Paris has drinking fountains, and London has a Cool Spaces map to find spots.

Watch out for signs of heat stroke. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention lists some of them as dizziness, a rapid pulse, nausea, headache and fainting. But symptoms can vary.

Nazaneen Ghaffar is a Times reporter on the Weather team.

Can Nebraska’s Definitely-Not-a-Democrat Candidate Crack the G. O. P. Hold on Red America?

The New York Times August 11, 2026

Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

OpinionMichelle Cottle

Dan OsbornCredit... Terry Ratzlaff for The New York Times

Supported by

By Michelle Cottle

Ms. Cottle writes about national politics for Opinion.

The Senate candidate Dan Osborn wants to be crystal clear that he is not a Democrat. No way. No thank you. Yes, he is running to unseat the Republican Pete Ricketts in Nebraska. And, yes, he has much to say about how President Trump’s policies are hurting Nebraskans. Not to mention how shamefully the Republican-led Congress is failing to stand up to the executive branch. But Mr. Osborn is a “Lifelong Independent” — as his campaign signs clearly proclaim — and when folks ask why, he has a simple explanation.

“I don’t like either party,” he told me over pizza and beer recently, after a morning of campaigning. “I think they both kind of suck.” His goal, as Mr. Osborn tells it, is to infiltrate the Senate and start breaking the unhealthy two-party death grip on the system.

Given this pox-on-both-their-houses posture, it might seem strange that the Nebraska Democratic Party is backing Mr. Osborn’s candidacy, that national Democrats have given him their tacit blessing and that his campaign events tend to be packed with more die-hard Democrats than fence-sitting unaffiliateds. Strange, maybe. But also smart.

Nebraska is a ruby red enclave where registered Republicans outnumber Democrats nearly two to one. Regardless of how the political winds are blowing nationwide, a Democrat winning a Senate seat in the state anytime soon feels about as likely as Tucker Carlson becoming the next mayor of Tel Aviv. But a salty, populist independent with blue-collar street cred, a progressive economic platform and moderate takes on cultural issues? That is a solid maybe.

So it is that Mr. Osborn, a Navy veteran turned industrial mechanic and union organizer, now finds himself at the center of a high-stakes electoral experiment being conducted by the Democratic Party he has openly rejected. It is an intriguing enough prospect that Democrats in other deep red pockets of the country, including Idaho and Montana, have been charting similar courses.

The strategy is not without risk. By boosting an independent like Mr. Osborn, Democrats could indeed be facilitating the demise of the two-party system over which they have long enjoyed co-dominance. Trickier still, by not running its own candidate, the party might give some voters the impression that it has given up on rural America. But those feel like long-term, second-tier concerns compared with the fierce urgency of rural America’s antipathy toward Democrats — an antipathy unlikely to be improved by the ongoing crusade of the party’s left flank to drive out moderates.

The potential reward, by contrast, is immense: reducing the number of Republicans in the closely divided Senate, with an authoritarian president in need of reining in. Sure, Mr. Osborn would presumably oppose pieces of the Democratic agenda. Maybe even big pieces. But in Nebraska — unlike, say, New York — Democrats don’t have the luxury of purity tests and ideological fragility. They need to figure out how to grow their tent. Now. And if the Osborn model works, they’ll need to carry it through across other hostile terrain.

Part of what has Democrats so fired up about Mr. Osborn in particular is that he has already proved himself a compelling pitchman for the anti-establishment, working-class message the party is interested in reclaiming. Two years ago, he ran against Nebraska’s senior senator, the Republican Deb Fischer. Back then, the state’s Democrats were annoyed by his upstart run. The state party chair, Jane Kleeb, accused him of being “inauthentic” and a threat to the Democrats’ agenda. His independent candidacy, always a long shot, was dismissed by many political watchers as beyond quixotic.

Mr. Osborn lost, but not by much, coming within seven points of beating Ms. Fischer. It was a startling overperformance that got Democrats asking themselves: What if we went all in on this guy next time?

Since his last run, Mr. Osborn has worked to up his game. He has traveled the state, talking with health care workers and bartenders, farmers and ranchers. (Nevada is a huge ag state.) His supporters say he has gotten better on the stump. He is not a smooth or charismatic speaker, but he comes across as solid and earnest. He won’t be “beholden” to corporate interests or “controlled by party bosses,” he promised the barroom full of people at a town hall in Columbus last month. “I would be the most popular politician overnight in this country,” he predicted, with people far-and-wide marveling, “Guys, did you see what Nebraska did?! A shop-floor mechanic beat the son of a billionaire? The times, they are a-changin’!”

Mr. Osborn speaks directly to voters’ widespread disaffection with the political establishment, accusing both parties of putting their own interests, and those of their funders, above the public good. He is a fierce critic of the Supreme Court’s Citizens United campaign finance ruling, he does not take corporate PAC donations, and he is adamant that politicians who do — be it Ms. Fischer or Nancy Pelosi — cannot be trusted.

Off the stump, Mr. Osborn passes the political test of a guy you’d like to have a beer with. He is loose, chatty, goofy and prone to saying stuff that would give the average campaign consultant a heart attack. Our lunch conversation, joined by several of his campaign staff, began with an explanation of his tattoos — two are of his daughters’ names, none are Nazi-adjacent — before touching on such colorful topics as the “I love Intercourse” T-shirt he bought for his wife while visiting Intercourse, Pa. (she won’t wear it); the parade of worker grievances he learned to deal with as a union leader; and how fun it would be to play in the annual Congressional Baseball Game. A pitcher in high school, Mr. Osborn joked that he would probably aim to hit the occasional batter — “I’d throw right into ’em” — to keep the opposition off balance. Notably, he did not say which team he envisioned pitching for.

Mr. Osborn’s supporters also consider the Republican opponent this cycle, Mr. Ricketts, a better foil than Ms. Fischer was in 2024. Ms. Fischer “is a ranch woman who knew how to pull a calf,” noted John Hansen, the president of the Nebraska Farmers Union. (For decades, Ms. Fischer worked on her family’s ranch.) Mr. Ricketts is the son of Joe Ricketts, the billionaire founder of the brokerage firm TD Ameritrade. “Pete Ricketts, there’s a lot of people that look at him and they kind of see a spoiled rich kid,” said Mike Gage, the president of the state’s A. F. L.-C. I. O., which is backing Mr. Osborn. “Replace a billionaire with a mechanic” has become a tag line for the Osborn campaign.

Also important: Mr. Trump will occupy a very different role in this race than in the last one. Among other economic challenges facing Nebraskans, this administration has poorly served the state’s farmers and ranchers, said Mr. Hansen. “We’re in crisis.” In 2024, the Nebraska Farmers Union supported Ms. Fischer over Mr. Osborn. This year, it is backing Mr. Osborn over Mr. Ricketts.

Maybe the most intriguing, and potentially significant, change from 2024 is Mr. Osborn’s improved relations with Nebraska Democrats. “It’s a totally different election cycle and relationship that we have with Dan,” said Ms. Kleeb, the state party chair. “In ’24, we felt like we were just on totally different pages. We didn’t feel like it was a coalition effort.” Back then, she recalled, Mr. Osborn was seen as criticizing Democrats too aggressively and identifying with Mr. Trump’s policy positions on border security too enthusiastically.

The state party has done some soul-searching as well, added Ms. Kleeb. Nebraska Democrats had to “put our egos aside” and acknowledge that backing Mr. Osborn gave Democrats a better shot at seeing their issues and values taken seriously. “We know we will disagree with Dan on various issues,” said Ms. Kleeb. The left wing of the party will most likely take exception to his views on gun control, she offered. Some of his positions on immigration, his concerns about government spending and his personal opposition to abortion (even as he supports codifying the Roe v. Wade position) may also rub progressives the wrong way. “And that’s OK,” said Ms. Kleeb. “We are comfortable being uncomfortable.”

National Democrats are being extremely quiet about Nebraska. But they are on board with the plan, Ms. Kleeb told me. Nebraska Democrats are backing several independents up and down the ballot this year, leaning into the independent boomlet that Mr. Osborn has been credited with inspiring. “We absolutely embrace them,” said Ms. Kleeb.

In fact, she hopes to see “more candidates within our own Democratic Party, as well as more independents, start creating more coalitions. Because I really do think that is the path back to creating more durable policies that look and sound more like America and that can last beyond one election cycle,” she said. “I think that is part of our party’s future, especially in red states, and we shouldn’t be embarrassed about it.”

Democrats elsewhere seem to agree. In Montana, several prominent Democrats, including the former Montana senators Jon Tester and Max Baucus, are promoting the independent Senate candidate Seth Bodnar. Unlike in Nebraska, the state party in Montana has not been on board, and its candidate, Alani Bankhead, dug in rather than bowed out. Ahead of Monday’s deadline to withdraw from the ballot, both candidates faced pressure to get out to avoid splitting the non-Republican vote, but neither budged.

In Idaho, the Democratic Senate candidate, David Roth, dropped out of that race last month, leaving it to the independent, Todd Achilles, to take on the longtime Republican incumbent, Jim Risch. A county Democratic group had warned Mr. Roth that if his campaign didn’t show prompt improvement, they would consider endorsing Mr. Achilles against him. Last Tuesday, the Democratic Senate candidate in South Dakota, Julian Beaudion, did the same for the independent Brian Bengs.

Joe Manchin, the former Democratic senator from West Virginia, wants to scale up this trend. Last month, he announced a new organization aimed at getting more independents elected to Congress. With the House and Senate so closely divided, he told CNN, it wouldn’t take many free agents to bring their more partisan colleagues “to their knees.”

Especially with democratic socialists making waves in the primaries, the Democratic Party seems unlikely to make much progress broadening its brand appeal this year. If Mr. Osborn succeeds, or even comes closer than last time, the blue team should ramp up its coalition building with red-state independents who are, if not in their camp, at least adjacent. It could make for a more dynamic, maybe even less polarized, Congress.

As the fighting with Iran has dragged on, American forces have been burning through their weapons, creating a shortage of munitions that is leaving the United States and its allies in the region much more vulnerable.

Today, Eric Schmitt, a national security correspondent for The New York Times, explains why Washington is running out of key missiles so quickly, and how the munitions shortage might affect the safety of U. S. troops and the Trump administration’s efforts to end the war.

Eric Schmitt, a national security correspondent for The New York Times.
